The Skeletons You Hide

Summary

Rio’s grin widened. “Touchy, aren’t you? You're cute when you're annoyed.”

Agatha took a deep breath. “Rio, you can’t lie your way out of this. I don’t know what you're trying to achieve, but you're only making this process longer for yourself.”

Rio leaned forward. “Do you get hit on alot? Because honestly, if you weren’t my therapist I would totally fu-”

Agatha cleared her throat, cutting Rio off. The look on Rio’s face made it obvious that she knew she had gotten a reaction, and Agatha hated it.

OR

Rio Vidal has just been released on probation from prison – only requirement being that she goes to meet a psychiatrist, Agatha Harkness, twice a week.

Chapter 1

Rio

As Rio stripped out of that orange jumpsuit for the last time, she swore to herself that she would never put it back on.

The clothes that she had been given were plain and slightly on the large size - they’d had to guess at her sizing - but they were something different and for now, that was enough. The jumpsuit had made her feel weak, powerless; it branded her as just another number in the crowd of people cast out by society.

It was finally time to shed that part of her life.

She was sitting comfortably, or at least as comfortably as the hard plastic chair allowed, with her legs spread wide open in a way that would have given her foster mother a heart attack. She knew she was probably supposed to behave, to act like a good little school girl who was incapable of doing wrong. But that was boring.

And besides, she herself believed she hadn’t done anything wrong. Sure, maybe the law said otherwise, but the majority of those were written by racist old white men who couldn't give a shit about fairness.

No one else believed that though.

The handcuffs around her wrists said as much.

Not that they did anything, Rio thought. If she really wanted to then she could pick the lock mechanism easily enough, but where would she run too? She would be back in her cell before she even managed to stand up.

The probation officer paced the room, hands touching his chin as he mumbled to himself. Rio couldn’t help but wonder what he was so worried about. He almost seemed stressed, like he was the one with his hands in handcuffs behind his back. The thought made her grin.

Behind her, two prison guards were standing on high alert. Hands on their holster, right by the gun, as if they needed to hide it from her – or use it on her.

As if she needed a gun to cause some damage.

A situation like this should’ve made her feel uneasy, but she honestly felt the best she had since she’d entered this godforsaken place. Plus, being in here meant she could evade all the other inmates, even if just for an hour. They’d been a pain in her ass for over a week – It was usual practice when an inmate had a nearby release.

Rio’s impatience was growing. She’d spent the last seven years in captivity, locked up in this hellhole.

Okay fine, maybe she was exaggerating slightly. It hadn’t been all bad. She’d actually made some friends - if you could call them that, and after her first cellmate had some kind of nervous breakdown (on her second day no less, pathetic), Rio had grown to like her replacement.

“What are we waiting for?” Rio spoke up, voice laced with irritation.

The probation officer, Bruce, she thought his name was, stopped in his tracks, as if he couldn’t quite believe that Rio had actually spoken to him. “Dr. Harkness.”

She sighed, leaning further back into the chair. Seeing someone was the deal, she knew that. But she still loathed it, hoping to find her way out of meeting with a man trying to psychoanalyze her behaviour or find a cure to her problems – she didn’t need help.

Okay maybe she did, but that was her business.

A moment later, a woman entered the room. She was extremely put together – a perfect posture, ironed gray suit and a pair of big brown glasses perfectly placed on her nose. Her long dark hair was the contrast, her curls in a mess.

“Dr. Harkness,” Bruce said, a feigned politeness in his voice – was he nervous?

“Bruce,” She greeted him, reaching out for a handshake with a stern smile on her face. “And this is Rio Vidal, I take it?” She nodded towards Rio, who was still relaxing back into her chair with an unamused look on her face.

Bruce nodded, pulling out a chair for the woman to sit in before taking his place behind the big desk.

“Lovely,” Dr. Harkness sat down, placing a big green folder on the desk and opening it with skillful hands. “So Rio,” She turned around, now looking directly at Rio, who suddenly felt small beneath her gaze. “Charged with involuntary manslaughter, seven years?”

Rio nodded. God she hated this already. She felt examined, stripped off her clothes and laid bare beneath the eyes of this woman, the intenseness in the blue of them.

“Three years probation, is that correct?” She asked, turning to Bruce who once again swallowed and nodded. “Great.”

“The terms for the probation is as follows; you will see a psychiatrist, Dr. Harkness here, for 2 sessions a week for three years, which would equal around 200 hours.” Bruce explained, “Dr. Harkness will also be the person who has the power to cancel, change or reschedule this plan.”

Rio groaned internally. God, she really fucking hated those terms.

Dr. Harkness cleared her throat. “Sessions on Mondays and Thursdays, 10-11:30 am,” she explained, pulling a paper out of her bag and placing it on the table in front of Rio, “All the information you need is on this paper.”

She looked at Rio like she was expecting some kind of response, but Rio kept her mouth shut. If this woman was going to come in here, order her around and have the audacity to treat her like she wasn’t even in the room – then Rio was going to make it her mission to be as uncooperative as possible.

Bruce cleared his throat, “Breaking the probation rules will result in you spending those three years back in here. The only way to avoid that is showing up to your sessions, and work compliantly with Dr. Harkness. Do that and these three years will be passed before you even know it.”

Rio snorted.

Bruce stood up and placed a paper above the one Dr. Harkness had just put on the table, “This is the official document concerning your release. Read it thoroughly even if most of it has been stated in this meeting, it might serve as a good reminder, at least.”

Rio looked out the window. The two birds in the tree right outside were more interesting to her in that moment than whatever Bruce was going on about.

“Rio,” Dr. Harkness stood up as well, “Looking forward to seeing you tomorrow morning.”

Rio didn’t respond, she didn’t need to. Everything that could be said had been. She wanted out now, to breathe in the air of freedom, to meet her friends. But most of all, she wanted to sleep in a real bed for once. The psychiatrist was tomorrow's problem.

“Bruce,” She addressed him with a nod of goodbye before slinking out the doorway. Bruce walked around towards the door to hold it open, “I believe this is goodbye, Vidal. Go to your sessions and behave, and hopefully, we never have to say hello again.”

Rio smirked and stood up, “You’ll miss me too much.”

Bruce chuckled nervously, standing by the door with his head held high. Despite the circumstances, Rio felt more powerful than him. Which she was, and he knew it too.

If it were up to him, her sentence would’ve been longer than seven years. He’d seen her behaviour inside. Behaviour which had gotten her into trouble more often than not – arguing with inmates, talking back to officers, not wanting to work. But she’d never gotten physical with it, or at least, she’d never caused any damage that could be led back to her.

Which is also why there were no grounds to prolong her sentence. Rio was calculated, she knew the risks and the all potential outcomes of every situation. Getting in serious trouble was something she simply didn’t do.

Though now she was thinking about it, maybe that was a bad rhetoric to have. If she was as good and smart as she believed herself to be, then she wouldn’t have been locked up in the first place.

Two officers, along with Bruce, silently led her through a corridor which ended in a big open waiting room.

Anticipation built up within her when she could hear the clicking noise of the handcuffs being removed. She wanted to bolt away, run out those glass doors and never look back. She felt her entire body becoming electric with energy, like she could run a marathon.

“Here’s your personal belongings,” Bruce said, handing her a see-through bag containing nothing more than a phone and a necklace.

Rio, hands now free from behind her back, snatched the bag out of Bruce’s hand – probably a bit too furiously – and walked out.

The fresh autumn air hit her face like a tidal wave, washing over her again and again with each breath. It felt surreal, she could feel the freedom down to her core, running up her spine and putting a big, satisfied smile on her lips.

The parking lot outside was empty except for a black car parked in the far corner. Rio made her way over, putting her hands in her pockets to shield them from the cold air.

Rio opened the door to the passenger seat. “Hello,” she smiled, greeting Lilia as if no time had passed at all – like everything was normal. Of course it wasn’t, but Rio kind of hoped they would act like it was.

“Rio!” Lilia sighed in relief, “Thought for a second they wouldn’t let you out today.”

Rio snorted, “I’d have liked to see them try to keep me.”

“How are you?” Lilia asked then, sounding a little bit concerned.

A fair feeling to have when your foster child had been locked up for seven years.

Rio shrugged, “I’m okay.”

Lilia looked over at her while starting the engine, “I’m gonna need more than that.”

Rio groaned, “What do you want to know?”

“How do you feel? Have you been eating enough? You look skinny. Did they treat you right?” Lilia asked anxiously, turning her focus onto the road as they drove out of the parking lot.

“I feel good,” Rio insisted. “I really need to sleep though.”

Lilia chuckled, “Those beds did not treat your back right, I can imagine?”

Rio nodded, smiling bittersweetly – she’d missed this, missed Lilia. She was the mother she could never have and, even if Lilia was to blame for a lot of the shit Rio was currently in, she still appreciated her. She’d even visited the prisons, on some occasions, something no one else except some friends had done.

The space within the car turned silent for a moment. Rio took the opportunity to open the folder of papers she’d gotten during the meeting and look them over.

The terms of your probation are as follows:

(a)be of good behaviour and not behave in a way which undermines the purpose of the

licence period;

(b) not commit any offence;

(c) keep in touch with the supervising officer in accordance with instructions given by

the supervising officer;

(d) receive visits from the supervising officer in accordance with instructions given by

the supervising officer;

(e) reside permanently at an address approved by the supervising officer and obtain the

prior permission of the supervising officer for any stay of one or more nights at a

different address;

(f) not undertake work, or a particular type of work, unless it is approved by the supervising officer and notify the supervising officer in advance of any proposal to

undertake work or a particular type of work; and

(g) not travel outside the United States of America except with the prior permission of your supervising officer or for the purposes of immigration deportation or removal.

(h) tell your supervising officer if you use a name which is different to the name or

names which appear on your licence;

(i) tell your supervising officer if you change or add any contact details, including phone

number or email

(j) attend twice a week therapy sessions for the entirety of your probation period

Failure to meet these requirements may lead to the individual in question being

recalled.

______

Rio woke up in her new flat after a full night's sleep – the first one in a long time.

Lilia had arranged for her to borrow the flat until she could get on her feet again, save up some money and buy a new place. For now, it was good enough.

It was small and had an overall very homey feeling to it. Lilia had added a little furniture – a bed, a couch, a table and some chairs. It wasn’t big, one bedroom, living room-kitchen area and one bathroom. But after spending seven years in a cell, it felt like the biggest place in the world.

The bedroom still had no curtains, so the morning light shone through enough to wake Rio up. She’d gone to bed last night and fell asleep immediately, feeling the weight and exhaustion of seven years catch up on her.

Frankly, she’d thought it would be harder to get used to this. Living alone meant hearing no one else breathe or snore beside you, nor did it mean she could get woken up in the middle of the night by someone kicking off about something or other. And the bed was like sleeping on a cloud compared to what she’d done for years .

But no, Rio was a quick learner. Getting used to this didn’t even take more than a minute.

It had probably something to do with her childhood, moving from foster family to foster family. Always having to get used to a new situation – a new bed, a new snore and a new lifestyle.

Lilia had prepped the fridge with some breakfast; orange juice, milk, butter and a can of coke.

Even if it meant being late to her first session with Dr Harkness, she took her time making breakfast at. She had no idea how far away she lived, if she needed to take an uber, walk or get there in some other way. Any rational person would’ve probably looked it up, afraid of what showing up late to the first appointment would mean.

Rio wasn’t a rational person. Not in that regard, anyway.

So she sat quietly, reading the newspaper while eating her cereal in a calm manner. If someone told her seven years ago that she would be reading the news, she would’ve laughed in their face.

But after all, catching up on what had been going in the world seemed like a logical choice. 20 minutes later, she was out the door.

To Rio’s absolute relief, Dr. Harkness’ office was located only a 15 minute walk from her new flat. This meant that she had more time than she needed – resulting in her stopping by a coffee shop to buy to-go.

She hadn’t had good coffee for so long, almost to the point where she’d forgotten how good it can taste. The prison coffee was drinkable, but far from the perfection of normal coffee.

She held the cup with both hands to let the warm liquid seep through and warm her up. She had forgotten that in early October, people usually wear scarfs, gloves, hats or something to keep the cold out. Rio was just walking around with her old jacket that Lilia had left for her in the flat.

She stopped by a corner shop to buy a pack of cigarettes and a lighter. She placed the cigarette to her lips, almost shaking in anticipation – or due to the cold weather – when she lit it.

Before prison, she had been determined to kick her smoking addiction. But she figured that she deserved one now. She took a sip of coffee and lit a cigarette, letting the combined taste of them rest on her tongue.

Instead of liking the taste, it just filled her with bittersweet nostalgia from before everything went wrong. Before she’d fucked it all up.

The office was in a normal apartment building, so Rio almost missed it at first. She put out the cigarette under her boot, swearing under her breath before walking in. Her goal today was to leave as early as possible, wanting to be out of this goddamn session as quickly as possible.

She walked inside and checked the map - Dr. Harkness’ office was on the seventh floor. She drummed her fingers on the gold handle that circled the mirrored elevator, waiting impatiently.

Dr. Harkness opened the door when she knocked with a feigned smile plastered on her face. She was dressed in a new ironed suit, this time in a deep purple. Her curls were placed on the top of her hair in a chaotic bun, and her big glasses still rested perfectly on the bridge of her nose.

“Goodmorning,” She greeted, opening the door for Rio to go in.

“Dr. Harkness,” Rio said back, the formality leaving a bitter taste on her tongue as she removed her jacket and hung it on a hook.

“No use for that, Rio. Call me Agatha,” she said, walking through the hall and into a room.

Rio followed with unsure steps. “Okay, Agatha,” She said, testing the name on her tongue.

Agatha smiled and sat down in an armchair placed in the middle of the room, nodding towards a gray chair that was placed alongside one wall.

“So, Rio,” Agatha started, placing a notebook in her hands and watching as Rio sat down on the chair in front of her, “Have you ever been to see a psychiatrist or anything else of sort earlier?”

Rio shrugged, “No.”

“Well then,” Agatha sighed. “ I think we should start with a brief overview.”

Rio nodded, feeling uncomfortable in this space. Something about this woman put her on edge.

“It’s not complicated, all I need you to do is answer my questions honestly, and we can have a discussion about things that have happened to you. Some days you might not feel like sharing, and I won’t force you to. But we’re here for a reason, and if you’re compliant during this process, I think it’ll go well.”

Rio shrugged, hating this overall. She’d never been a person to share her own personal experiences, it is no one’s business but her own – and those were words she’d lived by for as long as she could remember.

She dealt with her problems alone, because why would anyone else care?

“Do you want to start from the beginning?”

Rio looked around the room trying to convey her indifference without words. She didn’t really care what they did, if it ultimately led to her getting out of here.

“I’ll take that as a yes,” Agatha sighed. “How old are you?”

“29.”

“How are you parents?”

Rio looked at Agatha, almost feeling confused for a second. Surely Agatha knew all this already, it must say so in some file, some brief summary of her chaotic childhood and upbringing.

But Rio decided to play the game, “They’re well.”

“What are their names?” Agatha asked, smirking – Rio had walked right into that trap.

Rio was unimpressed, looking around the room, doing her best to avoid eye contact. “Which parents? My real ones?”

Agatha shrugged, “You decide.”

“Lilia is good,” Rio said eventually.

Agatha took a pen, preparing to write something down. “So, you see Lilia as your parent, I’m guessing?”

Rio shook her head.

“So who, then?”

“Can I get a glass of water?” Rio asked, wanting to prolong this process.

Agatha very obviously saw through the question. But nevertheless, she stood up and walked towards the kitchen – returning after a minute with two big glasses of water, placing one down in front of Rio and taking the other for herself.

“If you don’t want to answer a question right away, you don’t have to, you know?” Agatha said.

“I was just thirsty,” Rio tried, but judging by the look on Agatha’s face, she’s not convinced.

Rio hated it.

“I’m giving you a chance to respond to the questions I already know, otherwise I’ll just recap everything your file tells me about your past – don’t you want to be the one who tells it?”

Rio shrugged. Honestly, she didn’t really care if Agatha just recited her entire childhood in front of her, it would be kind of interesting.

Agatha sighed again, taking a big sip of water while scrambling through her pages. “It says here that your birth parents died when you were four years old.”

Rio nodded, “They did.”

“Did you feel like that still impacts you?”

“It happened 25 years ago, I’m over it now.”

Agatha looks at her, a curious look on her face, “Are you sure?”

Rio was surprised. No one had ever dug deeply into the loss of her parents more than the occasional ‘are you okay?’.

“Yes,” She responded instead, face empty of emotion.

Instead of responding, Agatha clicked her pen on and scribbled something done in her notebook.

Rio fought the urge to scream.

“So,” Agatha started again after a short pause, “After that, you moved between foster families for a long time.”

This time it doesn't come off as a question, more a statement. Rio just found it within herself to nod at that, not quite knowing what to respond.

“And at 15, Lilia took over your custody, and she kept it until you turned 18,” Agatha examined Rio carefully, almost with a gentleness in the sharpness of her eyes. “How’s your relationship with Lilia today?”

Rio hated this with her entire being, “It’s good.”

“You can do better than that,” Agatha simply stated, looking at her unimpressed.

Rio glared at her. “Lilia came to pick me up yesterday, and she fixed an apartment for me to stay in until I’m back on my feet.”

Agatha nodded, “Seems like a nice woman.”

“She tries her best,” Rio sighed.

For a second, Rio was scared that Agatha’s gonna dig deeper into that statement. Peel her open with her words, sparing no mercy as she forced out all the truths. But she didn’t. Instead, Agatha swiftly removed her glasses, placing them on the coffee table in front of her.

“Are you familiar with Freud’s theory of life?” Agatha asked instead.

Rio nodded, she’d heard about them in school, but wasn’t too interested.

“Not everyone believes it, but let's say we analyse your childhood on the basis of Freud’s theory,” Agatha said, starting to write some kind of timeline on the paper in front of her. “Since you were four, you’ve been moved around foster families – this was at the time of your phallic stage.”

Rio listened half-heartedly, resisting the urge to roll her eyes. She didn’t need anyone explaining her own life to her, especially not through the lens of some dead guy's weird obsessions.

Her attention drifted away from Agatha’s words, and instead focused on Agatha herself. Everything about her screamed control. Rio hated people like that. Hated the false niceties, the way they dipped in and out of her life like a tourist, never sticking around long enough to actually make an impact.

If Agatha genuinely thought that she was going to get anywhere with that crap, then she was sorely mistaken.

“You know this is bullshit, right?” she said, the snark in her tone cutting.

Agatha didn’t even blink. “Rio, I want you to come into these sessions with an open mind.”

“Nah, I think im good”, Rio shot back, leaning further back into the couch.

“If you don’t engage then this isn’t going to work”, Agatha said, her tone still maddeningly even.

Rio resisted the urge to slap her. Insted, she raised her eyebrow. “So… can I leave then?” “No.”

Rio had expected it, there was no way that she could get out of this that easily, but the simplicity of Agatha’s answer threw her for a second.

She glared at her. “How much do you make from this?”

Agatha tilted her head slightly. “Excuse me?”

“How much do they pay you to pretend to care?” Rio pressed, watching Agatha closely for any sign that she was getting under her skin.

“What makes you think I’m pretending?”

“I know how this works. You get me talking and then try to psychoanalyse me or some shit. Well, nice try. What if I just sit here in silence? You can’t make me talk.”

Agatha set her notebook down on her lap, looking at Rio with an expression that made her feel like she was being dissected.

“You’re right, Rio. I can’t make you do anything. If you want to sit here in silence for the rest of the session, we can. This is your space. It’s up to you what we do in it.”

Rio blinked, momentarily caught off guard. She opened her mouth to respond, before she caught herself. If she didn’t have to talk, then she wasn’t going too. Let Agatha try and see how much she could pry into Rio’s life if all she gave her was silence. Rio crossed her arms over her chest, glaring at the other woman in silence.

Agatha calmly returned to her notebook, the quote sound of pen on paper being the only noise in the room.

As the silence stretched on, Rio’s irritation grew. She had been expecting some reaction from Agatha, some kind of response that showed Rio was pushing her buttons. But instead she got… nothing . The longer they sat there, the more it felt like Agatha was winning some kind of unspoken battle, and Rio couldn’t stand it. She shifted in her chair, squirming under both the heavy weight of the silence and the ever growing realisation that maybe this wasn’t a fight she could easily win.

The quiet scratch of Agatha’s writing was a symbol of her defeat, and she couldn’t stand it.

“What are you writing?” She finally snapped.

Agatha didn’t even bother looking up. “I thought we weren’t talking.”

Rio’s jaw tightened. This woman was going to drive her insane. “You said that this is my session, and we can do whatever I want. Well, what I want is to know what you're writing.”

Agatha glanced up, and Rio could swear she saw a hint of amusement in her expression. Rio resisted the urge to stand up and stab her.

Agatha handed her the notebook.

Client Name: Rio Vidal

Session Date: October 14 2024

Initial Observations:

Defensiveness apparent from the outset. Arms crossed, slouched posture, avoids eye contact unless challenging.

Quick to deploy sarcasm and deflection as defense mechanisms. Exhibits need to maintain control of conversational dynamics.

Behavioral Notes:

Resistant to authority and structured environments.

Tendency to test boundaries through provocation (e.g., sarcastic remarks, dismissive tone).

Likely accustomed to being stereotyped as "difficult" or "uncooperative." Reinforces this perception to discourage further engagement.

Provocation as a tool for control: Pushes others away to preempt rejection.

Mistrust rooted in prior instability (e.g., foster care system experiences).

Underlying curiosity despite resistance (observed through questioning about note- taking).

Additional Notes:

Despite outward hostility, small indicators of engagement observed (e.g., leaning forward when reading notes, insistence on seeing written observations).

Possible avenue for rapport-building: validating feelings of frustration and skepticism without reinforcing defensive patterns.

Rio scoffed, practically chucking the notebook back at Agatha. “This is bullshit.”

“Then tell me what it should say”, Agatha said calmly, crossing her hands in her lap.

Rio opened her mouth, but no words came out. She clenched her teeth, refusing to give Agatha the satisfaction of a real response.

After a moment of letting Rio seethe, Agatha broke the silence. “Tell me, does being uncooperative make you feel a sense of control?”

Rio’s eyes narrowed. “What?”

“Do you enjoy pushing people away?”

Rio sat up straighter, staring daggers at the woman in front of her. “Yes, actually.”

Agatha’s lip twitched, a small, barely perceivable smile breaking through before she schooled her expression back to neutrality.

“What’s funny?” Rio spat.

“Nothing, nothing,” Agatha said, but the faint amusement in her voice was impossible to miss.

Rio clenched her fists. If she wasn’t on probation then she would have choked Agatha out until that stupid smirk was wiped off her face.

Agatha paused, closing the notebook and setting it down on the table between them. She leaned forward slightly, looking at Rio in a way that made her feel like one of the rats she had cut up in faith grade biology class.

“You're not used to people staying, are you?” Agatha said suddenly.

Rio’s stomach clenched. “What the fuck is that supposed to mean?”

“You push people away because it’s easier than letting them leave on their own. Probably a result of constantly moving foster homes. It gives you a sense of control.” Agatha continued, her tone steady and gentle in a way that was infuriating. “But that’s not going to happen here, Rio. You can be as uncooperative and unwilling as you would like, but the only way you get out of this is if I refer you on to another psychologist. And that’s not something I plan to do.”

Rio’s throat tightened and, for a brief moment, she didn’t know what to say. She felt picked apart, violated by this stranger sitting across from her.

“You don’t know anything about me” she snapped, her voice low.

Agatha simply shrugged. “You're right, I don’t. But I’d like to, when you're ready.”

What the fuck was Rio supposed to say to that?

She didn’t trust Agatha, why would she? From what Rio could tell, she was simply another idiot with a saviour complex who seemed to believe she knew Rio better than Rio knew herself. But, for the first time, in those 7 long years isolated from the rest of the world, Rio didn’t feel in control. Her usual tactic of keeping someone so far at arms reach that they never knew more than her name and her prison sentence hadn’t worked – and that terrified her.

The sound of an alarm snapped Rio out of her thoughts, and she snapped her head up to see Agatha standing in front of her.

“I think that's all we have time for today. It was lovely meeting you, Rio. I'll see you on Thursday.”

Rio just stared at her, speechless. She desperately searched for something to say, something to do to regain the power that she so desperately craved. But there was nothing.

So Rio did the only other thing that she could do. The thing that, if she thought about it hard enough, she has been doing all her life.

She ran.

Chapter 2

Chapter Notes

Hello!

Because this is a pretty dark fic (i mean Rio is a serial killer after all) we've added a few more tags for potential trigger warnings - so before starting this chapter I would say you should take a look at them and maybe even keep in mind to have an eye on potential new ones added later on!

Rio

Rio woke up with a newfound sense of freedom she hadn’t felt yesterday.

Sure, yesterday she was out of prison, but the session with Agatha had drained all her energy. She’d been thinking about Agatha ever since their session ended. She hadn’t wanted to, but it was something about the way she’d spoken, the way she’d looked at her like she cared – it all made Rio feel quite uneasy. Knowing that it will be at least two more days until they met again was liberating.

Today, she slept in, not bothering with setting an alarm since she had no obligations until later tonight.

For the first time in seven years, she was gonna meet up with her friends. She was honestly quite nervous about it; even though they had visited her when she was inside, meeting them now would be different.

But behind all that nervousness, she felt excited. She'd been stuck behind bars for seven years while the rest of the world carried on, and now finally she would be able to catch up with it. She couldn’t wait to hear all about their lives, and plus it gave her a good opportunity to rant about her new psychiatrist to them.

The day, however, she spent out shopping. The clothes she owned from seven years ago suddenly made her feel out of place – something she should have expected. She had a lot to restock on; food, makeup, clothes, furniture, decorations.

She didn’t cross it all off, of course, but when she got home in the afternoon she felt a new sense of satisfaction.

It didn’t last long.

Putting up the curtains and folding all her clothes was a lot more effort than Rio had remembered, so instead, she threw herself on the couch. To her dismay, she realised the TV still hadn’t been fixed, and she groaned loudly before standing up and stomping over to it.

She debated calling Lilia, unsure on whether or not she would understand how to work a cable, but she probably had higher chances than Rio ever did.

Eventually though, after a string of loud complaints – she wouldn’t surprise her if her new neighbors would call later – she figured it out. She cursed herself out for not understanding it earlier, resisting the urge to kick the TV in frustration.

She almost yelped in surprise when she heard a familiar voice from behind her. “What are you doing?”

Rio stood up, shaking her head before turning around, “Alice!”

Alice took off her leather jacket, hanging it on the back of the door. “Really, what are you doing?” She nodded towards the spot on the floor where Rio had just been hunched over fighting for her life.

“I was trying to start the TV,” Rio sighed, throwing herself back on the couch.

Alice smirked, “You could’ve just asked me to do that.”

“Well it’s fixed now isn’t it,” Rio nodded towards it and grabbed the remote from the vintage coffee table. “How are you, Alice?”

Alice shrugged, sitting down beside her on the couch, “I’m good. We’ve missed you though.”

Rio felt a smile tug at her lips, “Have you?”

“Of course,” Alice exclaimed, hitting Rio lightly on the shoulder. “Jen might not admit it, but I will.”

As if on cue, Jen entered her apartment. Her friend’s habit of not knocking would never fail to disturb Rio, but after all these years, she’d grown used to it.

“Hello!” Jen announced her presence, as if her arrival wasn’t obvious enough already.

“Jen,” Rio greets back, feeling a little out of place suddenly, having both of her old best friends in her flat like nothing had happened. She wanted to pretend nothing had, and go back to the way things were. But there was still an underlying feeling of difference. Something in their dynamic surely must’ve shifted during those seven years.

But Jen walked into her apartment, almost like she owned the place. She looked around at the lack of decorations and grimaced slightly, “You really need to elevate this space, Rio.”

Rio smirks, feeling a wave of nostalgia crash over her. Jen had been the one to help her decorate her apartment when she’d first moved out from Lilia’s – which had only been a year and a half before she’d landed herself in prison.

“I could use your help,” Rio said, leaning her head on the side of the couch and looking up at her like a lost puppy.

“You really could,” Alice chimed in, a smirk on her face as she looked around the poorly decorated and furnished room.

Jen smiled, looking unamused at Rio’s antics. She walked through the living room and sat down in an old-looking armchair – probably a piece of Lilia’s old furniture. Rio really needed to buy new stuff.

As Jen sat down, a bit of the fabric of her skirt turned up, revealing her ankle. On it, Rio could see a tattooed symbol she hadn’t seen for years – the three of pentacles.

Everyone in The Coven had matching ones. Rio’s was placed on the side of her back, by her shoulder blade, meaning that she wasn’t able to actually see it herself. But she knew it was there, and the thought comforted her.

Rio had first been introduced to Jen and Alice in her teenage years through Lilia. Being introduced to The Coven was the best and the worst thing Rio had ever experienced. From it, she gained two best friends and a wonderful parental figure – but it also ultimately led to her imprisonment.

Lilia had taken her in when she was 15, already on the wrong track in life. She had been one step away from landing herself in juvie, and had gained a notorious reputation of running away from her foster families.

This time, when social services found her, she had been walking down the highway barefoot and high out of her mind on cannabis. Lilia had been her last destination before ultimately being sent to a youth detention centre.

Rio never expected to stay around for too long. One night, she’d shoplifted a bottle of vodka from a local convenience store and chugged it down in her room. Lilia had found her puking her guts out in the bathroom. After that, Rio was sure she was going to be kicked out.

Instead, Lilia had brought her downtown the next day and introduced her to a group named The Coven – a group of women whom Rio would come to learn were contract killers, mainly targeting rich and powerful men.

Before too long, they’d become Rio’s family, protecting her in a way she hadn’t felt since her parents died. After so long being shoved around by the system, it was amazing to finally feel a sense of purpose.

Rio was good at it, too. Alarmingly good.

Lilia would continuously joke that killing must’ve been in Rio’s blood, and that’s why she took her in; sensing it from the moment Rio first showed up at her doorstep.

Honestly, Rio was grateful – who knows where she would’ve ended up without Lilia’s care and The Coven. Dead in a ditch was her best guess.

Alice and Jen had joined shortly before her and, being around the same age as Rio, the three of them had turned to each other in times of doubt and difficulty.

The three of them had a relationship like sisters. Rio knew she could always count on them, and they could always count on her. The bickering between them had become her safe space – the only place where she’d ever felt like home.

“I booked a table for us at 6 pm, so we better get going,” Jen announced, standing up and walking to grab her coat. “Unless you two would rather skip out on eating?”

Rio groaned and shook her head, standing up as well, “I’m starving.”

They made their way through the crowded Tuesday afternoon streets. The place Jen had booked wasn’t overly fancy, but it still fit the occasion; celebrating Rio being free.

Maybe ‘freedom’ was the wrong word. She wasn’t entirely in control of her own life. No, instead she had Agatha and those fucking therapy sessions looming over her. Every week for three years, and Rio loathed it.

She was going to have to come up with some way to make those sessions survivable, or else she was going to find herself back behind bars for yet another count of manslaughter.

When they sat down in their booth, Rio hunched forward immediately, leaning closely over the table and looking between Alice and Jen opposite her. “What do I do about this psychiatrist?”

Jen took a sip of her water, “What do you mean?”

“She’s driving me nuts, trying to psychoanalyze me and shit. I need to gain ground on her.”

“At least it’s a woman,” Alice said. “One time social services assigned me a man and it was awful.”

“Is she at least hot?” Jen asked.

Rio smirked, “Ask me when I’ve had a few drinks.”

Jen and Alice gave each other a knowing look.

“Guys!” Rio exclaimed, trying to gain their attention again.

“Just be your normal, annoying self. I’m sure she will get sick of you soon enough” Jen said, grinning.

The waiter interrupted them with the menus. Rio looked up at him, annoyed.

But the silence gave her time to evaluate. If she was gonna go to these godawful sessions, she could at least enjoy them. She didn’t quite know what would pull on Agatha’s strings, but she’d figure it out.

Agatha

It had been almost ten years since Agatha had attended her first AA meeting, and her hatred for it never faded.

Every Tuesday she would wake up feeling dreadful, trying and failing to come up with an excuse to back out. And now, as she made her way through the crowded streets, she could still feel the anxiety looming over her.

Thankfully, it wasn’t a far walk. The autumn air was already getting to her, creeping in underneath her clothes and leaving a trail of goosebumps in its wake.

She yanked open the door and walked into the warm lobby. A lot of people had already gathered, engaging in small-talk and loading up on coffee and cookies to the best of their abilities.

Agatha would like to think that she didn’t fit in here. That these people were all beneath her, struggling way more than she does. But every meeting she was proven wrong – she was exactly where she was supposed to be.

She was the first one to enter the big room, taking a place on a chair in the big circle that had been created.

A stupid concept, in her opinion. It was supposed to foster a sense of community, a way to help them connect or whatever bullshit Steve had rambled on about everytime she complained.

It was humiliating, in truth, to have everyone's eyes on her when she spoke, but Agatha had eventually learnt to deal with it.

The only reason she was still turning up was because, as much as hated to admit it, the group did help. She’d met a lot of men and women throughout the years, people who had started out as strangers but now knew some of the secrets she kept closest to her chest. She’d struggled with sobriety at first, but eventually a day had turned into a week, a week turned into a month, and now she was close to getting her 3 year chip.

Tony sat down beside her a few minutes later, his three piece suit out of place. When Agatha first met him just over a year ago, she had thought that he had the perfect life. He was a rich business owner with a wife and kid, a bunch of money, and on the surface an extremely well put together life.

Agatha had expected him to be shallow and egotistical, but instead he’d become one of the best friends that she’d made in the program. When he first joined, his wife had been threatening to leave him and take their daughter as a result of his alcohol problem. The prospect of losing his family had pushed him into recovery, and now he was almost ten months sober. Someone like Tony being there too helped Agatha feel better about herself. It helped her feel a little less like a failure.

“Tony,” she greeted him. “How’s Pepper?”

“She’s good,” Tony sighed. “She and Morgan are upstate for a getaway over the week.”

“I see,” Agatha nodded. “And you’re not with them?”

Tony took a sip of coffee out of the thermos in his hand. He always bought his own coffee – probably finding himself above the free coffee being served at the meetings. Agatha didn’t blame him. She tried the coffee once, and never again.

“We fought this weekend,” Tony explained with a deep sigh. “Apparently I work too much.”

“Well you can never work too much,” Agatha said. She knew the falseness of that statement, but she also knew Tony wouldn’t listen either way. She wasn’t at work, so she might as well validate him when she had the chance.

“That’s what I said,” Tony exclaimed, sounding exhausted. “She didn’t agree though.”

Agatha hummed, her focus fading out of the conversation and onto the old man, Steve, sitting down at the other end of the circle. Another proof that there really was no age requirement to go here – he was just a 75 year old man who lost his wife.

His story was beautiful, in truth. But he had spoken so slowly and so raggedly that Agatha lost interest about three minutes in – it was Tony who had retold it to her afterwards.

“Have you visited Nicky recently?” Tony asked carefully.

For a second, Agatha froze. It still felt weird when people asked about him, but Tony was gentle. He knew how to approach the subject, so Agatha turned to him and met the sad look in his eyes.

“I changed his flowers last week,” she said quietly, forcing her voice not to break.

Tony put a hand on her back, not saying anything. Sometimes, Agatha liked this type of comfort. The silent but still present comfort. It was better than people going on about how sorry they felt for her, or how they were praying for her.

The meeting started when Steve cautiously stood up, using his walker to stabilize himself and clearing his throat. “Good afternoon,” he greeted.

Agatha had to resist the urge to roll her eyes, crossing her legs and leaning further back into the chair.

“So,” Steve continued, speaking infuriatingly slowly. Agatha wondered for a second who was in charge when they put him as the chairman for this group – couldn’t they have picked Tony or someone who could at least speak or move quicker than a snail? “Today, I think we have a few new people in the group.”

Agatha looks around, seeing a woman she’d never seen before. She was sitting impossibly low into the chair, sitting rather ungracefully. Her demeanour did not match how put together she looked. Blond perfectly straight down to her shoulders, a shirt, cargo pants and boots.

“Why don’t you introduce yourself?” Steve suggested.

“Okay,” The blond woman started, sitting up straighter. “My name is Carol Danvers. I’m here because I want to fix my drinking problem.”

Agatha, once again, fought the urge to roll her eyes. Why the fuck else would she be here?

“I lost my girlfriend a few months ago. We were both in the air force, her plane crashed,” Carol stifled a sob. “I now have custody over our child, Monica, and I want to be there for her.”

Steve nods empathetically, “Thank you for sharing.” He took a deep breath, looking around the circle, “Anyone else feel like sharing their experiences with Carol and the group?”

A younger woman, her blond hair in a braid over her shoulder clears her throat. “I can,” She said, raising her hand a little. “Hello, my name is Yelena,” She introduces herself, russian accent seeping through every word.

Agatha took a deep breath. Out of everyone in the group, Yelena’s story was still the one that got her the most.

“When I was six, me and my sister were trafficked – forced to grow up and work for a criminal organization called The Red Room. I got out a few years ago, but the memories still haunt me, you know?” Yelena takes a deep breath, “I don’t know where my sister is. If she’s alive, even. And it scares me. I took up drinking after I got out. But I want my life back together, you know? I don’t want to let The Red Room control my entire life.”

Steve nodded solemnly. “Thank you for sharing your story, Yelena.”

Yelena shrugged, “Whatever.”

Agatha braced herself, she knew she needed to talk about her experience. It was important, both for her and for the group, but she hated it.

“Who else?” Steve asked, once again looking around the group for a volunteer.

Agatha cleared her throat, “I guess.”

The entire group turned their gaze towards her, and it made her skin crawl. She wanted to run away, to hide somewhere and never show her face again. She hated sharing her own experiences, she hated the empathy people gave her.

“My name is Agatha,” She started, the words tasting bitter on her tongue. “I’m here because – well – my son died,” She gave a bitter laugh. “And, after that, I buried myself in alcohol. I couldn’t take it, couldn’t help but to blame myself for his death – even if I know it wasn’t my fault. I wanted to stop drinking because, well, Nicky would want me to. He was always there for me, such a caring, sweet child. And now he’s gone, and I know he looks down at me in disappointment, I can feel it.”

She angrily brought her hand to her face,rubbing at her eyes furiously in an attempt to stop herself crying. She hated crying, especially in front of strangers. It showed a kind of weakness that she rarely put on.

The meeting ended an hour later, and Agatha made a beeline for the door, wanting to get home as quickly as possible. Tony followed her, catching her right at the bottom of the stairs, “Do you want to grab dinner?”

Agatha wanted to, really. It wouldn’t hurt to socialize at least a little. But the meetings always drained her, and all she wanted to do was curl up under a blanket and block out the rest of the world;

“Maybe next week?” She smiled dismissively, beginning to walk away. “Hope you clear things up with Pepper,” she said over her shoulder, turning the corner and going home.

______

Thursday morning came around, and Agatha very nearly slept past her alarm.

Her headache drummed in her temples as she put on clothes and made breakfast. The only session she had today was with Rio, and she was honestly kind of looking forward to it. Rio was a difficult person to work with, not compliant nor a good listener – but Agatha liked the challenge.

Few clients during her years had been this interesting.

The office where she held her appointments was only a mere five minute walk from her own apartment – which today she was especially grateful for.

She walked with hurried steps, drinking her coffee out of a thermos because didn’t have time to finish it at home. It was frosty that morning, but the coffee warmed up her insides.

With ten minutes to spare, she walked into the office building. She took off her coat, and prepared her notes from the last session. Sitting down, she sipped languidly on her coffee and scrolled through the newspaper on her phone.

Her attention was ripped from her phone the second Rio storms into the office. Agatha rolled her eyes, did her manners stop at knocking now?

She stood up, placing her phone on the table and walking over to the hall, where Rio was furiously taking off her coat. She leaned against the doorway, watching as Rio struggled.

“Goodmorning,” She said, amused.

Rio grunted, “It’s so fucking cold.”

Agatha chucked, trying to take a gentle approach, “Do you want coffee?”

Rio nodded, walking into the main room and sitting down comfortably on the couch, manspreading and looking around the room, a painting catching her attention.

Agatha huffed, leaving Rio on the couch and instead starting the coffee machine.

“Why do you have these ugly paintings?” Rio asked from the other room.

Agatha walked out of the kitchen, standing in the doorway to see what Rio was referring to. “What?”

“These,” Rio said again, pointing towards a horrible painting of Sigmund Freud on a wall.

“Oh,” Agatha sighed. “Don’t ask me, I want it down.”

“Then take it down,” Rio complained. “I don’t want a man staring me down when I share private shit.”

“But you haven’t shared anything, have you?” Agatha mused, walking into the kitchen again.

She could hear Rio scoff from the other room, and a faint annoyed mumbling.

When she returned, she placed one mug of coffee on the table in front of Rio, and settled into her armchair with the other.

“So,” Agatha began, keeping her tone as neutral and non-threatening as possible. “I thought we could talk about your childhood today, specifically the time before your parents passed.”

Rio raised an eyebrow, but to Agatha’s surprise she didn’t deflect. Instead she just shrugged, “Sure, what do you want to know?”

Agatha blinked. She wasn’t sure why Rio was suddenly being cooperative, but she carried on anyway. “Let’s start with something simple. What’s one of your earliest memories with them?”

“They travelled a lot for work. I lived with my aunt quite a lot of the time,” Rio said smoothly, leaning back into the couch.

It took Agatha’s brain a second to process that Rio had actually given her an answer to her questions. She picked up her pen, hovering over the page. “That must have been difficult. Did you miss them when they were away?”

Rio tilted her head, almost as if she were actually considering the question, “Yeah I did, but, you know, they were doing important stuff, and even if I didn’t understand that as a kid, I do now.”

Agatha wondered for a brief second if she’d woken up in a parallel universe.

“What kind of work did they do?” She said, not wanting to spook Rio’s newfound streak of honesty.

Rio smiled faintly, fixing Agatha with a look of such sincerity and innocence that it almost made her scoff.

“They were undercover agents. Secret branch of the FBI and all that.”

There it was . Agatha resisted the urge to roll her eyes. She knew what Rio was trying to do, she was trying to regain control by lying her ass off, and it was clear that she didn’t give a shit if Agatha actually believed her. She could tell that Rio was waiting for something, probably for Agatha to snap at her. She wasn’t going to give Rio the satisfaction.

“The FBI. Of course. What kind of work did they do?”

“They were posing as a family as a part of this huge operation. Like world-ending level shit. Sometimes they had to leave to do recon or complete missions. The whole family thing was totally a cover,” Rio’s tone was completely casual and her body language was relaxed, but Agatha could see the look in her eyes – the challenging, hungry look that practically begged Agatha to challenge her.

“That sounds…unusual.” Agatha said simply, taking a sip of her coffee.

“Oh it was”, said Rio, nodding solemnly. “We had to move around constantly as well whenever their cover got blown. One time, some guy they were investigating came to the house and we had a whole shootout. I was only four but dad had taught me how to use a gun and I took him down. Ugh, they were so proud. Said I have a figure in the family business.”

Agatha closed her notebook with a sigh.

“What?”, Rio questioned, feigning innocence. “You don’t believe me?”

“Rio, I know what you're doing.”

“Yeah, I’m speaking my truth. Hey, speaking of truths, what’s your deal, Dr Harkness. Are you married? Divorced? Into women?

“That’s not relevant to our session.”

“Sure it is. A patient client relationship has to go both ways, right? How can you help me if I don’t know anything about you? Have you ever been in love? You seem lonely.”

“That’s inappropriate, Rio,” Agatha said firmly, meeting Rio’s wolfish grin with her own annoyed expression.

Rio simply shrugged, kicking her legs up to rest on the arm of the sofa.

Agatha took a breath, forcing herself to not give in to Rio’s attempt of getting under her skin.“Let’s refocus. What about after your parents passed? How did you adjust to foster care?”

“Oh that was wild. My first home was a front for an underground fight club. They saw my potential immediately by the way. I practically became a billionaire after that-“

“Right, that’s enough.” Agatha said sharply, her tone snappier than she intended it to be. Rio’s grin only widened. “Touchy, aren’t you? You're cute when you're annoyed.”

Agatha took a deep breath.

“Rio, you can’t lie your way out of this. I don’t know what you're trying to achieve, but you're only making this process longer for yourself.”

Rio leaned forward. “Do you get hit on alot? Because honestly, if you weren’t my therapist I would totally fu-”

Agatha cleared her throat, cutting Rio off. The look on Rio’s face made it obvious that she knew she had gotten a reaction, and Agatha hated it. She pulled back, watching Rio like a hawk watches a mouse before lunging.

“I’m giving you homework this week. I want you to keep a dream journal and bring it to our next sessions.”

The cocky look on Rio’s face faded slightly. “A fucking dream journal? Do I look like a teenage girl to you?”

Agatha felt a sting of satisfaction. “You look like someone avoiding vulnerability. So unless you’d prefer another method, for example getting Lilia to come in here and I get everything I need to know out of her instead, I suggest you start writing.”

Rio crossed her arms tightly over her chest, fixing Agatha with a look that could only be described as murderous. “What if I just don’t show up?” she spat.

Agatha didn’t miss a beat. “You can either come to my office at 10am on Monday with your journal, or I can arrange a visit with your probation officer to your flat. It is entirely your choice, Rio.”

Rio’s jaw clenched, and for a second Agatha was worried that she would lob the coffee mug at her head. She didn’t flinch though. Instead she opened up her notebook, flicking through the pages until she found Rio’s page and started writing. “Close the door on your way out,” she said coolly.

She didn’t need to look at Rio to feel her rage. The tension in the room was suffocating.

With a sharp, frustrated exhale, Rio stood up and stormed out of the office, slamming the door with enough force that it made Agatha flinch.

Chapter 3

Rio

Rio threw the journal angrily on her bed.

It landed with a thud, bouncing a little on the mattress before laying there, abandoned in the sheets.

Rio had never heard of something more bullshit in her entire life – A dream journal? Really?

Of all the ridiculous and useless things Agatha could have asked her to do, this had to be one of the worst. As if it wasn’t enough that Agatha was trying to analyze her childhood, she now had to do it to her subconscious? Insane.

Not to mention that Rio never remembered a dream, ever.

She’d tried to remember, more for entertainment, when she woke up Friday morning. But it was entirely futile – the dream had slipped her mind the second she woke up. And even if she’d remembered it, would it have been anything interesting?

Rio was sceptical.

She scowled at it, hoping it would catch fire under the pressure of her eyes, and groaned loudly when it didn’t work.

Why was her life so unfair?

Alice, ever on time, entered her bedroom. “What are you doing?”

Rio groaned. “Do you ever knock? And I'm annoyed, obviously,” She stated plainly, like that would give enough context.

“About?” Alice asked, leaning against the doorframe.

“My psychiatrist gave me a stupid dream journal,” Rio explained, throwing herself headfirst on the bed.

“A dream journal?” Alice asked, confused.

“She wants me to write down what I dream about so she can analyze them or some shit,” Rio said, her words muted against the pillow.

She heard Alice snicker. “You can’t be serious.”

Rio sat up, picking up the journal and waving it in the air frantically.

“This is insane, right? Like she has to be taking the piss.”

Alice shrugged. “Maybe she’s trying to get creative with you. Honestly I can’t blame her for that, you’d make a terrible patient.”

Rio glared at her. “You're so helpful Alice. Really. Thank you for your incredibly insightful and unwanted commentary.”

“Anytime,” Alice started, sitting down at the foot of the bed, grinning. “Now hurry up, you need to get ready. I’m hungry.”

“Is Jen not coming?” Rio asked, standing up.

Alice shook her head, “She had some work to do. Said she might join us later if she can.”

Rio blinked, her aggravation briefly replaced by disappointment. Still, she knew that work had to come first. She quickly shook the feeling off, standing up and walking to her wardrobe.

“I haven’t had a night out for seven years,” She said, looking through her wardrobe for anything presentable to wear. “Jen be damned, I’m gonna enjoy this.”

Alice smirked, “That’s the spirit.”

After a minute of chaotic searching, Rio settled on a black, tight-fitting, long sleeved shirt. It definitely wasn’t anything out of the ordinary – but it was between that and a colorful blouse that probably belonged to Lilia 30 years ago.

“What happens if you just don’t do it? I mean she can’t force you right?”

“Don’t do what?”

“The journal,” Alice clarified.

Rio scoffed. “She said that either I show up with it for our next session, or else she’s coming by my flat with my fucking probation officer.”

Alice’s jaw dropped. “You're kidding.”

“I wish. She’s a literal nightmare.”

Rio sat on the edge of her bed to tie her shoe laces.

“Well, what better way of distracting yourself from it than downing a bunch of alcohol?”

“Now that is a plan I can get behind.”

*****

Walking through the busy Friday night streets, they still had no plan on where and what to eat. Which would soon become a struggle because Alice, nor Rio for that matter, was fun to be around hungry.

Walking past a small, intimate middle eastern restaurant, Rio could feel her stomach growling. “Falafel?” She asked, looking hopefully at Alice.

“Thought you’d never ask,” Alice sighed, holding the glass door open for Rio.

Rio walked into the warmth, feeling it engulfing her as she took a seat by a small table in the corner.

Alice soon followed suit, taking off her leather jacket, “What do you want?”

Rio thought for a second, “Could you just order me a roll? And a coke, please.”

Alice nodded. “Coming right up,” She said over her shoulder, walking over to the counter to order.

Rio smiled, relaxing into the chair and pulling up her phone. She’d bought it earlier that day and all the new settings and features were still mind blowing to her – she for sure needed to ask Alice about them later.

Soon enough, Alice returned with their food and Rio could feel her mouth watering. She hadn’t had falafel in a while, and god, she had missed it.

As they ate, Alice filled Rio on an upcoming date she had with this girl she’d met while out of recon. It felt good, chatting about trivial things such as dating. Prison hadn’t exactly been the place for friendly small talk.

It also helped her take her mind of Agatha and that stupid fucking dream journal. Rio was out of prison now, but she still wasn’t exactly free. That woman was infuriating and held far too much control over Rio’s life for her to feel comfortable. Still, at least she was fun to try and fluster.

After nearly being kicked out for overstaying, they stepped back outside onto the now quieter street.

They strolled along the sidewalk in search of a good bar. Eventually, they settled on a place located in a cozy neighborhood

This early in the night, only a few tables were full – the majority of them mostly composed of older people probably soon to be switched out by younger ones.

Rio walked over to the bar, ordering a beer for the both of them and waiting impatiently for the bartender to finish them, drumming her fingers against the counter. Meanwhile, Alice took a seat by the entrance so they had a good view of the street outside.

People watching had become a hobby of theirs. Watching people with their pets, teenagers fighting or old couples – anything really. They loved making up stories, or just judging the people as they walked by.

Rio walked over to the table, sitting down facing the window and giving a knowing smirk to Alice. Relief washing over her that Alice hadn’t forgotten about their old ways.

“So tell me about prison,” Alice said, leaning back and bringing the pint to her lips.

Rio raised one eyebrow, “What do you want to know?”

“Anything,” Alice shrugged. “Any funny stories?”

Rio thought for a second, “I don’t know, probably.”

“Did you have any friends in there?”

“Well,” Rio said, pausing to take a sip of beer, “Yes, of course. I’m a very friendly person.”

Alice almost choked.

Rio smirked, “I got a girlfriend and everything.”

“In prison?” Alice asked in disbelief.

“Well, as far as one can have a girlfriend in prison,” Rio explained. “We mostly fucked, you know? But it was nice, I guess.”

“And you’re telling me this now? ”

“Her name is Hela, but she’s probably in for life so it doesn’t really matter now.”

“Really?” Alice said, sounding intrigued. “What did she do?”

“Cold blooded murder,” Rio explained like it wasn’t a big deal at all.

“So not much different from you then?” Alice smirked.

Rio put her finger to her lips, silencing her. “I was convicted for Involuntary manslaughter thank you very much,” Rio said, shaking her head, “Totally different thing.”

“We both know that’s not true.”

Rio smirked, leaning back in her chair. “Speaking of Hela, what number are you on now?”

Alice groaned, taking another sip of her drink. “It’s not a competition, Rio.”

Rio raised an eyebrow, her smirk widening. “Isn’t it though?”

Alice rolled her eyes, sighing softly. “Fine, fifteen.”

Rio burst out laughing, drawing a few disapproving looks from strangers scattered around the cafe. Alice’s face reddened and she kicked Rio under the table.

“Fifteen?! I was out of action for seven years and you're still behind me?! That’s pathetic.” Rio snorted.

Alice narrowed her eyes and kicked her again, harder. “Well, what are you on then?” Rio grinned. “Twenty two.”

Alice groaned dramatically. “Well at least I wasn’t stupid enough to get caught. Unlike you.”

The grin on Rio’s face disappeared instantly, replaced by a sharp glare. “That was not my fault. How was I supposed to know the CCTV hadn’t been properly disabled? Besides, it could have been worse. I only went down for ‘involuntary manslaughter’.”

“The fact that they brought the accidental angle is honestly impressive, especially after the state you left the body in.”

Rio grinned. “What can I say? I’m a very convincing liar.”

Alice scoffed, “Anyway, I wanna hear more about prison. Did you meet any psychos?”

Rio thought for a second, scanning through memories, trying to remember everyone. “Oh god,” She said eventually, stumbling upon a memory. “There was this very annoying woman who I shared rooms with for a while.”

“What did she do?”

“I think she kidnapped some child or something. She was clearly insane,” Rio explained, frowning at the memory. “Think she was in a mental hospital or something before and all.”

Alice nodded, waving her hand as a gesture for Rio to continue.

“She drove me up the walls I swear . She wouldn’t stop talking about it and she would wake me up in the middle of the night. Sometimes, she was just a nice woman, and I almost forgot why she was locked in – but other times I wanted to fucking kill her.”

“Good thing you didn’t,” Alice simply stated, her lips quirking up in a smile.

“She also sleep walked like an idiot,” Rio continued her relentless rant, filled with rage at the mere memory. “Sometimes, I would wake up in the middle of night and she would just be pacing around the cell.”

“Is she still locked up?”

Rio shook her head, “She was released almost five years ago, I think. But I still had to spend two-”

She was interrupted by the door into the little bar opening – her mouth agape. Alice followed her eyesight, landing on two women walking in.

“You’re kidding me?” Rio mutters, completely stunned.

Alice leaned forward, scanning the room like she was missing something.

“That’s literally her,” Rio exclaimed, taking a big sip of beer to compensate for the shock. “Her?” Alice asked.

“The woman from prison,” Rio explained, whispering still.

“The red haired woman?” Alice asked, voice entirely too loud for Rio’s liking.

Rio hushed her, looking as Wanda and a dark haired woman she had yet to see the face of, crossed the room and went to the bar.

“What the hell are the odds of that?” Rio asked in disbelief, louder this time, and turned her attention back to Alice.

Alice looked pointedly between Rio and the bar, “You should maybe go up and say ‘hi’, you know? Manners and all that.”

Rio hesitated for a little, not at all feeling like interacting with Wanda again. She’d hoped to never run into her again when she left prison five years ago – but fate, apparently, had other plans.

With a deep defeated sigh, Rio stood up, abandoning Alice by the table and walking over to the bar.

She stopped for a few seconds, trying to control the anger that was bubbling up inside of her. Reaching the bar, she took a place next to Wanda, leaning casually on the counter. “Wanda,” She said, feigning politeness.

But it wasn’t Wanda who answered her, instead, the dark haired woman on Wanda’s side turned around. It is with surprise, and huge regret, a familiar face– Agatha.

For a second, Rio stood there, completely stunned. Agatha, really?

She blinked hard, trying to clear her vision – maybe the alcohol had a bigger effect on her than she originally thought?

But no, Agatha seemed just as shocked to see Rio, looking at her with her eyebrows raised. “Rio?” She asked, like it needed confirmation.

The name made Wanda turn as well, matching Agatha’s expression by a tenfold.

Silence stretched between the three of them, until Wanda spoke up. “Rio,” she said, faking a smile, “I haven’t seen you in five years! How long have you been out?”

Rio shrugged, looking at Agatha who was still standing in silence, watching the interaction.

“Less than a week,” she explained, faking a smile. “Agatha, here, can tell you all about it,” She then muttered, rather bitterly. “But I really need to get back to my friend. It was nice seeing you.”

Rio promptly turned around, almost ready to run out. She kept her posture composed, but her facial expression screamed of panic. Seeing Wanda was a whiplash enough – but Agatha as well? There was surely nothing that could top this.

She’d spoken too soon. Returning to the table with Alice, she sat down with a sigh. “Guess what?”

Alice looked at her with a smirk on your face, “She told you to fuck off?”

“No,” Rio shook her head and leaned in closer, her chest almost touching the table. “She’s with my psychiatrist.”

Alice opened her mouth in shock, “What?”

“Yeah,” Rio said, taking a big sip out of her pint, trying to calm herself. “I mean this is literally a fucking nightmare.”

“What’s a nightmare?” A familiar voice asked from behind her.

Rio stiffened, her fingers tightening on her glass. Reluctantly, she turned around, seeing Wanda and Agatha standing, hovering over their table.

“Can we sit down here?” Wanda asked, carefully but already inviting herself by pulling out the chair next to Rio and sitting down.

Agatha hesitated for a moment before sliding into the chair beside Alice.

Rio rolled her eyes, “I swear this has to break some kind of code, socialising with patients outside of work.”

“It’s not considered a breach if the encounter is accidental,” Agatha said. “And all the tables are taken. We’ll be out of your hair the second a table is free.”

Rio had to stifle a groan, leaning back into her chair and examining the women who’d just invaded their space.

“Hope you wrote about said nightmare in the dream journal, Rio,” Agatha smirked.

Rio had to hold herself back from launching at her.

Why did the universe hate her?

“Wait,” Wanda said, looking between Agatha and Rio, “You’re her client too?”

“Yeah,” Rio muttered, looking down into the empty glass in front of her, annoyed. The sudden realisation hit her, causing her to abruptly lift her head, “ Too? ”

Agatha gave Wanda a sharp look, but Wanda either didn’t see it or ignored it. “I mean, I’m not anymore. I went to Agatha when I got out for a few years,” Wanda explained, completely ignorant to the way Agatha was glaring daggers at her.

Rio smirked, “Oh, so you have no problem having intimate relations with clients?” Agatha huffed, “She’s not a client anymore.”

“Still,” Rio mused. “So you’re here on a date or?”

Wanda shook her head.

“Amazing,” Rio said slowly, not breaking eye contact with Agatha.

Agatha, in response, quickly looked away – instead focusing on something behind Rio’s shoulder. Rio diverts her gaze, noticing the way Agatha drummed nervously on her glass.

For a second, she’s confused – is Agatha really drinking straight vodka? But after a second of critical thinking, she realizes that it must be water.

Watching Agatha in this context opens up an entirely new world view.

She’d imagined Agatha to be a professional. Someone who looks so sharp and composed all the time, being friends with an old client? That was not professional nor controlled.

Rio almost felt a smirk ghost her face at the thought. God, she’s gonna have a blast with this newfound information.

But something within her felt a sting of jealousy as well – Wanda, out of all people became friends with Agatha? Her therapy must be working wonders, or, Agatha’s just as deeply fucked up as Wanda is.

Both thoughts were equally horrifying.

Rio excused herself and walked over to the bar, ordering another round of beer when Wanda showed up beside her. Rio groaned, leaning her head back and almost praying that someone would be kind enough to get her out of the hellhole that is this bar.

“A glass of red, please,” Wanda ordered, smiling sweetly at the bartender before turning her focus to Rio. “So how have you been?”

“Is Agatha not drinking anything?” Rio asked instead, far too uninterested in the conversation to even pretend to be.

Wanda shook her head, looking over to the table where Agatha was now talking to Alice, almost something of a frown on her face. Rio wondered silently what the hell they were talking about, gaining that reaction out of Agatha – but quickly pulled herself out of it.

“Why?”

Wanda shrugged, “She doesn’t want to.”

Rio looked at Agatha confused, who’d now pulled up her phone and was typing something down. “Never met a forty something year old woman who doesn’t drink,” Rio stated plainly, grabbing the two glasses of beer from the counter. “How old is Agatha, anyway?” “46,” Wanda said.

Hot.

“So how did you two go from patient-therapist to,” Rio thought for a second, not knowing quite what to call them, “Lovers?”

Wanda glared at her. “Friends,” she corrected through her teeth.

“Whatever,” Rio shrugged. “How did you do it? I assume it wasn’t with your sparkling personality.”

Wanda scoffed, “Believe it or not, I’m a good person.”

Rio laughed out loud at that, catching the bartender's attention who looked at them suspiciously. Rio waved him off with her hand, grabbing Wanda’s wrist and forcefully pulling her closer, “Tell me how you did it.”

Wanda looked unfazed, meeting Rio’s gaze with equal measure, “When you become friends with someone, Rio, it’s not usually a process you can recite,” Wanda practically spitted out. “But you don’t have many of those, do you?”

Rio rolled her eyes, ignoring the insult. “But you must’ve done something.”

Wanda smirked. “Why do you want to know?”

“I don’t-” Rio groaned, giving up and taking a step back. Her attention shifted to Agatha again. She and Alice engaged in a light conversation, but by the looks of it, neither of them seemed pleased to be.

“Whatever you say,” Wanda said slyly. “Oh, yeah, I totally forgot about Hela. How’s it going with her anyway? Or how did it go between you two?”

Rio looked back at Wanda sharply, “Why do you care?”

Wanda shrugged, “I don’t. But, you two seemed pretty close when I was released, I was just wondering if it ever went somewhere.”

“Yeah,” Rio sighed. “It did.”

“Ended badly?” Wanda asked, taking a sip out of her wine that the bartender had just poured up for her.

Rio shook her head. “No, we just saw each other less, and then by the end of my sentence we barely saw each other at all – but we’re still good friends.”

Wanda raised her eyebrows.

“Well,” Rio backtracks. “We were good friends. She’s in for life, I think, so the chances of us meeting again are slim.”

“Never say never,” Wanda smirked, but a hint of something else hid behind her eyes – hostility. “Maybe you’ll get yourself locked up again.”

Rio scoffed, not entertaining that thought which sent shivers down her spine. “If you’ve forgotten – I, unlike you, didn’t actually deserve to be in there. In case you missed it, it was involuntary manslaughter.”

Wanda laughed mockingly, “You and I both know you probably deserved to be in that prison more than anyone.”

Rio feigned an offended expression, “I don’t know what you’re talking about. At least I didn’t kidnap a child.”

By now, the bartender had turned entirely to them, looking at them with worried eyes.

Rio waved him off, rolling her eyes.

Wanda took a step back, looking at Rio with hurt. “You know very well I didn’t-”

“What?” Rio snapped, “Mean to? Control yourself?”

“I didn’t know. ”

“That you kidnapped someone?”

Wanda groaned, “That’s not what I meant.”

Rio scoffed. “Keep telling yourself that,” She said tauntingly. Winking and turning around, she left Wanda at the bar and returned to the table.

Alice looked up from her phone when Rio’s chair scraped across the floor. “What’d you talk about with Wanda?” She asked carefully.

Rio sighed, noticing the way Agatha turned her attention to her as well. “We caught up,” she shrugged.

Wanda sat down beside her a second later and Rio had to stop herself from moving the chair away from her.

Agatha looked between the two of them, a knowing look on her face. Rio hated it. Why did this woman know everything all the time?

But one glance at Wanda told her everything she needed to know. Wanda did not look happy, at all. Her face shone of annoyance and anger – so it probably wasn’t too difficult to realize that they had in fact not been catching up.

Still, Rio felt completely helpless under Agatha’s gaze and she loathed it. She needed to even the playing field, to make Agatha as helpless and uncomfortable as she felt.

“So what have you two been bonding over, then?” Rio asked instead, turning her attention towards the other side of the table, looking pointedly between the both of them. “Please tell me it was your love for women.”

Alice laughed, “No.”

“Shame,” Rio smirked, looking directly at Agatha who looked away instantly, refusing eye contact.

Instead, Agatha cleared her throat and stood up. “I’m gonna use the bathroom,” She excused herself.

Rio stood up shortly after. “Me too.”

The bathroom was small, much like the rest of the bar, only two stalls and one sink. Because she didn’t really need to go to the toilet, she instead stood by the sink, fixing her eyeliner in the mirror while waiting.

Agatha slinked out of one of the stalls a second later, catching Rio’s eyes through the mirror.

“The other stall is free, you know,” She said exasperatedly, brushing past Rio to wash her hands.

Rio watched her with a light grin on her face.

Something about Agatha in this setting made her more appealing. Not only didn’t she wear the suit, nor that excruciating, analyzing look in her eyes – but she seemed more relaxed. Her chaotic curls now rested against her lower back, and the suits had been changed for everyday wear.

Suddenly, she didn’t feel as intimidating.

Not that she ever did, not to Rio, at least. But she didn’t have that authority that she wore with such pride, anymore.

So when Agatha had dried her hands, and once again tried to brush past Rio without looking her in the eyes, Rio grabbed her and pushed her against the wall.

Agatha looked up at her with an annoyed expression, trying to wiggle herself out. But Rio held her stuck between her arms, her hands now resting against the blue tiled wall.

“What?” Agatha snapped.

Rio leaned in closer, still keeping eye contact – trying to decipher any little reaction she could get out of her. “You didn’t want to talk about it during the session,” Rio mused. “But I’m gonna ask you now.”

Agatha rolled her eyes.

“Please tell me at least, that you’ve fucked a woman before,” Rio said, leaning in to whisper in Agatha’s ear.

Agatha’s breath hitched, which only spurred Rio on.

“I feel bad for you,” Rio whispered. “It’s so much better than with a man.”

“Rio,” Agatha warned, her gaze sharpening. “Let me go.”

“Why?” Rio cooed. “Afraid you’ll like it too much?”

Agatha took a deep breath and placed her hands on Rio’s chest. For a second, Rio almost thought she’d given in, confusion sweeping over her. But then Agatha pushed her back harshly and walked out, door slamming behind her.

Rio leaned back against the wall, stunned. She could feel the heat rise in her cheeks. She expected Agatha to shove her away- hell she’d even wanted it. She’d wanted to push Agatha until that infuriating calm demeanour snapped.

What bothered her however, was the way that she felt, the way that her stomach flipped when Agatha’s hands were on her. And the way Agatha has shoved her - Rio shivered at the assertiveness.

God she felt pathetic.

This must be what being locked up for so many years had done to her. The first sign of human contact and she was freezing like a deer in headlights.

She forced herself to move, smoothing her hair down in the mirror before heading back to their table. She slid into her seat, trying to look as unbothered as possible. Agatha was already there, sipping her water casually.

Rio leaned back in her chair. “What’d I miss?”

Agatha’s eyes flicked to her for a brief second, and in them Rio could see something she couldn’t quite pinpoint.

Rio smirked, propping her chin up with her hand as she watched Agatha carefully.

She had gotten Agatha to snap, to drop that stupid therapist control that made Rio want to strangle her, even if just for a second.

This was a game now, and it wasn’t one Rio was planning to lose.

Chapter 4

Agatha

An overbearing feeling of dread hung over her when she woke up on Monday morning.

She knew she needed to get to the office, to have their next session. It shouldn’t be even remotely difficult, but after Friday, she didn’t know what to feel.

Rio had reached new limits on what Agatha could tolerate. She knew she’d lashed out in the bathroom, but it wasn’t unwarranted. Agatha was sure Rio did all of this on purpose, simply to annoy her – which aggravated her to no end.
