In the office, she prepared two cups of coffee so Rio wouldn’t cause unnecessary hold-ups, and sat down in the armchair. She desperately needed this session over and done with as soon as possible, so she could go back to feeling somewhat normal again.

As she was preparing the notes and writing a few bullet points on what to bring up, Rio walked in.

Agatha didn’t even bother to stand up, her head still over her notebook as Rio threw herself on the couch, “Goodmorning.”

Agatha looked up briefly, nodding accknowledgingly before continuing to write.

“Oh you prepared coffee for me,” Rio smiled, her voice bearing a mocking tone. “How sweet of you.”

Agatha slammed the notebook shut. Rio had a suspicious amount of energy today, and Agatha wasn’t a fan of it. “Shall we get started?”

A smirk tugged on the turner of Rio’s lips, “Sure.”

“So,” Agatha started, “We’re gonna take a different approach.”

Rio scoffed, bringing the cup to her lips and blowing on the warm liquid with a taunting look in her eyes.

Agatha sighed, “How about current relationships?”

“What about them?”

“Who are you close with?”

“Why don’t we talk about your friends,” Rio said, crossing her arms. Before Agatha could answer her, she chirped in again, “An old fellon, and an old client. Really?”

Agatha sighed, placing the notebook to the side. “These sessions aren’t about me. Let’s focus on you.”

“But I’m intrigued,” Rio said, her eyes narrowing. “Why Wanda?”

Agatha internally groaned. Her friendship with Wanda was not professional, and she knew it. Everybody knew it. Her other friends had told her so on multiple occasions that being friends with an old client was a recipie for disaster. But Wanda was kind, underneath everything. She was caring and gentle, and Agatha found herself feeling safe around her. She didn’t have many friends, so kept the ones she did have close.

Rio bringing it up shouldn’t have surprised her, but she’d hoped Rio would have forgotten about it – which she knew, deep down, wouldn’t happen.

She’d already questioned the morality of her friendship with Wanda so many times, and Rio finding out sure wasn’t ideal.

Her phone buzzed in her pocket. She’d made a habit of turning it off before every session, but nothing in terms of Rio seemed to be following the careful rules she had made for herself.

She flicked it onto silent, placing it on the table in front of her.

Rio watched her carefully. “Is that her?”

Agatha rolled her eyes, “It’s really none of your business.”

“Because I’m a client?”

Agatha took a deep breath, “Precicely,”

“But clients getting involved in your personal life shouldn’t be a problem for you?” Rio said, the smug glint in her eyes returning. “You are, after all, best friends with one.”

“A former client,” Agatha corrected, feeling her patience waning.

“Whatever you say,” Rio said, bringing the cup to her lips to hide the smirk that was forming.

Agatha shook her head, grabbing the pen and notes paper to her lap. “How is your relationship with Alice?” She asked carefully, hoping that Rio would move on from Wanda.

“Why?” Rio asked smugly, leaning in and searching Agatha’s face. “Are you jealous or something?”

“Guess I have to start again,” Agatha sighed yet again, looking up from her notes and making eyecontact, trying to keep a stern look on her face. “I’ve only met Alice, but from what I gathered she’s very protective over you. She knows you, Rio.”

Rio shrugged, “Well, we’ve been friends for a while.”

Agatha leaned back, studying Rio’s facial expressions “Why’d she give me her phone number?”

“She did what?” Rio asked, momentarily stunned.

Agatha backtracked, knowing the way Rio would be able to spin this. “She said it’s for ‘emergencies’. What did she mean by that?”

“How the fuck should I know?,” Rio held her hands up in defeat. “Ask her, maybe.”

Agatha scoffed but tried her best to remain composed. “How long have you guys been friends?”

“Are we counting the years I was in prison or should I remove them from the equation?”

Agatha had to fight the urge to roll her eyes – did Rio ever give up? Instead, she put on a fake smile, “You decide.”

Rio leaned back, her back hitting the couch cushions, “Fourteen years then, give or take.”

“How did you two meet?”

Agatha could see the way Rio retracted for a second, before that familiar glint reappeared in her eyes, “Through Lilia.”

Agatha waited for Rio to continue, until it became clear that she had no intention of carrying on. Agatha sighed. “You're going to have to give me more than that.”

“Fine. We met when I was 15, a couple weeks after Lilia took me in.”

“Was this at school, or through a social event Lilia took you too?”

Rio just shrugged. “I dont know.”

Agatha fixed her with a look. “You can do better than that, Rio.”

Rio glared at her before sighing dramatically. “Ugh fine, we met at an underground secret meeting of an organisation of contract killers. Is that better?”

Agatha rolled her eyes and put her pen down, “This is never going to work if you keep lying to me.”

“I’m not lying.” Rio protested.

“Let’s move on onto another point,” Agatha said sharply.

“Oh yes, of course,” Rio said, a bit too overenthusiastically and dipped down to reach for something in her bag. “The dream journal.”

The next point on Agatha’s list was not the dream journal, but she allowed it in the hopes that Rio maybe for once put effort into something. If that was the case, she didn’t want to interrupt.

“Thank you for introducing it to me,” Rio said, opening it. “Honestly, I feel like I know myself on an entirely different level now.”

Agatha scoffed. Either this was too good to be true or Rio was pulling another trick on her – she hoped for the former, but knew it was probably the latter. Nevertheless, Agatha relaxed back into the armchair, preparing her pen and notepaper. “Go ahead.”

Rio opened the journal, scrambling through the pages.

“Ah,” She exclaimed, presumably having found the page she was searching for. “I dreamt this Saturday night.”

Agatha could feel her pulse racing again - that was the night after they’d met at the bar. It probably wouldn’t have anything to do with her, but she also didn’t want to find out.

But being the professional she was, she remained quiet, watching as Rio took a deep breath and began to read aloud.

“Okay, so. I was in this bar and it was super dark. It was the kind of place where the music is so obnoxious you can’t hear yourself think. Anyway then this super hot woman came up to me, older, dark hair – totally my type.”

Agatha narrowed her eyes. She wasn’t sure if Rio was messing with her. She was probably overthinking things. This could be any older, dark haired woman, right?

“She had this vibe, like she could wreck my life and I would thank her for it. She kissed me hard, and It was messy, wet. Her lips were soft but her teeth - God the way she bit my bottom lip? I felt it everywhere.”

Agatha sighed heavily. “Rio.”

Rio stopped midsentance, her eyebrows furrowing in such a way that made Agatha think that maybe, for once, Rio was being genuine.

“I thought this was supposed to be a safe space?” She said, her voice tinged with dissapointment.

Agatha considered her options. There was a strong chance that Rio had written this just to get on her nerves, and Agatha wasn’t about to let her win this round of whatever twisted game seemed to be going on between them. On the other hand, there was the small, almost impossibly unlikely but still there, chance that Rio had genuinely engaged with a task. She didn’t want to risk losing potential progress.

She closed her eyes and exhaled through her nose. In all her fifteen years of working as a psychiatrist, she had never found a patient more infuriating than Rio.

“Fine, carry on.”

Rio grinned and turned her attention back to the notebook in front of her. “Right so i'm wearing a skirt in this dream right, one of those super short mini ones. The kind that barely covers anything, you know what I mean?”

Agatha pinched the bridge of her nose. “Rio, I’m not sure that’s relev-“

“Hmm, maybe that was a sign that I need to go on a shopping trip. God I missed skirts in prison, do you know how hard it was get fucked quickly in those ugly-ass jumpsuits?” Rio said absentmindedly, ignoring Agatha’s comment completely.

Agatha forced expression to remain as neutral as possible, both out of professionality and not wanting to give Rio any reaction. It was harder than she anticipated. The more Rio talked, the more she began to feel warmth creep up into her cheeks, and she forced her hands to stay still in her lap.

“Anyways, her hands were all over me like she owned me or something. She stuck her hand up my skirt and for some reason I wasn’t wearing any underwear - I guess dream me went out with an agenda.”

Agatha shifted slightly in her chair, crossing one leg over the other. She told herself that the heat in her chest was purely her frustration.

“She kept teasing me at first, but then she slipped her fingers inside of me and Jesus it was good. I don’t know if you’ve ever been fingered, by a woman I mean, but you should absolutely try it sometime.” Rio tilted her head, her voice honeyed. “I’d be happy to give you a demonstration.”

Agatha’s throat felt dry. She took a deep breath, forcing herself to stay calm, her gaze fixed sharply on Rio. She was absolutely not going to be the one to back down.

“And then right before I came, I woke up.” Rio shrugged, dark eyes looking up at Agatha expectantly,

It took Agatha a moment to snap back into focus, and she forced herself to sit up straighter clearing her throat.

“What do you think it means?” Rio asked, a proud grin on her face. “I like older women? I like edging?”

Agatha has to stop herself from scoffing. She exhaled, her professional mask slipping back on. “I was referring to the symbolic meaning, Rio. Dreams present us with opportunities to get to know ourselves better. For example, maybe what you want in connection. After all that time in prison, in sure you feel isolated-“

She trailed off when she saw the faint smirk at the corner of Rio’s lips.

If Rio wasn't going to take this seriously, then why did she even bother?

“Not a fan?” Rio asked, crossing her arms. “Or did it fluster you?”

Agatha shook her head, “I’d be happy to hear all about your dreams in a future session, but please take this seriously next time.”

“What? You don’t think I’m serious?” Rio asked, feigning a hurt expression.

Agatha pointedly ignored her, changing subjects.

“We have ten minutes left,” Agatha announced. “So we have to go through your homework for next week.”

“Homework, really?“ Rio groaned. “What do you mean next week?”

“You have a meeting with your probation officer this Thursday, so we won't meet until next Monday,” Agatha explained. “Your homework is preparing for the next session, when we’re gonna work with mindfulness. I want you to find something you do to relax. For example, taking a walk, breathing exercises or meditating.”

Rio sighed, “Is that what you do?”

“What I do is irrelevant. You need to figure out what helps you relax,” Agatha said sharply.

Rio leaned back, looking at Agatha playfully, “I think I can do that.”

“Great,” Agatha closed her notebook and stood up. “Then I’ll see you Monday.”

She doesn’t even bother to see Rio out the door. Maybe it’s rude of her, but at this point she honestly couldn’t care less. If Rio was insistent on messing her around, then why should she bother being polite? Still, maybe some progress has been made today. On the off chance that Rio’s dream was genuine – and honestly Agatha wouldn’t put it past her – then maybe, just maybe, Rio was beginning to open up to her.

Agatha walked over to the desk. She had rather an elaborate filing system, and she ran her fingers down the alphabetically ordered folders until she found V , pulling out Rio’s folder and adding her notes from this session.

She could hear Rio shuffling around behind her, no doubt trying to linger as much as possible to annoy her.

She rolled her eyes. “Please don’t let me keep you,” she said shortly.

Rio scoffs. “Oh you’re not, don’t worry.”

Agatha lips pressed into a thin line, but she didn’t rise to the bait. She couldn’t even be bothered to figure out what the hell that was supposed to mean. With Rio, it could have been anything.

It wasnt until she heard the door click shut that she turned around, letting out a soft sigh. The gentle tick-tock of the clock on the mantle filled the room. and Agatha closed her eyes briefly, allowing herself a moment to enjoy the peace.

She was pretty sure she had a headache coming on.

She bent down to pick up Rio’s discarded coffee mug, and it was then that she saw the journal, left on her chair. For a brief second, Agatha considered going after her. But no, Rio’s session was over, and she had no desire to prolong their interaction.

Still, she picked up the journal, intending to slip it into Rio’s folder for safekeeping. Her fingers hesitated as she held it, the temptation gnawing at her. She knew she shouldn’t. It would be unprofessional. But then again, Rio had been less than cooperative, hadn’t she?

Agatha’s lips pressed into a thin line. If Rio wasn’t going to play by the rules, then maybe she didn’t have to either.

She opened the cover, her curiosity winning out, and began flipping through the pages. Perhaps there were more entries, ones Rio hadn’t shared aloud. If nothing else, it would be evidence that Rio was at least engaging with something she’d been asked to do.

But as Agatha flicked through the journal, all she found was… nothing.

Her brows knitted together. Page after page, the journal was completely blank.

Agatha paused, turning back to the beginning, convinced she must have missed something. She carefully flipped through again, slower this time.

Nothing.

Her frown deepened, confusion prickling at her. She hadn’t expected much from Rio, but this? Even for her, this was a surprise.

And then she saw it.

The folded corner of a single page stood out, the one Rio had presumably read from. Agatha turned to it, her breath catching as she spotted two hastily scrawled words in the middle of the otherwise empty sheet:

Got you :)

Rio

Friday morning, Rio wakes up with a mission.

And so, she walked.

Agatha walked with hurried steps through the busy Tuesday afternoon streets.

She was late for the AA meeting for the first time in forever. It wasn’t entirely her fault, the client she’d had in the afternoon had demanded her time, and her being the professional that she is had allowed her to.

Something she deeply regretted when she walked into the silent AA meeting room, everyone already seated and ready to begin.

Embarrassed, she walked across the room, her heels clicking against the wooden floor was enough to turn everyone’s attention to her.

Tony looked up at her with a gentle, greeting smile as she sat down. Agatha tried to reciprocate it but it never reached her eyes.

Soon enough, once she’d settled in her seat, Steve started the meeting.

Everytime Agatha was here, she regretted it. But every week, she’d go anyway. It had become a habit, something stable in her life. Her clients came and went, as did her friends, but the AA stayed there – helped to keep her grounded.

This week though, she regretted it even more. Carol sat on the other end of the make-shift circle of chairs, listening half-heartedly to what Steve was saying.

Agatha averted her eyes and focused on Yelena, who was chewing her gum and blowing bubbles obnoxiously, instead.

The date with her and Carol hadn’t been horrible, per-se. But after half the evening had gone by, they’d both mutually realised that they might be better off as friends. They shared the same humor, dealt with the same kind of problems and they could keep a conversation going – but somewhere within Agatha something told her that it was not right .

Carol wasn’t inherently unattractive, blond hair falling flawlessly over her shoulders, dark brown eyes that served as a great contrast to her, otherwise, light features. But Agatha didn’t feel a pull to her, and that wasn’t Carol’s fault.

They had hugged goodbye after the date, having shared a lot of intimate feelings and stories, and left.

They hadn’t spoken since.

Maybe it was for the better, she had enough shit to deal with in her life already. Rio still hadn’t left her mind since yesterday. She wasn’t happy about it, obviously, and still tried her best to shove the thoughts away.

If Carol took up space in her brain as well, it would probably explode.

The meeting went by quicker than Agatha had anticipated, a few people sharing stories, a group discussion about peer-pressure and then they were out.

Well, almost.

As she hurriedly stood up to walk out, wanting to get home more than anything, Tony stopped her with a hand on her shoulder, turning her around.

Agatha rolled her eyes, “What?”

“Are you okay?” Tony asked carefully, the look in his eyes soft. The question wasn’t welcome, but that didn’t mean it wasn’t expected. “About tomorrow, I mean.”

Agatha felt her entire body freeze. She’d been ignoring the thought of tomorrow like the plague. She couldn’t face it, she couldn’t .

“It’s been ten years, Agatha, allow yourself to grieve.”

Agatha’s face turned stone cold, “Don’t tell me how to mourn.”

Tony put his hands up in defeat. “I’m not. I’m simply saying that you can call me tomorrow, if you need someone.”

Agatha turns around promptly. She was thankful, truly, for Tony’s support, but she didn’t need it now. It wasn’t even tomorrow yet and ‘each day has enough trouble of its own’ – or whatever the bible says, she barely paid attention to Steve’s ramblings .

Facing it now would make her succumb to the sadness, and she couldn’t do it. Not yet, and definitely not mere seconds after an AA meeting had adjourned.

Tony caught up with her right by the door, “At least consider it. If you want to spend the day alone, I get that. But the 10 year anniversary is a hard day to get through, I don’t want you to do it alone.”

“My dad,” Tony sighed, “And my mom.”

Agatha’s face turns softer, suddenly feeling bad. “I didn’t know that.”

Tony shrugged, “It never came up.”

Agatha couldn’t stop herself from prying, “When?”

“1991. Car accident,” He said, trying to sound indifferent but Agatha could hear the hint of something bigger buried beneath his words. “So it was very sudden, just like-”

“Do not finish that sentence,” Agatha took a step back.

“I didn’t mean to bring it up,” Tony said, tone suddenly more defensive.

“I’ll see you next week, Tony,” She said dismissevly, turning around and walking outside.

“Agatha.”

Agatha, rolled her eyes again. Could she ever catch a break? First she’s reminded of her entirely too annoying client all day by her own brain, then her friend pushes her about her son's death, and now her date from the weekend shows up.

Carol walked up to her, her breath turning into small clouds in the cold air. “Can we talk?”

“Whatever for?” Agatha asked, trying not to let her irritation get the best of her.

Carol shrugged, “I just wanted to make sure everything is good between us, still.”

Agatha looked over Carol’s shoulder, not daring to meet her eyes. “Why wouldn’t it be?”

Carol looked her dead in the eyes, “You can’t be serious?”

Tony’s words rang in her mind, “the 10 year anniversary is a hard day to get through,” and yeah, it probably would be. She wanted to curse him out for making her even think about it.

She had a lot of apologies to say next week – Carol and Tony didn’t deserve the way she treated them – and she knew that, deep down.

She wouldn’t, though.

It had just started raining when she started to walk home, the icy drops falling onto her skin kept her close to reality as she almost flinched everytime a drop landed on her. She entered her apartment, throwing herself on the bed with a heavy anxious feeling in her chest.

She dreaded the fact that she would wake up tomorrow.

Rio

The graveyard was quiet, and almost completely empty. That was the way that Rio liked it. The place hasn’t changed in the 7 years she’d been away, and in an odd way that comforted her. So much of the world has shifted, but not this place. This place was exactly the same as the day she had first visited, a four year old girl in a little black dress, watching as her parents' bodies were buried below the earth. A lot has happened since then.

She walked along the gravel path, boots crunching as she let her memory guide her. Soon enough, she came to a plot near the far end.

As a kid, Rio had hated visiting the cemetery. The place reeked of death, and it made her stomach twist to know that her parents were a part of that. That had changed as she’d fallen further and further into the foster system. After spending so much of her time bouncing between group homes until she inevitably got herself kicked out, sitting at the foot of her parents headstones has become one of the only places where she felt like she could breathe . Before prison she’d always tried to visit as much as she could.

She sat down in front of their headstones, reaching her hand out to trace the familiar letters inscribed upon it. One of the first things she’d done when she was locked up was instruct Alice to make sure their grave stayed tidy, and it seemed like she’d done a decent enough job.

She crossed her legs beneath her, and placed the flowers she had brought from a store on the way there against the stone. She closed her eyes, and for a moment she didn’t feel like the mess of a person she was now. She felt like that little girl again, waiting for her parents that were never coming home.

“Long time no see, huh,” she said, voice sounding too loud in the still air. She winced at the harshness of it, but kept talking anyway, “I brought you some flowers, and no I didn’t steal them, I promise. I know you would never approve of that.”

She shifted, her fingers playing with a blade of grass. “I’m sorry I was away for so long. Lots of stuff has happened in the seven years since I last saw you. Well, actually that’s sort of a lie.” She snorted at herself. “I went to prison, and that was kind of it. It was boring as hell really.”

She pulled her knees up to her chest, resting her chin on top and wrapping her arms around her legs. “I promise I didn’t forget about you though. I talked to you from time to time while I was locked up, mostly at night. It got pretty lonely.”

“Anyways, that’s enough about my shit. I’m out now, and I’m trying to get back to normal. Alice has been helping me get back on my feet.”

She smiled softly. “I hope you guys have been okay. And if you can hear me, wherever you are, then I want you to know that I’m sorry. I’m sorry for the person that I’ve become. I know it’s not what you would have wanted for me.”

She stood slowly, brushing the dirt off her pants. She stuffed her hands in her jacket pockets, looking down at her parents grave a moment longer. “I'll be back, promise.”

She turned and trudged back along the path. Her eyes skimmed over the rows of graves as she passed, some cracked and overgrown, others newer and bright.

She could see the gate now, but before she could reach it something else grabbed her attention. A figure was standing by one of the graves, shoulders slightly hunched and dark hair catching in the midday light.

For a second, Rio was reminded of Agatha. She scoffed at herself. That woman was taking up far too much of her headspace.

She shook the thought off and kept walking. She needs to get back to her flat - she was supposed to see Lilia that afternoon and she didn’t like to be kept waiting.

The figure leaned down, brushing what was probably dirt off the headstone.

Wait a second.

Rio’s steps slowed. She recognized that face.

“You have got to be kidding me”, she muttered, sighing heavily.

Agatha .

What the fuck was she supposed to do now? Yes, Rio loved winding her up, but this was a graveyard, and even she had her limits.

So, she just stood there, staring like an idiot, and hoping that the universe didn’t hate her enough to make Agatha turn around and see her.

Agatha straightened, wiping her eyes before smoothing out her coat and walking out of the entrance.

Rio let out the breath she didn’t realise she had been holding. She really should leave, forget that Agatha has ever been here and get on with her life. Everyone has their secrets, right? God knows Rio had more than the average person.

And yet, she found herself veering off path, instead heading towards the grave Agatha had been standing at. When she reached it she hesitated, looking round to make sure that the other woman was truly gone. When she was sure that she was alone, she turned back around, reading the inscription.

Nicholas Scratch

May 13th 1998 - October 30th 2014

Beloved son, Wonderful friend

‘But what is grief, if not love preserving?’

Huh. Of all the things Rio had expected, the grave of a child was not high on her list.

The headstone was immaculate, no moss or dirt clinging to its surface. Clearly it was all looked after.

She read the name again.

Nicholas Scratch.

Agatha wavered on the offer of Tony’s contact for a while.

He’d always loved music, and sometimes, during late nights, it would drive Agatha up the walls. Thinking about it now, she didn’t understand why. If she could just hear it one more time…

She stood up, her entire body numb as she walked into her bedroom and threw herself on the bed. Dehydrated and exhausted, she laid there, with her face against the soft pillows and felt the tears running dry.

Her breath heaved as the sobs continued without any tears to gratify them.

“My son,” her voice cracked. “Nicholas is… was… my son.”

She forced herself not to cry. She wouldn’t do this here, not in front of Rio. She forced herself to take a deep breath, even though it felt like her chest might collapse under the weight on their conversation.

The smirk on Rio’s face vanished. Her eyes widened, and for the first time since they had met, the mask behind which Rio seemed to hide herself slipped. “Oh. I’m sorry. I didn’t know.”

Agatha’s hands trembled slightly, and she forced them to still. She didn’t need anyone’s pity, least of all from the woman sitting across from her. “Let’s move-“

“Is that why you started drinking?” Rio cut in.

The abruptness of the question took Agatha by surprise, and she had to take a moment to actually understand what Rio had asked.

“What?!”

“I’m assuming that it’s after Nicholas died that you started having problems with alcohol.” Rio stated matter of factly.

“Why do you know thi-“

“That night, when me and Alice ran into you and Wanda at the bar. You were drinking water. I thought it was some sort of posh, pretentious ‘I’m too good for cheap beer’ thing, but now it makes sense.”

Agatha’s mouth opened, but no words came out. The fucking audacity or Rio to start analysing her . She felt exposed, some of her closely guarded secrets suddenly being pulled into the spotlight.

“Rio, I’m not comfortable discussing this with you.”

“It’s nothing to be ashamed of.” Her voice was almost gentle now, the shift so jarring that Agatha could only stare at her.

“What do you mean?”

Rio’s gaze dropped to the floor, her brow furrowed. After a moment, she turned back to Agatha. “I’m sure you coped the best you could at the time. And, like, look at you. You're here, with this smart-ass job and everything, so clearly you didn’t go completely off the deep end. However you needed to cope, that’s okay.”

Agatha didn’t know what it was about the words, but they made a part of her she had long since buried ache. It made her feel raw in a way that physically hurt. She swallowed, her throat feeling like sandpaper. “That's… a surpassingly compassionate take for you to have.”

Rio shrugged again, lounging further back into the chair. “I was an addict. Well, I guess I still am, but I haven’t used in 7 years. Maybe that’s the one thing prison was actually useful for. It forced me to get clean.”

Agatha reached for the notebook, desperate to shift the conversation back to neutral and safer ground.

“No.” Rio stated, simply.

Agatha looked up, and was met with Rio’s gaze focused on her sharply.

Agatha arched an eyebrow. “Excuse me?”

Rio crossed her arms. “I said, no. You shouldn’t be doing this. Not right now. You’re clearly a mess, I mean you look dreadful.”

Charming.

“And besides, it would be no fun to mess with you when you’re practically on the edge of some kind of nervous breakdown. It’s only fun when you fight back.”

Agatha just stared at her, incredulous. “Seriously?”

“Even I have my limits,” Rio said with a shallow laugh. “Go home, Agatha.”

Before Agatha had time to respond, Rio was out the door. Agatha didn’t go after her.

The silence that followed felt different. Usually after sessions with Rio, Agatha had to resist the urge to put her fist through the wall. But this time, the silence wasn’t one of rage or anger. It was just… quiet.

Leaning back in her chair, she pressed her fingers to her temples, trying to process what the hell had just happened.

She came to two realisations.

The first was that Rio was dangerous. Not just in the obvious, reckless, ‘I killed a guy’ way Agatha has always assumed her to be, but in a subtler, more insidious way. When most people brought up Nicky to her she was easily able to ignore them, their comments deflected by years of practiced detachment. But Rio’s words… they stuck. They peeled back layers that she had carefully built.

The second: Rio has a conscience. She could push and push Agatha’s patience all she wanted but at a certain point, even she had her limits. Agatha had seen in her eyes a vulnerability, one that eerily mirrored her own.

She exhaled slowly. She wasn’t sure which of the two realisations was worse.

On the bright side, Rio had opened up to her more today than ever before. Sure, it was small, but it was a start. And with someone like Rio, a ‘start’ was all that Agatha needed.

Rio

“I thought we would take a different approach today,” Agatha suggested as Rio sat down on the couch Monday morning.

It threw Rio off, but only slightly.

Rio rolled her eyes, crossing her arms over her chest. “If it’s another mindfulness exercise then I might stab you with your own fucking pen.”

“No,” Agatha said, shifting in her seat. “I’ve taken what you said into consideration, and I think we should do something more of a back-and-forth. I’m gonna ask you a question, and in return, you can ask me one.”

Rio leaned forward slightly, eyes narrowing. “These questions. Can they be… anything?”

She wanted to tread lightly with Agatha for now, but this opportunity was almost too good to pass on.

Agatha sighed, “Yes. I’m gonna start, and just like with the other sessions, if you don’t want to answer a question, just say so. As long as I can abide by the same rules.” She paused as her expression became more serious. “Don’t lie for fun, and I won’t either.”

Rio put her hand on her chest, feigning offence. “I would never lie for fun. But okay, fine.”

Agatha glared at her, clasping her hands together over the notebook. “You wanted a fair exchange, did you not?”

“Mhm. Okay whatever, we’ll do it your way.” Rio said, crossing one leg over the other and leaning back into the couch.

“Easy, green.” Rio leaned forward, a smirk on her face. Her mind went to the date Agatha and Carol had – she so desperately wanted to see how it went. “Are you seeing anyone?”

“That’s inappropriate.”

A smug smile reached Rio’s lips. She tried not to get overly joyful over Agatha’s failed romantic endeavours, but deep down she was thrilled over it.

Rio wasn’t sure what game Agatha was playing with her, but she sure as heck wasn’t going to lose. “Extremely.”

Jesus, was she not even allowed to find someone attractive these days without it being some kind of bullshit trauma response?

“Are you asking me if I have fucking mommy issues?”

“I don’t know,” Agatha challenged. “Am I? Do you?”

“Five or six months I think.” Rio shrugged. “Hard to remember, I moved around a lot. Were you a single parent?”

Rio chewed lightly on her bottom lip as she thought. “Fine, I guess. I got used to it pretty quickly. I learnt how to take care of myself. Why did you become a psychologist?”

She could see Agatha write down something in her notes. She didn’t look up from them as she answered; “My mom forced me to go to one as a child and I hated it. She told me I wasn’t good for anything, so out of spite I swore to become even better at her job than she was.”

Rio could respect that. Most of the things she did in life were out of spite.

Agatha continued after a brief pause, “Have you ever reflected on how that could have affected you? Having to take care of yourself at such a young age, I mean.”

“Nothing like a change of scenery to keep things fresh. New house, new rules, new people pretending to care. It was like speed dating, but for trauma. Why didn’t you go to rehab? I thought you were all about accepting help from others and all that bullshit.”

“Because I didn’t want to get better,” Agatha stated with a sigh like it was obvious. “Why do you think people only pretended to care?”

“What do you want me to say, Agatha? That sometimes the adults were shitty and that sometimes the kids were even worse? That every time my problems took over they just sent me away, to live with a new family? That no one could deal with my real problems?”

Agatha writes down something in her notes and it pisses Rio off . Could she not just be present for one fucking minute? Did she have to go all ‘therapist-y’ the second she said something of value?

Rio scoffed. “Do you have to write everything down in that stupid fucking book?”

Agatha’s voice was infuriatingly calm as she continued, “Your turn to ask a question.”

Rio took a deep breath, trying to steady herself. She had no reason to be this furious about Agatha just doing her job. She was mostly angry with herself for allowing herself to answer all these questions truthfully. Like the second Agatha became vulnerable she needed to as well.

But it felt so right. It felt so easy to just, for once, allow herself to open up. And it was easier when Agatha did it as well – it made her feel less alone. Less stripped naked and torn apart.

“Why didn’t you want to get better? With the alcohol, I mean.”

Agatha’s expression became more serious, her guard falling slightly. “Because I didn’t think I deserved to be happy. If Nicky couldn’t, then why should I?”

Suddenly, Rio could no longer remember why she had been so furious mere seconds ago. She leaned forward, looking Agatha deeply in the eyes with a sincere look. “That’s stupid, you know?”

“That’s what I came to realize.” Agatha paused, studying Rio with her eyes as if she was searching for another question. “What do you remember about your parents?”

Rio shrugged and leaned back into the couch again. “Not much, to be honest. They worked a lot, but I think they tried their best.”

“Do you know what they worked with, that caused them to be so absent?”

“I don’t remember, honestly. I think my mom was a lawyer?” Rio chuckled slightly. “Ironic, isn’t it?”

Rio could see a smile tugging at the corner of Agatha’s lips.

“You said that your mom forced you into therapy as a kid. Why?”

Agatha furrowed her eyebrows, “I’m gonna pass that question.”

Rio frowned. “Okay… let me ask another-”

“No. My turn.” Agatha interrupted promptly. Then her expression turned more soft, her eyes more gentle. “Do you remember anything about your parents' death?”

Rio could feel her blood run cold. “Pass.”

She could see Agatha write something down. Presumably exactly what she’d just said. Rio wished she had a notebook as well that she could intimidate Agatha with.

Agatha

She hadn’t expected this session to go well. The idea had sparked in her head last week, when she remembered what Rio had said Monday, and then how Rio had shared more than ever during the last session – as soon as Agatha herself opened up.

She knew there were probably more orthodox ways to go about this, instead of laying herself bare in front of her client. But it felt right in the moment, and for whatever reason, it still felt right.

She leaned back in her armchair, pen still on the note paper as she waited for Rio to ask the next question.

“How old were you when you got him?”

Rio didn’t have to say the name for Agatha to understand the question.

“20.”

“How’d university and a baby go? Maybe that's why you're so shit at your job.”

“It was hard to pay him the attention he deserved, he deserved to have a present mother and I couldn’t give him that. Even if he definitely complicated my education.”

“I’m sure you did your best.”

“I tried to. He was such a good kid, even without the parenting he needed. He always had good grades, people loved him, adults loved him. And he cared for other people, trying to help everyone, all the time. A girl in his class once came to school crying, and he supported her the entire day, sat with her in class and during lunch – he was only nine at the time, and surely would like to spend time with his friends instead. But he didn’t, he always put other people first.”

“He sounds like a sweet kid.”

“He was.”

Rio’s expression turns impossibly softer, “How did he die?”

Agatha felt her stomach turn at the question. Really, it is a fair question to ask, even if it's a bit intrusive, she gets why Rio would be curious. “Pass”.

Almost no one knew the cause of Nicky’s untimely death. Agatha couldn’t face them knowing. Partially because she was ashamed. If only she hadn’t let him go to that party…

If he ever went, that is. The entire night was a blur, and after a few weeks Agatha started questioning her own memory. Maybe he said something else?

Because when the police eventually interrogated Jonas and Chris, who Agatha had confirmed Nicky would be with, they’d said that Nicky hadn’t been with them that night.

It confused her further. She remembered so vividly how that entire night went, like it was just yesterday. It didn’t make any sense.

Did Nicky lie to her?

Why would he?

Crippled in her own self doubt, she started to make up scenarios to explain Nicky’s departure that night. Maybe he needed to get out of the house, maybe he couldn’t stand being with her, and needed a valid reason to be on his own.

Maybe he found her overbearing, too present. Eventually, she started blaming herself, once again, for that night. Not anymore because of the fact that she let him out of the house, but she started questioning her entire life as a parent.

Thoughts of self-doubt clouded her judgement, and she forgot about how good of a mother she really was to him, despite the circumstances.

Why else would Nicky lie?

Over the last ten years Agatha still didn’t know the answer, and it haunted her.

But the truth, whatever it was, hurt too much. After time, she settled on thoughts of ‘ maybe she heard the names wrong.” Whatever that could mean hurt less than ‘he didn’t want to be with you, he needed out of that house’.

After investigations from the police, they’d eventually labeled it as a freak accident – which had caused yet another spiral. They never caught the killer, or the accidental shooter, if it was an accident.

Agatha had blamed it on herself as well for allowing Nicky to go out, in the middle of the night on a Wednesday, dressed as a robber. Maybe someone had confused him for someone else? It didn’t make any sense.

Nicky – her sweet boy, who had the highest grades and sat in his room until late in the night, playing the guitar – shot? No.

The truth hurt, and every time she thought of it, the knife twisted even deeper. He wasn’t supposed to be dead, it was an accident. She wasn’t sure whether or not to be angry or sad, because how does one face that?

Agatha could feel her own thoughts spiral. Switching them off, like she’d perfected over the years, she went back to therapist mode.

“You described yourself as an addict, earlier. How did that start?”

“Is it not my turn to ask a question?”

“No. Answer mine.”

Rio shrugged, “Probably when I was about 13, 14 maybe. It was mainly weed back then, it was always easy to source, you know? But when I was 17 this girl got me in contact with the guy who dealt ket, and Jesus that shit was good. Kid became my regular for a while. After that it was heroin.”

Agatha shifted in her seat, and started writing down what Rio had said. “Why do you think you turned to drugs?”

“Real life was shitty enough. Sure, once I moved in with Lilia it was better, but at that point I was already fucked up.”

“You talk about Lilia like some turning point. What happened?”

“Hey, it’s my turn to ask a question.”

Agatha rolled her eyes. She knew she would regret the decision of a back-and-forth, but so far, this session is the most successful one yet. She felt vulnerable, opening up to Rio this way. But being the professional that she is, she allowed it – whatever would make the sessions more progressive.

Agatha gestured with her hands, waving Rio on.

“You asked me, so now I’m gonna ask you. Do you find me attractive, Agatha?”

For a second, Agatha forgot to breathe. She knew she wasn’t prepared for another hard- hitting question about Nicky, but at least that was predictable. This question should be as well, and Agatha cursed herself out for not seeing it coming.

Deep down though, she was almost grateful for the lighter turn of the conversation.

She couldn’t look Rio in the eyes, instead focusing on a spot on the wall behind her shoulder. “That is highly unprofessional to answer.”

“What happened during the night you’re referring to?”

“I shoplifted a bottle of vodka and chugged it in my room. Lilia found me when I was puking my guts out. Which-” Rio lets out an empty laugh, “-well, let’s just say her not giving up meant a lot.”

Agatha, once again, turned to her notes. She honestly hadn’t expected Rio to be this compliant. But if it worked, it worked.

“Has Wanda ever mentioned me?”

Agatha could almost hear the jealousy seeping through the words, it was genuinely amusing. “No. Should she?”

Rio shrugged. “We shared rooms back in prison. I was just wondering if she ever talked about her very charming and affectionate roommate.”

Agatha smirked, “If she did, she probably wasn’t talking about you.”

Rio scoffed.

Agatha glanced at the clock on her wrist. Realising there were only a few minutes left, she went back to resume their back-and-forth. “How did five-year-old you cope?”

“She got good at disappearing. Made sure she didn’t take up too much space. Didn’t make too much noise.”

“I think I was about seven when I realised that the world is a fucked up place and it doesn’t owe you anything. I might as well make as much noise as I could to let them know I wasn’t going to conform to their stupid little regime.”

Agatha wasn’t amused. She wasn’t. But she couldn’t stop the smirk that forced its way to her lips. She hid it by standing up and turning around to walk to her desk. She went through her folders until she eventually found Rio’s and pulled out the journal.

That’s a change.

What she expected to be Rio with a smug smile on her face, self-satisfied and confident – turned out to be the complete opposite?

Is Rio being for real?

She knew that it wasn't an unusual procedure. Sometimes, when clients had shared something deeply upsetting or traumatic, Agatha would offer comfort. But this, this , was new – especially with Rio.

Professional.

Fucking hell .

She cussed herself out silently for falling into Rio’s trap – everyone could have seen this coming. But Agatha, apparently, was too oblivious.

“It’s alright if you do,” Rio continued, her breath still hot against her ear. Agatha wished she had a knife to stab Rio with then and there, because what the fuck? “Next time, just think about me doing the same. Because I am.”

Agatha is left completely stunned. How the hell does someone move on from that?

It felt almost too pointed, too personal.

She looked at the door – which Rio had slammed shut mere seconds ago – and tried to regain her composure.

She felt a little victorious over the session though, despite everything. Even if it demanded a lot from her, she’d still gotten far with Rio. Further than she ever thought imaginable just a week ago, at least. She had to pat herself on the shoulder for that one.

“Nothing, nothing just–”

“She’s been a pain in my ass during our sessions. Like trying to rile me up or something.”

And she lied, of course, because that’s all she can seem to do, but she made it basically be about me .” Agatha rambled, taking a deep breath before continuing. “And then , last week, she fucking touched herself in my office. I mean I told her to do it, because she said it helps her relax or whatever, but… I don’t know and I hate myself because I think I somehow like it? And it’s so twisted and I know that and I don’t know what to do.”

She paused, feeling her own breathing becoming heavier as she tried to catch her breath.

Agatha groaned. “Touch herself.”

Wanda laughed. A genuine laugh that rippled through the phone and probably disturbed everyone in Wanda’s vicinity. “And what, you… like her?”

“No,” Agatha’s answer came too quickly. “But even I can’t fucking lie to myself anymore. She’s interesting, to say the least.”

“Sounds like you like her,” Wanda sing-songs.

Wanda sighed. “Do what you feel is right.” She explained like it was obvious. “If you want to drop her as a client, drop her. If you like her you should pursue it, although maybe consider therapy yourself because Rio is one of the most fucked up people I’ve ever met and you being into that is… interesting. And if you don’t, well… then be clear about it to her.”

“I’m not even sure if she likes me or if she’s only doing it to rile me up,” Agatha muttered.

“I’m afraid I have to go now. But good luck– Rio is not easy to deal with.”

Wanda hung up before Agatha could ask her to elaborate. Once again leaving her completely alone in the silent office.

She was totally, and utterly, fucked.

Rio simply glared at him, holding her ground. “Take another step, and I'll make sure you limp home,” she hissed. The man backed off with a sneer. Men like him were predictable, all bark and no bite. She’d grown up knowing exactly how to handle men like that.

She climbed up the cracked station steps and emerged into the heart of the neighborhood. The streets were narrow, graffiti crawling up every available surface like ivy. Apartment blocks with peeling paint and broken windows towered above her.

It wasn’t pretty, but Rio knew it relatively well. She’d been out this way a few times on work related tasks.

She wound her way through the maze of side streets and back alleys, her boots scuffing against the uneven pavement. She eventually found the building she was looking for – an apartment building that looked like it should have fallen down years ago, with rust streaked walls and a front door that hung slightly off its hinges.

She figured that she might as well try to be polite, so she knocked. As she expected, no one answered.

So much for manners.

She pulled out her switchblade from her pocket and used it to pick the lock.

The inside was no better than the exterior. The wallpaper was peeled in jagged strips, the air heavy with the stench of damp. Rio moved through the cluttered downstairs, stepping over empty bottles and cigarette butts as she made her way towards the stairs.

She took them two at a time, the faint murmur of voices along with the acrid smell of weed getting stronger.

Reaching the top, she turned into a small room. Inside were three men, well, they looked more like teenagers. One was slouched in an armchair, a joint hanging loosely between his lips, while the other two were sprawled out on a moth bitten sofa. They fell silent as she entered, looking at her with narrowed eyes.

“Who the hell are you?”

Rio ignored the question. “I’m looking for Matt Murdock.”

The guy who spoke – he looked around 19 – snorted, leaning back and tagging another drag of his cigarette. “Well, you're not gonna find him here. So why don’t you fuck off?”

Rio rolled her eyes, not even sparing him a second glance. Men and their stupid egos. “Do you know where I can find him?”

The tension in the room thickened and the teenager stood, squaring up to her as the other two used their hands to drum roll on the edge of the couch. ‘I said-”

“Is that my favorite coven member I hear?” A deep, familiar voice drawled from the doorway.

Rio bit back a scoff. Wade always had to make an entrance. She sighed as she turned around, plastering a smile on her face Wade Wilson, Murdock's second in command, stepped through the door.

“Relax boys,” he said, waving his hand. “This is someone you do not want to pick a fight with. Now scram.”

Rio waved them off with a sarcastic smile as they left the room, glaring at her one last time before she turned her focus back to Wade.

He’d taken a seat on the stained couch in the corner. “Lady death, what an honor. Heard you got locked up. You know, I wasn’t expecting to see you here so soon, but I guess some habits never die.”

That stupid fucking nickname had been given to her after her first few kills, and for some reason it had stuck. Jen and Alice had found it hilarious, but it wasn’t all bad. Maybe it was for the best that the gangs on the streets didn’t know her real name.

She sat down on an armchair opposite him, trying not to let her mind linger for too long on the array of stains that littered it. “I need something.”

Wade grinned. “You’ve come to the right person. What will it be? Coke? MDMA? Something stronger?”

Rio crossed her arms. “Information, Wade.”

Wade tilted his head, narrowing his eyes at her. “Information doesn’t come cheap sweetheart.”

Without hesitating, Rio pulled out a stack of wallets she had nicked on her way to the station and tossed them at him. Wade snatched them up, looking inside each one with eager fingers. He let out a low whistle. “You have got yourself a deal. What do you want to know?”

“October 2014, a kid died. Gunshot wound if I remember correctly. What was his name?”

“You're going to have to narrow that down for me. We’re in Boston, not some Disney suburb. Kids drop like flies around here.”

“He was around 16. Brunette, hazel eyes, hair down to about his shoulders. He was killed on your territory.”

Wade's grin faded slightly. “Sixteen, damn that’s young. Let me think. October you said? I think I remember that. Uh Nathan? Noah? Nah that’s not right…”

Rio’s fingers tapped the edge of her jeans, her patience thinning.

Wade snapped his fingers. “Nicholas. That’s the one. Nicholas something or other.”

Rio’s stomach dropped. She bit back the wave of nausea that threatened to overwhelm her. She had been clinging to the desperate hope that she was wrong, that maybe this boy looking so oddly familiar was purely a coincidence.

But no. It was Nicky.

She quickly schooled her expression back into indifference. She had a reputation to keep up after all.

“Do you know who killed him?”

Wade tutted, holding up his hands in a ‘what can I say’ gesture. “That’s gonna cost you extra.”

Rio took her switchblade out of her pocket, a sharp click sounding as she flipped it. Wade's grin faltered as she twirled it between her fingers with practiced ease, the glint of sharp steel catching on the dim light.

“Let me make this simple for you, Wade,” Rio said cooly, her gaze locking onto his. “You tell me what I want to know, and I don’t start getting creative with this.”

“Jesus, fine. You really know how to ruin the mood, God,” Wade muttered, sinking into the couch to put as much distance between himself and Rio as he could. “Honestly, I don’t know who killed him. There was an altercation downtown and people got caught in the crossfire. It could have been a multitude of people.”

He paused, the glint in his eyes returning. “I can tell you who was responsible though.”

“You just said you didn’t know who the killer was.”

“And I don’t. But I can tell you who organized the attack in the first place. You know the Serrano gang?”

Rio nodded as Wade continued.

“Back in 2014, the one who called the shots was a guy going by the name of Castillo. If you're looking for someone to blame, that’s the best you're gonna get.”

Rio’s grip on her blade tightened. “When can I find him?”

“South end, using hanging around Melnea Cass Boulevard. Ask around and someone will point you in his direction.”

Rio snapped her knife shut and slid it back into her pocket. Without another word she turned to leave, ignoring Wade as he called after her.

______

The Serrano gang's territory wasn’t hard to find. The South End was notorious – a collection of crumbling sidewalks and dilapidated warehouses that had been out of use for years.

As Rio headed towards Melnea Cass Boulevard, she couldn’t help but feel a sense of déjà vu. She had never been to this specific part of Boston before – she hadn’t had a reason to once Lilia had taken her in – but on the many occasions she had run away from her foster families, she had very often ended up in places like this.

Back then she had been a messed up kid searching the streets for her next high. Now she was a messed up adult, searching for a man who would very soon regret the day he was born.

She supposed that was some form of character development.

She walked aimlessly through the streets, peeking behind corners to alleys in search of - she wasn’t quite sure what. But someone around here had to be able to help her.

Patience slowly waning, she played absentmindedly with the straps of her backpack. She wasn’t nervous, instead she felt almost excited, adrenaline rushing through her body. This would be her first kill since she got locked up, and God she had missed the feeling.

She came to a sudden halt when she turned a corner, seeing a group of men sitting down by a makeshift fire. Some guy threw something on it, chuckling when sparks went flying everywhere.

Rio could feel her heart pounding heavier – if someone knew the way to this Castillo guy, it was most certainly them.

Walking closer, she could make out some key features. Two of the guys had tattoos running down their neck, creeping underneath their collars. A majority of them had a shaved head, their sweaty scalps from the warmth of the fire almost glistering in the afternoon light.

What a charming bunch of clichés.

She straightened her back before letting her posture sink to something like feigned nervousness. She adjusted her backpack, taking her hair down and letting it fall to frame her face.

“Um, excuse me?” She called out timidly, voice a perfect mix of innocence and uncertainty.

The men looked up at her, their conversation dying as they noticed her. A man with the cigarette looked her up and down in a way that made her want to squirm. “You lost, sweetheart?”

Rio bit her lip, looking at the men worriedly. “I think so. I’m looking for someone but this place is a little… scary, if you don’t mind me saying so.”

The group laughed, the sound low and mocking in a way that made Rio’s skin crawl.

“Who are you lookin’ for darlin’? Maybe we can help?”

“Oh, that would be so helpful, thank you! I was told to give this,” she gestured to her backpack, “to someone named Castillo.”

“Who sent you, huh? And what’s in the bag?” One of the men asked, standing up. His bald head tattooed with a poisonous vine circling up behind his ear.

He, like the rest of them, was wearing obnoxious jewelry. A big chain hanging low down his neck – just as low as his jeans, barely holding it together, even with the big belt.

Rio had to resist the urge to roll her eyes. God, these men were repulsive.

But she was skilled, having dealt with way too many men like this for her liking. So she continued the act;

“I think they called themselves the X-men or something. And I don’t know, I didn’t look, I swear! I was just told to make sure it went straight to him, and that he would know what to do with it.”

“What’s your name?”

“Riley.”

“And what’s a pretty little thing like you doing running errands for the X-men?”

“This guy online said he would give me 100 dollars if I helped him with something, and I agreed. I didn’t know it would be this. I wanted to say no, but he said he would hurt me if I didn’t. Oh god, I’m such an idiot.”

The men started to look uncomfortable as tears streaked her cheeks. One of them cleared his throat. “You're lucky you ran into us Riley, this place ain’t safe for a pretty thing like you. You’re looking for Castillo you say? He’ll probably be hanging around the warehouse district.”

Rio pretended to wipe her eyes with her sleeve. “Where is that? I’m sorry, I’m not from around here.”

“No shit”, one of the men scoffed.

“Turn left down fifth until you see the old clock tower. Go through it and you should see this big building with broken windows. Will have this symbol,” he motioned towards the one on his neck, “on the door.”

Rio nodded, forcing a look of gratitude on her face. “Thank you so much. I don’t know what would have happened if I hadn’t come across you all.”

“Our pleasure, little lady. Once you're done with Castillo why don’t you give us a visit, eh?”

Rio would rather drink a carton of bleach, but she nodded, batted her eyelids at the men and promised that she would.

______

The warehouse district looked ahead of her, a collection of decrepit buildings with broken windows and rusted metal siding. The air smelled of oil and damp concrete, and Rio resisted the urge to cough.

The muffled sounds of activity grew louder the closer she got. She stepped over the chain link fence and headed towards the open sliding door.

The warehouse was dimly lit by hanging bulbs, casting an unnatural light on the men busy doing various tasks – stacking crates, smoking, moving boxes. At least half of them seemed to be carrying guns, and Rio could already tell they had big ‘compensating for something’ energy.

It didn’t take her long to spot the man who had to be Castillo. He was in the middle, yelling at some greasy haired teenager. He was six foot something, his shaved head gleaming under the harsh light. Even from a distance, she could see the way the other way acted around him – nervously flitting in and out of his space. He wore dark shades which made it impossible to see his eyes.

Rio paused, thinking. She needed a way to get close to him without raising suspicion. A few of the men had noticed her now and were watching her with curious, hungry eyes. The nervous, desperate girl act wouldn’t work here – not with these men. They’d eat her alive if she showed any signs of weakness.

She curved her lips into a slow, deliberate smile and let her posture shift, hips swaying slightly as she stepped properly into the building. She unzipped her hoodie, letting the crop top she has specifically worn for this occasion show.

She tilted her head, voice full of fake sultry confidence. “Don’t all stare at once,” she said, loud enough for the men closest to her to hear, “I’m looking for Castillo.”

The nearest man, a wiry guy with tattoos snaking up his neck glared at her, narrowing his eyes. “Who the hell are you?”

Rio gave a casual shrug, grinning at him. “Let’s just say… I’m a little gift from the X-men. They wanted to apologize for the disruption they caused during your last shipment.”

Rio was lying her ass off, but she’d picked up on the dispute between the two gangs and assumed it was a safe bet. Clearly she had been right.

The man exchanged a glance with one of his buddies. “The X-men, huh. Those little pricks have been screwing with us for years now. Why are they suddenly sending gifts?”

“I don’t ask questions, I just go where I’m told.” She ran a hand along the strap of her backpack, drawing attention to the curve of her shoulder.

The man hesitated, looking her up and down before calling over to Castillo. “Boss, there’s a girl here for you. Says she was sent by the X-men.”

The warehouse seemed to quiet for a moment as Castillo turned his head in their direction. His expression was unreadable, his dark shades hiding any emotion in his eyes, but the smirk on his face was undeniable. He walked towards them, circling Rio like a predator. She fought the urge to gag.

“Murdock sending me girls now?” He drawled, his voice uncomfortably smooth. “Thought he owed me cash, not company.”

Rio forced a coy smile only on her lips, tilting her head just enough to seem unbothered. “Maybe he wanted to smooth things over with a… more personal touch.”

Castillo chuckled darkly, reaching out to cup her face and tilt it towards his. Rio didn’t resist, smiling at him sweetly.

Honestly, it wasn’t a surprise that so much shit went on inside the gangs around Boston. These men were some of the most stupid people Rio has ever met. She thanked the universe for making her a Lesbian.

Castillo continued to inspect Rio’s face. “Smart man.” His hands moved to Rio’s waist, and she fought the urge to slit this throat right there and then. “Why don’t we go somewhere a little more private, huh?”

Keeping one hand firmly on her waist, he led her through a side door and around to the back of the building. Rio kept up the act, leaning into him slightly as her hand rested lightly on his chest.

“Alright sweetheart, let’s see what you’ve got.”

Rio smiled sweetly, stepping closer until she was standing between his legs. “It would be my pleasure.”

In one swift motion, she pulled out her switchblade, flipped it open with practised ease and drove it straight into his chest.

Castillo’s smirk vanished as he let out a choked gasp, his body jerking against hers. His hand shot up to try and grab her wrist, but Rio was relentless. She twisted the blade harshly, Castillo’s shirt quickly turning red with his blood.

“This is for Nicky,” Rio hissed, ripping the knife free and driving it into his chest again, right below his sternum. Blood bubbled at Castillo’s lips, his dark glasses falling to the ground as his body spasmed forwards. His eyes were wide, looking at her in shock.

“Do you even know what you did?” She snarled, dragging the blade downwards. “He was 16, just a kid, gunned down because of your orders.”

Castillo’s mouth opened, but no sound came out except for a wet, ragged gasp. Rio’s grip on his shirt tightened as she withdrew her knife again, Castillo’s hands doing nothing to stem the heavy flow of blood.

With a grin that resembled that of a shark sizing up its prey before a kill, Rio leaned in until her mouth was inches away from his ear. “See you in hell,” she hissed, slamming the blade directly into his neck. Blood coated her hands and face, but she didn’t care. Her only focus was watching the light fade from the man’s eyes as he choked around her blade.

Castillo’s body slid to the floor as Rio stepped back, his glasses crunching under her foot. She learned down and picked them up, smearing the splatters of blood away with her sleeve before pocketing them.

It wasn't like he would be needing them any more.

She looked at the body on the floor one last time, spitting on it for good measure.

What a piece of shit.

She pocketed her blade and scaled the rusted gate towards the end of the alley, slipping into the shadows. It was getting dark now, and the cold air bit at her skin. She took off her jacket, using it to wipe the blood off her face and hands before taking a clean one out of her backpack and shoving the ruined one inside. She had come prepared.

With any luck, it would be an hour or two before anyone realized something was wrong. Castillo didn’t seem like the type who liked to be interrupted, and that arrogance would work in her favor. By the time someone came to check, Rio would be long gone. Hits like this happened all the time – a turf war, a deal gone wrong, an eye for an eye. The Serreno gang would assume it was retaliation from a rival gang, probably the X-men. That didn’t bother Rio – Murdock and Wade could handle their own. The police wouldn’t even bother digging into it. Men like Castillo weren’t worth the paperwork.

As she approached the nearest station she looked at her phone, checking the time. It was only then that she realized it was Monday.

Shit.

She’d completely forgotten about her session with Agatha.

Agatha

Agatha nervously drummed her fingers against the armrest, her eyes flicking to her wrist watch every ten seconds.

Their session was supposed to have started 10 minutes ago, and even though Agatha wouldn’t put it past Rio to be late, she’d never been before.

A million scenarios ran through her head. Maybe the bus was late? Maybe Rio got stuck in traffic? Maybe she accidentally slept in?

It didn’t make any sense – Rio skipping out on a session, that is. Firstly, it could create problems with her probation officer, if Agatha decided to report her absence.

A very big if.

If Agatha decided to report her, it wouldn’t surprise her at all if Rio pulled the ‘my therapist slept with me’ card. That was not a cute story, nor one that she could save. Because, well, it was true.

She settled on letting it slide. She knew that was neither the moral or the professional thing to do – reporting a client's unannounced absence was mandatory when they were on probation. But for now, she decided against it. Rio’s words from Friday night echoing in her mind loudly;

“I'll tell them that you kissed me and then you’ll have your license revoked.”

As if that wasn’t bad enough, Agatha had only gone and fucked her. She mentally slapped herself for being such an idiot.

For a second, she doubted everything. What if Rio only slept with her to have something over her – something to gaslight her with so that she could start skipping sessions – and Agatha wouldn’t be able to do something about it?

That hurt for multiple reasons. Not only would that mean Rio didn’t actually want to sleep with her, and only did it for personal gain – but it would also mean Rio didn’t care, and didn’t want to have these sessions.

Agatha already knew that much. But the part about Rio not really wanting to sleep with her hurt more than she’d like to admit. She hated the idea of being used.

It didn’t make sense though. Because beneath the flirtatious, and mostly annoying, act Rio was putting up, there was something real. Agatha knew there was, she had seen it in Rio’s eyes when she had gripped her hand and begged her not to leave.

When five more minutes went by without a sign of Rio, she angrily closed her notebook and stood up. If Rio wouldn’t even bother to show up, then Agatha certainly wasn’t going to spend the entire morning stuck in a boring office, either.

The early November weather wasn’t forgiving – the icy wind hitting her face the second she walked outside.

She had no pre planned destination, she just needed to breathe. To clear her head.

Ever since Friday, she hadn’t been able to think about anything, or anyone, else. Rio was stuck in her mind like a stubborn splinter she couldn’t get out.

For the most part, it was just pure regret. She hated herself for letting it happen, for letting herself lose control in a way she’d never experienced before. And not even just once, but twice in the span of two days.

The last few minutes of Friday night also ran through her mind on a loop. The way Rio had suddenly left with hardly an explanation not only worried her, but also made her feel guilty.

Rio had clearly felt vulnerable after they’d had sex – something she’d made a mental note to bring up in their session today actually. Agatha had held her for as long as her conscience would allow, but then the rational part of her brain had kicked in and she needed to distance herself from her, both physically and mentally. She excused herself to shower, deliberately ignoring the look in Rio’s eyes that begged her to stay for a little longer.

She’d spent about ten minutes sitting on the bathroom floor, desperately trying not to completely freak out, before she’d even turned the water on. She knew she had been gone for longer than was necessary, longer than she’d promised, and maybe that had made Rio upset. Sure, Rio was a pain in her ass 90% of the time, but Agatha never wanted to hurt her. Rio was younger than she was, and she had been through an unimaginable amount of shit.

Also thinking about that made her want to violently throw up. She had been so stuck on the entire therapist-client situation of it all to even consider the age difference.

She ran her fingers through her hair, tugging at the roots like it would bring her back to earth.

What the hell was she doing?

Walking aimlessly through the streets of Boston, she highly debated on whether or not to stop by the closest corner shop, buy a pack of cigarettes, and smoke them all in one go. She wasn’t a smoker, per se, but it was a great help when she needed to calm down.

This plan, however, crashed before she could even place a foot inside the threshold.

“Agatha!”

Confused, she looked up to find the source of the familiar voice.

Seeing who it was, she let out a sigh of relief. “Tony.”

Wearing his perfectly tailored suit and expensive sunglasses, he didn’t fit in – neither with the weather or the cheap corner shop.

“Sorry I didn’t go to the AA meeting last week, Morgan got a stomach bug.”

Agatha took a step forward to get inside, the cold air had started to nip at her skin uncomfortably. “It’s fine,” She muttered.

Of course it was fine , she just wished Tony would have warned her before she, unprepared, had to spend about an hour in a room with random people. But she couldn’t tell him that – the way they’d left off last time they’d met loomed too awkwardly in the air.

Tony cleared his throat. “Well, do you have any plans tonight? I know Pepper would love to catch up.”

“Uh, yeah, why not?” Agatha said, a bit thrown off. She knew she had been rude, and she’d imagined Tony would be one to hold grudges for longer. “Tonight?”

“If it’s not too much trouble to come to our house, I think Morgan would be happy to see you,” he said, biting obnoxiously onto his sandwich.

Agatha shrugged, “I’ll come over. But only if you promise not to serve that awful kale salad you did last time.”

Tony feigned offense, “I like that salad.”

Agatha rolled her eyes. “No you don’t.”

“I can at least pretend to.”

“Oh come on, I saw the way your face scrunched up when you had that first bite.” “It’s not my fault Pepper has been on some ‘healthy climate-friendly’ diet. I’ll tell her to lay off it for an evening. See you at seven?”

Agatha nodded and stepped past him further into the shop.

Behind her, the bell above the door rang, announcing his exit.

She took a deep breath and straightened her back with a roll of her shoulders. Tony wasn’t mad at the way she treated her. That was good – great even. She didn’t have many friends, and she couldn’t afford to lose the few who put up with her.

And, to be honest with herself, she really needed the distraction.

______

The rest of the day had gone by in a haze, but at least she had something to look forward to now.

Dinner with Tony and Pepper wasn’t always the highlight of her week, but they were good entertainment, and the food was good, most of the time .

She knocked on the door to their house. It was a bit of a drive out from the central parts of the city – Tony had a more central apartment for late work-nights, but he tried to spend most of his time here, with his family.

Thinking about that almost made her cry.

Morgan opened the door, clinging to the handle like she’d fall if she didn’t. She smiled up at her. “Hi Agatha,” She said, a bit shy as she hid her face into the door. She’d just turned six and recently lost a few milk teeth, so she lisped slightly.

Agatha leant down, resting her palms against her thighs to get on eye level. “Hi honey,” She said gently, her heart warming at the sight. She remembered that stage with Nicky.

“Morgan!” Called Pepper from within the house, “Let our guest in, it’s cold outside.”

Morgan looked at Agatha with innocent eyes before taking a step back. Agatha, who indeed had turned very cold, smiled appreciatively and walked inside.

“Thank you for having me!” She said as a greeting once she was instead, looking around the corner to find Pepper in the kitchen. Her gray apron was tied around her waist and her blonde hair was in a lazy ponytail as she stood by the stove and stirred absentmindedly in a stew.

Agatha removed her coat. “Hope there’s no kale in that.”

Pepper let out a light laugh, “No, Tony made me promise not to. I’m cooking truffle pasta.”

Agatha scrunched her nose, but her thoughts were interrupted by a small hand grabbing her arm.

“I wanna show you something,” Morgan said, tugging her towards the living room.

Pepper shot an apologetic look before Agatha turned around, letting herself be guided through the house.

Morgan sat down on the carpet, in front of a beautifully detailed dollhouse. “Look!” She said, grabbing two colorful toy horses and shoving them way too close to Agatha’s face as she sat down as well. Her back would not thank her later.

“What are they?” Agatha asked kindly, leaning back a little.

“My little pony,” Morgan answered, rolling her eyes playfully. “Dummy.”

Agatha smiled, “Oh, I should’ve known.”

Morgan laughed, bringing the horses down and placed them on the roof, muttering something to herself.

Agatha squirmed uncomfortably, feeling a bit out of place. But she sat still, enjoying the simplicity of the moment. Sometimes, meeting Morgan was like a stab to the heart – a reminder of what she had lost. But today, it didn’t feel unwelcome, rather like a warm hug.

“Are you crying?” Morgan asked suddenly, pulling her out of the moment.

Agatha hadn’t even noticed it. She quickly brought the heel of her hand to her face and brushed away a stray tear that had fallen to her cheek.

“No,” She tried, giving a reassuring smile to Morgan. “I…” she paused for a second, coming up with some excuse. “Sometimes I cry when it’s cold outside.”

“That’s stupid,” Morgan said bluntly before returning to her toys.

“Morgan, don’t be mean to our guests,” Tony said, appearing from behind them. He swiftly picked Morgan up and spun her around before placing her on his hip.

Morgan squealed in laughter.

“How’s it going for the house chef?” Tony asked over his shoulder.

“Ready in a minute,” Pepper's voice could be heard from the kitchen along with a few plates being placed on the wooden table.

Tony released Morgan and bent down to her eye level. “Go help your mom in the kitchen,” He stage-whispered with a reassuring pat on her shoulder.

Agatha tried to stand up, but her knees gave in beneath her.

“I made her promise not to put any kale in it.” Tony winked before throwing himself on the couch.

Agatha rolled her eyes, “Did you have to make it obvious?”

“Your facial expressions last time were more revealing than you’d think.”

“Dinner!” Pepper called.

Agatha held out her hand, feeling completely stranded on the floor. Tony smirked before helping her up. “You need some exercise for those knees.”

“And back,” Agatha added with a groan, earning a slight chuckle.

Dinner, believe it or not, actually tasted great. Agatha had been sceptical at first, and the smell definitely wasn’t convincing. But she’d learned, over the years, to not underestimate Pepper’s cooking skills.

“So, how’s work treating you?” Pepper asked, taking a sip of her cider.

It was alcohol-free, of course – which Agatha, truth be told, highly appreciated. She always felt awkward having to ask for something non-alcoholic during dinners when she went out, which is why she appreciated these dinners with Tony and his family – she never felt out of place in that regard.

“It’s good, yeah,” Agatha forced, swallowing down the lie with a mouthful of pasta.

Tony looked at her concerned, “Are you sure, you’ve been… tense the last few weeks.”

“Well that might have to do with other stuff ,” Agatha gritted through her teeth, throwing a pointed glance at Morgan.

Morgan had no idea what happened to Agatha ten years ago, that she’d had a son. And frankly, there was no need to put all that weight on a six year old. Which is another reason Agatha liked these family dinners – they never brought up Nicky.

“Right,” Tony muttered, looking down at his plate. “So it’s all good? No new fun patients to talk about?”

“I have patient confidentiality,” She said sternly. “I can’t go around talking about my patients with you, that would be unprofessional.”

“Never stopped you before,” Tony commented nonchalantly, leaning back into the chair.

The words dug deeper than probably intended. Because yes she wasn’t a professional – this prior weekend has proved that enough. Did Tony have to shove it in her face, too?

“I’m done now,” Morgan said, climbing out of her chair.

Agatha lets out a thankful sigh that the conversation had been interrupted.

“What do we say?” Tony asked, nodding to Pepper.

“Thank you,” Morgan muttered.

“And what do we do?”

Morgan groaned but went to grab her plate and place it in the sink. Tony smiled proudly before turning his attention back to Agatha.

His expression turned gentle and Agatha’s stomach turned in anticipation for what conversation was to follow. “What’d you do on the anniversary? You didn’t call.”

“Didn’t think I’d be welcomed, after…” Agatha cleared her throat, interrupting herself before the conversation could take an uncomfortable turn.

Tony smiled, “You shouldn’t have been afraid to come over.”

“I treated you like shit.”

“Yeah but you had a reason to.” Tony said firmly, and Agatha couldn’t argue with that. “So, what did you do to pass the time?”

“I drank an entire bottle of vodka.”

“Oh my god,” Pepper exclaimed, exasperated.

Tony simply smirked, “No you didn’t.”

“Okay, maybe I didn’t. I wanted to, though. I went to sleep early, but honestly the entire day was a blur.”

Pepper chimed in, “And how are you now? Are you feeling okay?”

Agatha shrugged, “I appreciate you caring, but really, if I want to talk about it I will bring it up myself.”

Tony held his hands up in defeat.

“So…” Agatha paused, trying to think of a topic to shift the conversation. “Business, huh?”

Tony chuckled. “Going great, thank you for asking.”

“I didn’t,” Agatha muttered.

The tension was weirdly interrupted by Morgan, who was now standing awkwardly at Agatha’s side. “Can I show you something?”

“You already showed me your new ponies, kiddo.”

“I know, silly! I mean the episode,” She then turned to Tony and Pepper, on the opposite side of the table. “Mom, dad, can I please show Agatha my little pony?”

“If Agatha wants to see it,” Pepper said, her voice kind as she watched Agatha carefully with a calculating expression.

“I’d love to,” Agatha said, letting herself be dragged back into the living room. Honestly, she was just thankful to have a proper excuse to leave the dinner table and the conversation behind.

She sat down beside Morgan on the couch, letting her rest comfortably against her side as she pressed play on the episode. Almost out of habit her hand went to Morgan’s hair, massaging her scalp reassuringly.

For the first time in a long time, it didn’t hurt. Instead, the small action was a comfort, something that brought her the closure she needed at that moment.

Morgan sighed happily, laughed along to some of the dialogue. Agatha didn’t pay any attention to the program, her mind drifting away slowly.

Primarily, it drifted to Rio again – along with a sense of dread.

What the fuck was she gonna say during their next session?
