Agatha

Agatha drummed her fingers on her notepad as she waited to see if Rio would show up.

The past few days had been a tug of war between irritation and guilt – both of which left her feeling nauseous.

She needed to approach this session carefully. Not was it only Rio, who she always had to tread lightly around – but Friday night, and the endless teasing she was doubtlessly going to get from it, loomed over her.

The back-and-forth had worked surprisingly well in her favor during the last couple of sessions, so changing that system would probably not be a winning concept. However, she had to somehow keep away from the topic of Friday night, the topic about them.

So, when Rio walked in unannounced through the door, Agatha spoke before Rio even had the chance to.

“Good morning.”

“‘Morning,” Rio said tiredly, sitting down on the couch and bringing the freshly brewed coffee to her lips. “You made me coffee.”

“I always make my clients coffee before morning sessions.”

Rio brought a hand to her chest, feigning a hurt expression. “You have other clients?”

Agatha rolled her eyes, not even bothering to respond.

“I’m joking.” Rio took another sip, continuing with a smug grin on her face. “I know you always make me coffee.”

Agatha resisted the urge to scowl at the smirk Rio was giving her. Her hopes that Rio would even give a voluntary explanation for her absence during the last session hadn’t been high. But for her to not even acknowledge it in the slightest irritated Agatha immensely. Thankfully, the fact that Rio acted as her usual, infuriating self served as enough confirmation that it wasn’t about the sex. Surely, she would’ve acted differently if that was the case.

She knew the words coming out of Rio’s mouth before she even spoke.

Rio’s smile faltered, her posture slumping slightly before she caught herself.

“So now that’s out of the way,” Agatha crossed her legs and leaned forward slightly, resting her elbows on her knees as she fixed Rio with a pointed stare. “Care to explain why you skipped out on our session on Monday?”

Agatha arched a brow. “Try again.”

There was a beat of silence before Rio sighed, her posture stiffening. “I had to look after my foster mom. It was an emergency,” she said, her voice firmer this time, but there was a flicker of unease in her expression.

Rio’s jaw clenched, and she sat up straighter, defensive energy radiating off her. “I know,” she said quickly, her eyes narrowing. “I’m not lying!”

The raw edge in her voice made Agatha pause. She studied the tension in Rio’s shoulders, the way her fingers gripped the edge of her chair as if bracing for a fight. Agatha blinked, taken aback by the unexpected sincerity in Rio’s voice. Her stomach twisted, not unpleasantly, at the mention of Friday. At least it was confirmation that Rio didn’t regret it. She didn’t know whether that made her feel relieved or more irritated at herself. She wasn’t supposed to care about this. She wasn’t supposed to care about Rio like this.

What a fucking mess.

Agatha arched a brow. “That’s a start. Care to expand on that?”

Rio hesitated for a moment, her jaw tightening as her eyes darted from Agatha’s face to the wall and back again like she wanted to say something. After a beat or two of silence, she turned and walked out, grabbing her coat as she went.

Agatha sat frozen, her hands gripping the armrest of the chair.

Rio was hiding something, and she was determined to find out what.

______

Agatha found herself at the graveyard, like she usually did when she needed to clear her head.

Walking along the gravel path, she spared glances at the deserted gravestones – her heart heavy. She tried her best to entertain Nicky’s grave, bringing flowers almost every week.

She hoped that somehow Nicky knew. Hopes that somehow he could see the flowers she brought, and know that even now, ten years later, she still cared about him. That there wasn’t a single week where she didn’t think about him, miss him and visit him.

She tried to find comfort in that thought.

When she visited on Saturdays – the day when she had the most free time – it was sometimes hard to find the privacy she needed. But today, the entire graveyard almost felt deserted. The only other person she could see was an old man standing next to a plot a few rows over from her, a bouquet of roses in his hand.

It was almost completely silent, except for the steady pace of her shoes against the gravel and the birds chirping from the dead winter trees above.

The first thing she noticed when approaching Nicky’s grave was the flowers. They weren’t hers – someone else had placed flowers on his grave. And based on the look of them, pretty recently, too. No more than two or three days ago.

She recognized them, Rainflower, Iris and Orchids.

She took a step closer, crouching down and running her fingers over their stems in thought. Sometimes people would leave flowers on other graves – but Nicky’s was well kept, and those people usually prioritized the ones without any flowers already there.

When she moved them to the side to allow her newly bought flowers to replace them in the middle, she halted. There was something underneath them.

The glasses were the first thing she saw. They looked expensive, dark shades set in a silver frame. The glass was chipped and cracked, almost like they’d been crushed by something or stood on.

She picked them up, studying them further. She didn’t recognize them at all. The pair looked way too expensive to be anything she or Nicky ever owned. Unless they were Tony’s? She couldn’t possibly understand why he would leave his broken pair of glasses on Nicky's grave.

That was when she saw it.

A white envelope had been weighed down by the glasses, and was now lying exposed on the damp soil. She picked it up, turning it around to see if there was any writing on the front.

There wasn’t.

She opened it with shaky hands, hoping that something would be written inside, at least. She went to go to the grave to clear her head , whatever this was felt like the complete opposite.

Inside the envelope was a letter.

Agatha

I want to start this by saying how sorry I am for your loss. I know it was a long time ago, but it never really gets any easier.

You see, I’ve lost people close to me too. And I’ll never forget them, no matter how much I try to move on, I feel like I never can.

I blame myself for a lot of deaths, but the ones closest to me the most. I know I shouldn’t, and you would probably tell me that, too. But I think I know you well enough that you blame yourself for Nicky’s – so I think you should stop with this hypocritical shit you’ve got going.

You know, sometimes it brings me comfort to know that karma is real. That whoever hurt me in the past, will be hurt the same amount, if not even more. I don’t know if you believe in that or not, but I say that you probably should start.

Whoever hurt Nicky hurt you. And trust me when I say that I think karma has gotten to him already.

Somehow, I hope this will make you happy to hear. And honestly, I think it will. Make you happy, that is, if that wasn’t obvious already. I’m writing this with a goddamn inkpen, I knew I would regret that.

I hope it’s you reading this and not someone else checking up on all the graves, although I could never imagine why someone else would open it. This letter belongs to you.

Again, I’m sorry for your loss. I’m sorry the world is such a shitty place. I’m sorry you never got to see your son grow up. I’m sorry they never found his killer. I’m sorry you have problems with alchohol because of it. I’m sorry you never got the help you clearly needed. And, most of all, I’m sorry for the loss of the wonderful, kind hearted, caring and trusting person your son was.

I’m not sorry, however, for what I just did.

oh my god it's patti lupone!

Agatha

Agatha had been standing outside of Rio’s door for almost five minutes now, furiously pressing on the buzzer repeatedly.

After finding the letter by Nicky’s grave, she’d made her way back to her office feeling even more confused than she’d left it.

She knew there was a very high probability that this was just more of Rio’s bullshit, another attempt to get under her skin. The smart, and perhaps the most rational, thing to do would be to wait until their next session on Monday and question her about it then.

And she almost managed to wait the entire weekend. She told herself again and again to just hold on , to not let her curiosity win – to prove to herself that she was patient, that she wouldn’t let Rio get under her skin.

“I talk to them. Sometimes out loud, or sometimes in a letter which I leave in an envelope next to their flowers.”

Rio’s words echoed in her mind.

Oh for fucks sake.

What could ever be more dramatic than leaving an envelope, addressed to someone, by a grave? And not even signing it with a name. That really felt like something Rio would do.

The mystery of the last line, however, had eventually brought her here; to Rio’s front door, aiming to confront her about it.

Knowing Rio, that last line could refer to anything. And the not knowing of it all made her go insane. For two whole days, she’d been trying to figure it out. Running different scenarios through her mind, biting her nails frantically, while pacing back and forth in her living room.

Sunday, and with only one day left until they would inevitably see each other again, she tripped on the finish line.

And so, after yet another almost sleepless night, she’d checked her records for Rio’s address and headed over to her apartment. She needed to get some answers out of her, and unexpectedly confronting her felt like the best approach.

The only flaw in her plan, now that she stood outside the door, was that Rio was either not home, or deliberately ignoring her. She wished for a second that she had Rio’s lock picking skills, so that she could show up unannounced and see how much Rio liked it.

She was already berating herself for this mess, she didn’t need someone else doing it too.

“In case something happens and you need to call me. Use it for emergencies, please – can’t have Rio’s therapist calling me. That wouldn’t be a great look.”

Agatha had scoffed, thinking Alice was being paranoid. But she also found it quite adorable how caring Rio’s friend seemed. She never thought she’d use it, but she took it either way.

For emergencies.

Well this situation certainly was one.

Alice didn’t pick up at first. Agatha groaned loudly to no one in particular before she, determined, rang again. So what if it was only ten to eight in the morning?

This time Alice picked on the second ring.

“Dude it’s too fucking early, what do you want?” She grumbled, voice heavy with sleep.

Agatha cleared her throat. “Hi Alice, it’s Agatha, Rio’s… therapist.”

Agatha heard a mumbled ‘oh shit’ before Alice spoke, this time sounding much more alert. “I’m so sorry Agatha, I didn’t even check to see who called, I just assumed it was Jen or something. How can I help?”

“It’s fine,” Agatha said curtly. “I was wondering if you knew where Rio was. I need to talk to her and I’m outside her apartment, but no one’s answering.”

On the other end of the line, Alice paused. “Uhhh, maybe try Lilia’s? She’s sometimes around there visiting on weekends.”

“Okay I'll do that. What’s her address?”

“23 Kane Road. Oh, and if you see Rio there, don’t tell her I’m the one who gave the address. It’s too early in the morning to deal with her being mad at me.”

______

It took Agatha around an hour to get to Lilia’s. She lived a little bit outside the city in a cozy neighborhood with white picket fences, and in the morning sun some children were already playing along the side of the street.

By the time she was knocking on the door of the address Alice had given her, she felt like she might throttle Rio on sight for dragging her around so early in the morning. She rang the doorbell once, not even bothering to wait for a response before hammering on the door. Let Rio try and ignore her now.

The door was flung open by a woman who looked to be in her mid sixties, hair frazzled and wearing a mismatch of clothing. She glared at Agatha with sharp eyes. “Who the hell are you and why are you trying to kick my door down?”

Agatha had been expecting Rio to answer, in which case she had planned to simply start yelling at her until she caved. Coming face to face with her foster mom had not been a consideration.

“Uh, I’m Agatha Harkness, Rio’s therapist. I was wondering is she-“

“You're the one she’s also talking about?

“Excuse me?”

“Well aren’t you going to come in? It’s cold outside and you're letting out all the heat.” Before Agatha could respond, she grabbed her arm and practically yanked her through the doorway, the door clicking shut behind her.

“I’m actually just looking for-“

She trailed off as Lilia walked away from her into the kitchen. Agatha stood there for a few moments before following. When Lilia saw her she pulled out a chair at the kitchen table, nodding at Agatha to sit as she turned the kettle on. Lacking an excuse not too, Agatha sat.

She let her eyes look around the kitchen. It was a sharp contrast to the surrounding, fancy neighborhood with colored wallpaper clinging desperately to the walls, dark wooden furniture that could’ve been around for generations. It felt like a home, the mismatched dinnerware still unwashed by the kitchen sink and the excessive amounts of candles on every possible surface.

There were pictures scattered here and there, Agatha assumed they were other kids Lilia had fostered previously. Her eyes caught on one in particular. The girl in it was undoubtedly a 15 year old Rio, her scowl a mirror image to the one that so often made an appearance in their sessions.

“Rio’s not a bad kid you know.”

“I’m sorry?”

Agatha focused her attention on Lilia, who was now standing by the counter glaring at her. “I assume you're here because she’s done something?”

Agatha debated on telling the full truth, but that would mean opening up to yet another person. Instead, she just nodded.

Lilia sighed. “She does some bad stuff sometimes, stupid stuff. But she’s a good person, underneath all of that. She’s just been dealt a crappy hand in life.”

“Do you know where she is now?”

Lilia waved her hands, “I don’t know. Home, probably?”

Agatha frowned, “No, I tried her apartment.”

Lilias eyes narrowed, worry apparent behind them. “She might be at Alice’s, or Jen’s.”

Jen? Agatha hadn’t heard that name before.

“Right,” She said sharply, “I should’ve checked all possibilities before bothering you.”

“It’s fine.” Lilia stood up again, walking to the kettle to pour up two cups. “You might as well stay for tea. I should be glad of the company, it’s been a little while since Rio visited.”

Agatha pursed her lips. “Is that right? She missed a session last week and told me she was with you.”

“She told you that?” Lilia looked bewildered for a second. “The last time I saw her was around a fortnight ago.”

Agatha gave in to the urge of letting her forehead fall to the table in frustration, the thump thud satisfying. She knew that Rio had been lying to her, but having confirmation of that fact made her anger spike. Rio had deceived her, not once but twice , and that felt downright disrespectful.

She heard Lilia sigh softly, the slightly creak of the opposite chair letting Agatha know she had sat down.

“Rio’s… complicated.”

Agatha scoffed under her breath.

Understatement of the century.

“But everything she does, she does for a reason. Whatever’s happened, she’ll have done it because she believes it was her best course of action.”

Agatha lifted her head up to argue, but Lilia cut her off before she could get a word out.

“I’m not saying that it was the right choice, but it will have come from a place of her trying to make things better. As her therapist, I would have hoped you had figured that out about her by now. ”

Agatha fought back the urge to scowl at the fact that maybe Lilia had a point. Rio was her patient for Christ's sake, and these were all patterns that she’d seen in her since she first met her. She was letting her emotions get in the way, and that was why she was currently stuck having tea with some kooky old lady who, for some reason, was doing a better job at psychoanalysing Rio than she was.

Talk about unprofessional.

She inhaled deeply, letting the breath out through her nose. She gave Lilia a grim smile. “Yeah. Maybe.” She put her palms against the side of the cup, letting the sensation anchor her. Now really wasn’t the time for anger.

She looked around for something to distract her. It was then that she saw the opportunity quite literally sitting directly in front of her. Although Rio had started opening up about her experience as a child, she’d only given Agatha bits and pieces so far. If anyone could fill in the rest of that picture, it was Lilia.

Agatha knew that Rio would hate it, but Rio did a lot of things she knew Agatha hated and that had never stopped her before. The reasoning was vague, but the idea was there – Agatha couldn’t help but act on it.

“What was Rio like when you first took her in?”

Lilia looked at her slightly surprised, taking a long breath before chuckling softly. “A handful, to put it mildly. Jesus that girl gave me a lot of grief, turned a few of my hairs grey. Especially those first few weeks, she was constantly running away for days at a time. Honestly, though, I don't blame her.”

Agatha’s brows furrowed. “What do you mean?”

Lilia sighed again, her expression softening. “Rio has been through a lot of shit in her life, and she’s been hurt by a lot of people. It isn’t a surprise that she finds it hard to trust people. It’s not that she doesn’t want to, she’s just scared.”

Agatha took a deep breath. It was really now or never. “Her childhood, right after her parents died – has she talked about that with you? More specifically, her relationship with her first foster family?”

Lilia shook her head firmly. “That’s Rio’s story to tell. It took years for her to tell me enough that I could even begin to understand, and it’s not my place to share that information.”

Agatha nodded slowly. She was curious, yes, but she wouldn’t push Lilia to share anything unless she wanted to – Agatha could understand boundaries.

Not like some people.

So instead of pushing, she settled on changing the subject. “She cares about you alot now. What changed?”

“Time, I think. She threw all kinds of things at me – coming home high, breaking everything in her room, shoplifting, stealing money, drinking till she’d pass out. I never got angry at her though, not once. I don’t know if you’ve noticed, but Rio doesn’t respond particularly well to being told what to do.”

Agatha snorted. That was putting it lightly.

“But once she realized that I wasn’t there to punish her, I think she began to trust me a little more. Just in little ways at first – like calling me at two in the morning to come pick her up after she’d snuck out and gotten drunk in the park. It doesn't sound like much, but the fact that she reached out for me to come get her was a big thing. Baby steps, of course, but that was how I knew I was getting through to her. After enough time, she began letting her guard down around me more and more.”

Agatha sat completely still. It hurt more than she’d like to admit, hearing Lilia describe those early days, how difficult Rio had it. It was normal to have empathy for clients, Agatha knew that – but this, whatever this sick feeling turning her stomach was, did not feel the same.

“All that girl wants more than anything is to be loved and cared for. She just finds it hard to let herself be.”

Agatha nodded, her throat suddenly feeling tight. She felt sick. Fuck, she really needed to find Rio. Whether to hug her or shake some sense into her, she wasn’t yet sure. But she needed to do something.

Instead she settled for muttering, “She acts like she doesn’t care about connection.”

Lilia smiled softly. “At first, I thought the same thing. She was difficult. I didn’t want to be too overbearing, in case that would scare her away. But, on the other hand, I couldn’t be too absent, because that would result in her thinking I didn’t care about her.”

Agatha cleared her throat, praying that her voice wouldn’t betray her and expose the vulnerability she felt. “So what did you do?”

“At first, I kept a safe distance, allowing her to come to me in case she needed anything. She rarely did, but when she did I was a supporting hand. Didn’t put many boundaries on her, either, because I knew that would cause her to freak out.”

Agatha couldn’t help but smile. After hearing everything Rio had gone through before Lilia, hearing the care Lilia had for her made her feel warm. She also had a massive load of respect for the women – dealing with Rio was a struggle now, she couldn’t imagine how difficult it must have been when she was a teenager.

Lilia continued. “And then, after a really hard night I took her to meet the co-” she paused. “... I took her to a youth centre. She was comfortable there, and I think when she realized that I saw a future for her, she started listening more. Started respecting me more.”

“Sounds like you did great with her,” Agatha mused.

“It wasn’t always a walk in the park, though. We fought a lot.”

“You did?” Agatha asked, trying to sound surprised – she wasn’t, at all , of course. Around 70% of her conversations with Rio ended up in arguments.

Lilia chuckled, “Oh yeah. Mostly over the drugs, because even if I tried to support her through everything, it was hard to see her struggle like that.”

“Yeah, we’ve talked a little about her problems with drugs in sessions. Do you know when that started?”

Lilia took a sip of her tea and signed, wrapping her hands around the mug and she put it back on the table. “Honestly, I’m not sure when exactly it started. By the time she came to me it was already a problem. It’s the whole reason she ended up under my care really, it was that or juvie.”

“So they just dumped a drug addicted teenager on your doorstep and expect you to do… what, exactly?”

“I’ve taken in a lot of kids with similar issues. I was supposed to give that up, but then I got the call about Rio and I knew I had to take her,” Lilia fought a smile tugging at the corners of her lips. “And it was clear to me, already back then, that the only thing she needed was a constant, something she could rely on. So I became that.”

Agatha tilted her head, skepticism showing in her eyes. “Did it work?”

“Sort of.” Lilia said, shrugging. “Monitoring a teenager's drug use is like trying to hold water in your bare hands – it slips through the cracks. And Rio’s too skilled for her own good at hiding things.”

Agatha stifled a snort. She could say that again.

“She used to hang out with some kids on the streets, and that’s when she first got hooked onto ketamine. She was pretty good at hiding it at first, but as it got worse she got more desperate. I caught her once stealing cash from my wallet.”

Agatha raised an eyebrow. “And you let her live? I admire your self restraint.”

Lilia chuckled dryly. “Barley. The screaming match we had over that… I’m pretty sure the neighbours thought we were trying to kill one another.But yelling at Rio only makes her dig her heels in more. I tried grounding her, but it’s not like she particularly cared.”

“Yeah well, that’s Rio,” Agatha muttered. “She only really ever looks out for herself.”

Lilia downright laughed, raising her brows at Agatha in disbelief. “Are you kidding? All Rio ever does is care about others. Even back then, she was always looking out for the people around her – especially the younger ones. I think they saw her as some sort of protector.”

Agatha blinked, surprised by the conviction in Lilia’s voice. “Protector? Rio?”

“Yes,” Lilia said firmly. “There was this one boy – her dealer, actually. I think he was running drugs for someone else, but she became… fond of him. She spent a lot of time with him, and, ironically, I think that connection helped her want to get clean. She didn’t want him to end up worse off.”

Agatha frowned, tapping her nails against the table. “And what happened to him?”

Lilia hesitated, her gaze flicking away. “He… moved on, I guess. People on the streets come and go.”

The answer felt too quick, too rehearsed, but Agatha let it slide. “So, what then? She was older, more independent, but…”

“She started withdrawing more,” Lilia admitted, her voice quieter now. “I thought it was a good thing at first – that she was finding her own footing. But then she disappeared for two weeks without a word. That’s when I went to her apartment and found out about the heroin.”

The word hit Agatha like a punch to the gut, even though she already knew. Still, hearing it laid out like this brought a bitter taste to her mouth. “What pushed her to that?”

Lilia sighed, rubbing a hand over her face. “Everything, I think. Living alone, struggling to keep it together, and then… her parents. She never really processed losing them. I think the grief, the guilt—it just became too much. The heroin was her way of drowning it all out.”

Hearing about Rio’s struggles affected Agatha, more than it probably ought to. So sue her for caring about a patient – It’s not like that’s out of the ordinary. She took a deep breath. “Rio told me prison helped her get clean.”

“It did,” Lilia said with a small nod. “That’s the one good thing about prison. It forced her to come off it. Those last few months before her arrest, I was terrified she’d overdose. I sat with her through cravings, tried everything to keep her from slipping back, but… it never lasted.”

Agatha swallowed hard, forcing her voice to stay steady. “She wouldn’t talk to you?”

“Not to me, not to her friends – none of us could get through to her. Prison was awful for her, but it got her clean. And, so far, she’s stayed that way.”

Lilia’s voice grew quieter, and she paused, parting her lips as though to say more but stopping herself. Agatha leaned forward, her brow furrowing. “What is it?”

“It’s just…” Lilia hesitated, her hands clasping together. “I don’t know how much she’s told you about that time in her life. But it wasn’t easy. For anyone.”

Agatha leaned forward. “What do you mean?”

Lilia let out a shaky breath, “I want her to share those things on her own prompting. It feels wrong to… expose those parts of her.”

“Of course,” Agatha nodded, trying to swallow a scoff.

God, this woman was stubborn – what could possibly be so secretive that Rio had to be the one to share it. Rio never shares anything. If Agatha wanted to know what Lilia is referring to, she’d have to push harder. Relying on Rio for answers, at this point, felt futile.

“Is there anything you can tell me, though? Did she have any friends? A job? Something in her life that brought an escape – except for the drugs – that she turned to?”

“Rio had a lot of people close to her, but she considered few of them friends. Her closest were Jen and Alice, and that trio is still going strong,” Lilia laughed lightly, although Agatha was quite certain it was more to lift the underlying tension between them than a result of humour.

“I’ve met Alice.” Agatha explains, hoping that if she talked Lilia might become more comfortable to open up.

Lilia’s eyebrows flew up, “You have?”

“Yeah,” Agatha sighed. “I ran into them at a bar a few weeks ago.”

“Jen wasn’t with them?” Lilia narrowed her eyes in thought.

“No,” Agatha shook her head. “But I could see, in the few minutes we talked, how strong their friendship is. How they’d do anything to help each other. I’m guessing it was like that even back then?”

“Yeah,” Lilia nodded. “They’ve always taken care of each other. I guess, when she moved in here and eventually met Alice and Jen, we became like a little family – Jen and Alice became her sisters. I rarely saw one without the other two.”

Agatha couldn’t help but smile a little, imagining teenage Rio doing teenage stuff with her best friends. Alice and Jen seemed like good people, not that she knew anything in particular about Jen. But with the amount of respect and care Alice held for Rio, she couldn’t help but admire their friendship.

“But that must’ve changed when Rio was put in prison. Do you know if that dynamic shifted because of that?”

Lilia knitted her eyebrows. “No, even seven years later it’s like nothing ever happened.”

“But your best friend killing someone must have put a strain in their dynamic, no?”

Agatha knew it was a blunt question, but in all fairness it was an honest one. It would be useful for her session if she could understand the wider picture on how Rio’s prison sentence had affected not only her but those around her.

But Lilia shook her head, “No. Since she was charged with involuntary manslaughter, her friends never viewed her any differently. Instead, they were very supportive throughout the whole process, and ultimately very disappointed and sad when she was put in prison.”

“Disappointed… in her?”

“Absolutely not!” Lilia snapped, but she quickly regained herself. “They would never blame Rio for that.”

Agatha realized she had unintentionally hit a nerve. She took a deep breath, the beat of silence filled the room and danced between the walls. She could feel Lilia’s unrelenting gaze on her, and she had to stop herself from squirming out of it.

Instead, she cleared her throat. “How did Rio meet them?”

What she thought would be a fairly easy subject to switch to, turned out to hit yet another nerve. Agatha furrowed her eyebrows in confusion as she saw the way Lilia froze mid motion, her mind visibly spinning.

“They met…” Lilia cleared her throat. “Well, when they were still teenagers.”

“I know,” Agatha nodded, prompting Lilia to continue, looking at her warily.

“I think they met…” Lilia paused again, her vision going blank. A moment or two passed, and all Agatha could do was sit there, completely stunned in the silence.

What the fuck is going on? Was she having some kind of seizure?

As quickly as Lilia had stopped, she started again. “They met through work,” she hurried the sentence, as if it pained her to say it. She paused, taking a few deep breaths – Agatha had never been more confused in her entire life. “Or… not through work but through a place where they would have a community for young girls who needed- I don’t know… help? Who needed company. I took her to it, and Jen and Alice were there.”

Agatha nodded slowly, trying to puzzle the pieces together. Lilia had given her vague information, but still no information at all – it was mildly infuriating. She shook her head, trying to gather her thoughts. “Okay,” she said, dragging out the word for too long. “So they met through the youth center you mentioned earlier?”

“Oh yes, that one!”

Agatha knitted her eyebrows. She was certain, by now that Lilia was holding back on something, although she couldn’t really put her finger on what.

In a hurry, she chugged down the last of the tea, feeling the warm beverage cling to the sides of her throat as she stood up. She had been here for quite a while now, and she needed to head home to prepare her notes for a week full of clients.

“Thank you for the tea. But I don’t want to hold you any longer.”

“Of course,” Lilia forced a smile. “You’re welcome, the pleasure was all mine.”

Agatha nodded sharply, putting on her coat. “And thank you for sharing your experiences with Rio. The insight will be truly useful in future sessions.”

“No problems at all,” Lilia smiled, standing up and putting the cups in the sink. “And say hello to Rio from me in your session tomorrow. I miss her, please ask her to come by, will you?”

“Of course” Agatha opened the door, feeling the cold winds hit her the second she took a step outside. “Take care, Ms Calderu.”

“Goodbye, Ms Harkness!”

Chapter End Notes

Rio will be back in the next chapter... get ready

Chapter 13

Chapter Notes

Our favourite little psycho is back :)

Rio

Rio was exhausted.

On Saturday evening, Matt had asked her to meet him – how exactly he’d gotten her number she wasn’t sure – and she assumed that they had finally figured out she was responsible for Castillo’s death. It had taken them far too long, in her opinion. Almost a week? Pathetic, really.

Saturday night had bled into Sunday morning, hours spent with Matt and Wade tying up any loose ends. They’d staked out the Serreno Gang’s hideout, knocking back around six cups of disgusting, and far too strong, black coffee just to stay awake.

“You think they’ll retaliate?” Matt had muttered, passing Rio a cup of the lukewarm coffee.

“They’re not stupid,” she had replied, taking a sip and trying not to gag. The coffee was terrible, but at least it kept her awake. “If they do end up blaming the X-men, they know going after you would be a losing game. Besides, Castillo was a liability for them anyways. I basically did them a favour.”

Wade had scoffed from where he was lounging in the backseat. “Liability or not, you can bet that they will want to make a point somehow.”

They’d stayed there until shortly after four am, but they didn’t see anything out of the ordinary – just the usual deals and territorial aggression. By the time they’d wrapped up, Rio’s body had felt like lead. Jen’s flat was the closest and she’d given Rio a key for emergencies, so she had let herself in and crashed on the couch, kicking her boots off haphazardly before she’d passed out.

Jen was less than pleased to find her there in the morning, and had settled on waking her up with a sharp kick to the ribs.

“What the hell?” Rio groaned, rolling over and squeezing her eyes shut against the sunlight pouring through the window.

“At least text me when you’re staying over,” Jen snapped, her arms crossed as she stood over Rio. “I thought you were an intruder and almost killed you.”

Rio peeked up at her from beneath a messy tangle of hair, grimacing. “Good morning to you too, Jennifer.”

“You look like shit,” Jen informed her, entirely unsympathetic.

“Yeah, well,” she sighed, rubbing her eyes and forcing herself to sit up, “being stuck in a car with Matt and Wade for an entire night does that to you.”

Jen’s eyebrows shot up. “Jesus, you voluntarily spent a night with them? If I was around Wade for more than ten minutes, I’d stab him.”

“Believe me, I wanted to,” Rio replied, her voice dry as she stretched. Her back popped loudly, and she winced. “Got anything to eat? Like toast or cereal or something?”

Jen sighed, muttering something under her breath about freeloaders as she walked into the kitchen. “Yes, but don’t finish everything like you did last time. You’re a guest, Rio, not a vacuum cleaner.”

“Noted,” Rio said, hauling herself off the couch and shuffling after her.

Jen tossed a box of cereal onto the counter and gestured vaguely toward the cupboard and fridge. “Bowls are where they always are. Milk’s in the fridge. Help yourself.”

“Thanks, Mom,” Rio said sarcastically, grabbing a bowl and pouring herself some cereal.

Jen leaned against the counter, watching her with a faint smirk as she sipped from her mug. “You don’t have anything stronger than coffee, do you?”

“It’s nine in the morning,” Jen said flatly.

“Exactly,” Rio replied, stuffing a spoonful of cereal into her mouth.

Who could blame her for wanting something to take the edge off, it had been a long fucking weekend.

_____

She woke up Monday morning feeling uneasy. It wasn’t that she was dreading the session – Rio Vidal didn’t get nervous – but the pit in her stomach was undeniable. The walk to Agatha’s office felt longer than usual, and Rio stuffed her hands in her coat pockets, fiddling with her keys. She’d gone back to Nicky’s grave the night before, and sure enough the letter and the glasses were gone.

When Rio had first left them there, she was convinced it was a brilliant idea. Now that Agatha had actually seen it though?

It made Rio’s stomach twist slightly. She’d been more open in that letter than she had been with Agatha ever before, and the idea of Agatha actually reading it made her feel uncomfortable - vulnerable, even.

Oh well, there was nothing she could do about it now. She already expected Agatha to be mad at her after she’d slipped up and seconds later ran out the room during their session last Thursday, and this would just be the icing on the cake.

She knew that she probably should have spent the weekend forming some sort of plan, making up some lie that would explain how she'd known about Nicky’s cause of death. Telling your therapist that you’d murdered someone wasn’t the smartest move she could make. But she hadn’t had much time to sit and think that weekend.

Now, as she stepped inside Agatha’s office, she could feel something was off. Agatha was already sat in her chair, hands folded loosely in her lap as she watched Rio shrug off her coat. Why was she so calm? Rio expected to have been met with anger after the way things had ended in their last session, but instead, Agatha was looking at her in a way that made every instinct in Rio’s body scream at her to turn around and walk back out through the door she’d just come in from.

She hung her coat on the back of the couch, deliberately taking her time to avoid sitting down. Agatha still hadn’t spoken, and Rio was all too aware of the silence. “Morning,” she muttered, voice rougher than she intended. She cleared her throat, hoping that she didn’t sound as confused as she felt.

“Good morning, Rio,” Agatha replied, her tone smooth. Too smooth.

Rio hesitated for half a second before sitting down on the coach, trying her best to appear comfortable and in control. “Um, are you… okay?”

Agatha smiled at her, which was more terrifying than any glare she could have given. “Of course, why wouldn’t I be?”

Rio shifted. “No reason.”

The silence stretched uncomfortably. It felt like Agatha was waiting for something – for what exactly, Rio had no idea. “Uh, did you have a good weekend?”

“Yes, thank you, my weekend was fine.” Agatha waved her hand, her tone light. “How about yours? Did you get up to anything fun?”

If ‘fun’ described loosing her mind being stuck in close proximity with two assholes for hours on end, then yeah, maybe she did have fun.

“It was okay,” She said carefully. “Spent most of looking after Lilia. She wasn’t feeling great so, you know, family stuff.”

The gleam in Agatha’s eye made Rio feel like she’d just walked straight into a trap. Agatha leaned forward, looking at her like she was a fresh kill. “That's very interesting, Rio,” She said, her tone so sickeningly sweet that it made Rio feel nauseous. “Because I had a lovely little chat with Lilia yesterday, and she told me you haven’t been to visit her in weeks.”

Shit.

Rio stiffened, her mind racing for a way to backpedal. “Wait, how the fuck did you talk to Lilia?” she demanded, voice sharp.

Agatha had no right to start having private conversations with Lilia. Invading her private life in sessions, well, that was her job after all. Invading her private life outside of sessions, however? That felt like a big leap out of line.

Rio saw a flicker of hesitation before Agatha responded. “That’s not important,” she said, voice firm. “What is important is that it’s not okay to lie to me.”

“I wasn’t lying,” Rio muttered, crossing her arms over her chest defensively and pouting slightly.

Agatha brow arched. “Really? So what, Lilia has short term memory loss? Maybe an identical twin sister? Oh, maybe she was being brainwashed by aliens? Any of those sounds plausible to you?”

Rio glared at her, but kept her mouth shut.

“Why didn't you feel like you could tell me what was really going on?” Agatha pressed, her tone softening slightly.

Rio shrugged, tightening her jaw. “Don’t know.”

Agatha held up her hands as if she was giving Rio the stage. “Tell me, Rio, what is the truth?”

Rio sighed - she’d walked right into that trap, and it’s not like she could lie herself out of it. The Lilia lie hadn’t worked, she should’ve known it was bound to catch up with her sooner or later. But the truth felt like too much, she couldn’t very well tell it, could she?

She searched for another lie, a more plausible one. A lie Agatha couldn’t possibly detect. “I-” Rio paused for way too long. She squirmed in her seat, trying to look confident although she felt nothing of the sort under the pressure of Agatha’s searing eyes.

“I was sick,” Rio forced between her teeth. “I was… embarrassed,” she said, her tone softer now, trying to appear insecure. “So I didn’t tell you, I told you it was Lilia because I didn’t want you to force me to go home because I wanted to see you Thursday.”

Agatha smiled sharply, although it didn’t reach her eyes. “Plausible lie,” She nodded, “but that doesn’t explain to me where you went after the session Thursday.”

“I felt bad after… what I said, and I felt like I should leave you alone. But I knew if I said that you’d force me to stay – that one I did to protect you.”

Rio feigned a smile, feeling proud of herself. Not only had she come up with a good lie, but she also managed to come up with one where she could deflect it onto Agatha and add empathy to it – God, she really needed to pat herself on the shoulder for that one.

Agatha’s smile faded, her expression turning sharp and pressing. “No.”

“No?”

“No.” Agatha shook her head, placing down the notebook on the table. “It's a good lie, Rio – but I need you to tell me the truth. Go ahead, one more chance. Don’t lie to me again.”

Rio squirmed in her seat, playing with the edge of her sweater. “I’m not lying to you,” She muttered, hoping it would come out as sincere.

Agatha signed, her expression darkening. “Well, that is a shame. If you don’t feel able to be honest with me, then maybe we should find you a new therapist?”

“What?” Rio snapped, her voice rising. “You can’t do that.”

“I assure you, Rio, I very much can do that. And I will, unless you decide to cut the crap and start telling me the truth.”

Rio’s jaw dropped. The tone Agatha had taken with her was underlined with threat, and although Rio found it undeniably hot, she also knew that Agatha wasn’t kidding. How was it fair that Agatha got to pull the ‘I’m in a charge so I have the power’ card when Rio was the one who had put her ass on the line taking out the person Agatha probably hated most in the world? Talk about unfairness.

“Where were you last week, Rio?” Agatha’s gaze was unrelenting.

“Oh my god, you’re so fucking annoying!” Rio threw her hands in the air, her voice laced with exasperation. “You want to know why I missed our session? I had to kill someone. Happy now?” She slouched back into the couch, muttering under her breath like a child throwing a tantrum.

Agatha blinked, looking at her in stunned silence. “Excuse me?”

Rio rolled her eyes. Was Agatha going deaf now as well? “I said I killed someone, okay! Are you going to lecture me about lying, or can we move on?”

The room went quiet. Agatha didn’t speak, didn’t blink. She just stared at Rio with a mixture of confusion and shock, and something else Rio couldn’t quite place.

Agatha’s expression didn’t change at first – she just blinked, then tilted her head slightly, like she hadn’t heard right. Then her face hardened, her jaw setting. “Alright,” she said sharply, standing up and yanking her phone from the desk. “I’m calling your probation officer.”

“What the- no! I’m not lying!” Rio blurted, sitting up straight, her pulse suddenly hammering.

Agatha froze, her thumb hovering over the screen, turning around and locking her piercing gaze onto Rio. “You’re serious?”

“Yes, I’m fucking serious!” Rio snapped, her voice rising defensively. “Did you not see my letter?”

Agatha stared at her, her phone still clutched tightly in her hand. “I did see your letter,” She said carefully, moving to sit down again, her voice slow like she was testing the waters. “But I didn’t think it meant you had killed someone.”

Rio rolled her eyes, throwing her hands in the air. “Well, that sounds like a you problem. I think I was pretty clear about it.”

Agatha let out a sharp laugh, one that carried no humor. “Clear? Clear ?!” she repeated, her voice cracking with incredulity. “So you’re telling me – no, let me get this straight – you actually killed someone?!”

“Yes,” Rio said, her tone flat, like she was stating the obvious.

Agatha gaped at her, her composure breaking in real time. “What the fuck, Rio. What the actual fuck?”

Would it have killed Agatha to just say ‘thank you’? Jesus.

“It’s not that big of a deal,” Rio muttered, averting her gaze.

“Not that big of a- are you hearing yourself right now?” Agatha’s voice rose. “You killed someone, Rio! Again! That's what – two people now? That’s the literal definition of a big deal!”

Rio resisted the urge to laugh. Two? God, imagine if she has only killed two, how pathetic would that be. She’d be hanging her head in shame. But Agatha couldn’t know that, so instead she fixed her with her best ‘I feel terrible about this’ expression and just nodded solemnly.

“Yes, I’ve had a lot of sleepless nights about it,” She tried, tasting the lie bitterly on her tongue. She forced herself to keep a straight face.

“Why?” Agatha demanded, her voice laced with rage. “You just got out of prison and now you’ve killed another person?”

“I did it for you!” Rio felt her voice crack, anger bubbling inside of her. She tried to see the situation from Agatha’s point of view, really tried. But it didn’t make any sense – Agatha should be thanking her, no?

“You did-” Agatha let out a humorless laugh. “You’re telling me you killed someone for me?”

“Yes!” Rio sighed, exasperated.

“Now,” Agatha held up her hand, as if to physically stop her from continuing. “What do you mean by that?”

“I…” Rio didn’t quite know how to finish that sentence. The reaction her confession had gotten out of Agatha felt completely uncalled for – she already fucking told her in the letter. This shouldn’t even be anything new.

“‘I what?” Agatha pressed, her gaze serious as it bore itself into Rio.

“Fucking hell,” Rio muttered under her breath. “I fucking killed the guy responsible for Nicky’s death. Is that a good enough reason for you?”

“You-” Agatha paused, her entire body freezing.

Rio felt the room fall silent, the only noticeable sound being Agatha’s heavy breathing. She kept her eyes locked on Agatha, watching the staggering rise and fall of her chest, the way her eyes narrowed as she looked away, eyes focused on something beyond Rio’s shoulder.

“You killed-” Agatha’s voice cracked. “Nicky’s killer?”

Rio could hear the vulnerability, the silent sadness slipping between the cracks of Agatha’s facade.

“Yep,” She said, calmly.

“Nicky’s killer-” Agatha continued, her eyes now completely unfocused. Rio wasn’t even sure Agatha was present anymore, her mind deep within repressed memories. “Rio, Nicky’s killer was never found.”

Rio felt a shiver run down her spine when Agatha’s eyes caught hers once again, suddenly completely present and weighing her down immensely.

“Well,” Rio said, throwing her hands up in surrender. “Clearly they didn’t do a very good job looking because I found him. Or at least found the guy responsible.”

“You found what the police couldn’t?” Agatha asked weakly, disbelief plastered on her face. “The investigation went on for three years, Rio. Three years!”

“Yes,” Rio shrugged. “I found him. I killed him, and I made sure it hurt, too.”

Agatha flinched, her eyes widening. “What was his name?” Her voice was low, like every word hurt.

“Castillo was his street name, but-”

“Street name?” Agatha interrupted.

“Yes?” Rio said, like it was a matter of fact – but based on Agatha's startled expression, it wasn’t.

Rio could hear the sob escaping Agatha’s throat before she straightened herself. “There was a… gang involved?”

Rio nodded slowly, her eyes searching Agatha’s face.

“How do you know this?” Agatha said accusingly.

It threw Rio off. Was Agatha suddenly blaming her? “I know a few people.”

“You know a few people?” Agatha’s eyes narrowed. “And those people just happen to be at fault for my son’s death?”

“Not the people I know!” Rio practically yelled. “They knew of the gang responsible, so they helped me.”

“Gang responsible?” Agatha’s facade fell, the devastation behind the cracks appearing. “How? Why? When?”

Rio felt her throat tightening. The woman in front of her was no longer the fierce, unrelenting psychiatrist she thought she knew, but a woman mourning the loss of her son. Someone who’s entire world had come crashing down in less than a second.

“He wasn’t-” Rio didn’t quite know how to word it. “The shot wasn’t meant for him. He wasn’t a target. He was just in the wrong place at the wrong time and-”

Rio was interrupted by a sob escaping Agatha as she silenced it by pressing her palm to her lips, shaking slightly as she looked down at the floor. Honestly, Rio didn’t know how to… comfort her. It has never really been her strong suit. She’d been so sure when they first started talking about it that none of this was news to Agatha, that she already knew the circumstances her son had died in.

How wrong she’d been.

Agatha

Agatha felt as if the floor was caving in under her, swallowing her whole and dragging her down.

If Rio was telling the truth – which she now was fairly certain of – however scary that was, it also meant that she had answers. She knew more than Agatha had ever known about that night.

It was so deeply unfair. That this random woman, who meant nothing to Nicky, knew so much, and Agatha was just there… left in the darkness, not knowing if it was some random lunatic, a shot gone wrong, a targeted assassination-

She knew nothing. And Rio, sitting right before her, knew everything.

A gang? Really? Out of everything she’d ever dared to even think about… that rarely made the list.

“And you… killed him? This man?”

“Yes,” Rio said proudly. Too proudly.

Agatha had no idea on how to approach this situation anymore. She couldn’t shake the earth shattering thought of Nicky’s death, the memories of that night coming flooding back. But she also had a client, right in front of her, previously convicted for murder – who had just admitted to killing someone else.

And this one was not involuntary.

The right thing – her duty in this job, for god’s sake – was to turn her in. To put her back in prison where she rightfully belonged. But something within Agatha’s stomach churned at the thought. Did Rio deserve it?

The ethics behind it, the sheer motivation causing Rio’s actions, made Agatha believe otherwise.

Hell, Agatha was certain that if she were in Rio’s position – knowing Nicky’s killer, seeing him with her bare eyes, she would’ve done the exact same. She would smile as that bastard’s life ran out of his eyes, as his body became limp.

But she was Rio’s psychiatrist for fucks same. Wasn’t she supposed to condemn this?

“How did it make you feel?” Agatha asked finally, her voice low but steady.

Rio leaned back, her head tilting slightly as though considering the question. “It felt fine,” she said with a casual shrug, her tone as light as if she were commenting on the weather.

Agatha frowned, her gaze sharpening. There was something about the way Rio said it, something off. That look in her eyes – the faintest glint of something unspoken. Satisfaction? Thrill? It wasn’t remorse, that much was clear.

“Fine?” Agatha asked, her voice strained. She leaned forward, her elbows on her knees, trying to pin Rio down with her stare. “That’s all you’ve got?”

Rio’s lips curled into a faint smirk, but she didn’t say anything. The silence was deafening, and for a moment, Agatha swore the temperature in the room dropped.

Agatha’s jaw tightened. “Rio, this isn’t normal. You don’t think there’s something wrong with how… detached you are about this?”

Rio shifted, her expression unreadable. “Why should I feel anything else? He hurt Nicky. He deserved it.” She met Agatha’s gaze, and that glint was back, sharper this time.

Agatha swallowed hard, weighing her next words. “You understand I could turn you in for this, right?”

Something flickered in Rio’s eyes; surprise, then amusement. She leaned back in her seat, arms crossing over her chest. “You won’t.”

Agatha frowned. “You sound awfully sure of that.”

“Because you get it.” Rio’s tone was casual, but her gaze was sharp, almost daring. “ I know you do. You’d have done the same. Hell, you probably wish it had been you.”

Agatha froze, her stomach twisting uncomfortably because – God help her – Rio wasn’t wrong.

Agatha studied her, unsure whether she should feel horrified, impressed, or something in between. “And you seriously don’t feel guilty?”

“Should I?” Rio asked, tilting her head. “He hurt Nicky. I don’t regret it. Would you?”

The words hit Agatha like a punch to the gut. Would she? The truth simmered beneath her skin, burning hotter with each second of silence. Would that make her just as bad? Maybe. But after everything she and Nicky had been through, how could she not want justice? How could she not want vengeance?

“How did you do it?” Agatha asked, the blunt question visibly throwing Rio off guard.

“I- well,” Rio paused, searching Agatha’s face, probably for some type of trap. The question had been out of the blue, yes, but Agatha wanted – no needed – to know how the bastard went out.

Maybe it could bring her a sense of relief. She thought back to Rio’s letter.

“You know, sometimes it brings me comfort to know that karma is real. That whoever hurt me in the past, will be hurt the same amount, if not even more.”

When reading it, Agatha hadn’t quite understood the meaning. Yes , karma was a nice concept, and Agatha sure delighted in it sometimes. But this feeling spreading across her chest showed that the thought of vengeance brought her true happiness, even for just a second.

“I stabbed him,” Rio finished, her voice eerily stable despite the harsh words.

“Shame,” Agatha muttered, mostly to herself before she looked up at Rio, dead in the eyes. A smile crept up on her face. “You should’ve strangled him, too.”

_____

After the session, Agatha had called Wanda, demanding them to meet. She didn’t care when or how, she just needed to talk. Wanda had sounded worried over the phone. Even if Agatha tried to keep calm, the quiver in her voice undoubtedly exposed her.

“What is going on?” Wanda asked the second she stepped inside Agatha’s apartment later that night.

“Wanda, always a delight,” Agatha said sarcastically, helping her to hang up her coat. “Can’t I just ask to hang out because I want your company?”

“You called me after your session with Rio, completely shaken. Don’t play around, tell me what the fuck happened.” Wanda demanded as she stepped into Agatha’s apartment, sitting down by her kitchen table and nodding for Agatha to do the same.

Agatha scoffed, did this woman think she owned the place?

Nevertheless, Agatha sat down opposite Wanda, playing with the edge of her sweater. “Whatever I tell you has to stay between us.”

Wanda rolled her eyes, “I would never-”

“I’m serious,” Agatha interrupted. “Do not tell anyone what I’m about to tell you.”

“Who would I even-”

“Rio killed someone,” Agatha interrupted once again, having to get the sentence out before she would regret even calling Wanda over.

Wanda’s eyes went wide. “She killed someone?” She whispered as if someone was listening.

Agatha had to resist the urge to roll her eyes.

“Yes,” She sighed. “What do I do? Turn her in? Help her through it?”

“Of course you’re turning her in!” Disbelief was plastered on Wanda’s face. “You have a convicted felon confessing murder during her probation, it’s not even a question.”

“But-” Agatha stopped herself from continuing. It was clear in her head, but how could she possibly convey her thoughts?

“But what? But what, Agatha?” Wanda was practically yelling now. “Don’t tell me you’ve got a fucking crush on her or something, because I’m telling you there’s nothing that could justify it.”

“She killed the guy responsible for Nicky’s death,” Agatha explained in one breath, trying to distance herself from the words so they would hurt less.

“The guy responsible-” Wanda paused, her gaze turning softer. “You told me Nicky was sick.”

“I lied,” Agatha said through her teeth. She really couldn’t have this conversation again. “Nicky was shot, by whom I don’t know – but Rio did, and she killed him.”

“Shot?” Wanda shouted before calming herself. “You never told me that.”

Agatha stiffened, “Don’t take it personally, I never tell anyone that.”

“Agatha,” Wanda said, voice sincere. She reached out her hand, leaving it open on the table in invitation. “You shouldn’t have carried all that baggage alone. And I’m sorry if you felt like you couldn’t share that with me.”

Agatha scoffed. It was really, really hard to keep her composure at that moment. The way Wanda looked with her with so much care – her comforting palm within reach. It physically hurt her stomach, she could feel it knotting.

“Can we focus on the conversation at hand?” She said sharply instead, forcing away the tears that threatened to spill.

“Oh right,” Wanda straightened up, retracting her hand. “Rio…”

“Yes,” Agatha sighed. “You knew her in prison, so I’m just wondering-”

“Hold on,” Wanda interrupted. “If she really killed the guy responsible for Nicky’s death, that means she fucking killed someone for you.”

“I know,” Agatha hissed, dragging her fingers through her hair. “I fucking know, okay? I’ve been freaking out all day.”

“God, she’s either psycho or in love with you,” Wanda said, chuckling slightly to herself. “Although maybe for her that might be the same thing.”

Agatha rolled her eyes, “I don’t know her motivations, they don’t make any sense. But please , I called you over because I need your help with this, not because I need you teasing me endlessly about it.”

“Okay,” Wanda said warily, dragging out the word for too long.

“I just want to know if you- I know this is highly unprofessional to ask, but you’ve known her longer than me. Does it… make sense that she would kill someone on purpose? Like did you see any signs of it?”

Wanda paused for a second, staring down at the table. “I mean she’s always been pretty violent and… threatened to get physical sometimes, when I was annoying her,” her voice was weak, like the memory pained her.

Agatha remained quiet. She regretted not bringing her notebook to the table. Fuck.

“And… there was this rumor going around in the prison.”

“What rumor?” Agatha asked, suddenly very intrigued.

“Well, I’m not saying it’s true. In that place, rumors spread quickly, and a lot of them weren’t true. There was one rumor about me that I was some kind of witch living on a farm doing dark magic to try and hunt children – crazy right?”

“Wanda,” Agatha sighed. “I’m sure there were some hilarious rumors about you, but can we please focus.”

“Right,” Wanda snapped back to reality, her expression serious. “There was this one rumor – and again I’m not saying it’s true – that Rio was convicted for the wrong thing.”

“How so?” Agatha tried to keep her voice calm as her mind kept spiraling.

“That,” Wanda swallowed, “she didn’t just kill one person, but a lot. I think the rumor was seven.”

“Seven?” Agatha felt her words get stuck in her throat. “What do you mean? Like she’s some kind of serial killer?”

“I didn’t believe the rumors, at the time. People told them all the time to have some kind of entertainment.” Wanda paused, looking Agatha deeply in the eyes. “But, after what you just told me – if Rio did actually kill a guy – I’m not so sure those were just rumors, anymore.”

“You’re telling me you think Rio killed seven people? Not just one? Not just the one involuntary?”

“That’s the thing too. Was it involuntary? You just told me she killed someone for you, that isn’t normal, Agatha. That’s not what people do.”

The words lingered in the air. There was some truth beneath them – if Rio could kill someone, so cold heartedly, it was certainly something to consider. Seven people, however, felt like a reach.

Rio might be an annoying, time consuming and overly confident person. But she wasn’t a serial killer.

Right?

Chapter 14

Chapter Notes

happy new year!!

Rio

The prison was as depressingly grey and lifeless as Rio remembered it to be. She tried not to wrinkle her nose as she leaned against the check in counter, waiting for the guard to finish with her visitor paperwork. She tapped her fingers on the surface impatiently, chewing on her bottom lip. The sooner she was out of that place, the better.

The guard – Foley, if she remembered correctly – scanned her up and down. “Back so soon Vidal?” he grunted, tone somewhere between disdain and begrudging familiarity.

Rio smirked. “Why? Did you miss me? I’m touched.”

Foley rolled her eyes, sliding a visitors badge under the glass. “Just don’t cause any trouble, got it?”

“Trouble? Me?” Rio slipped the badge around her neck and grinned. “I'm a reformed citizen now, Foley. Ask my probation officer.”

She breezed past him, ignoring the daggers he was staring into the back of her head. The guards here were far too easy to wind up, and in Rio’s opinion, it would be a waste for her not to take the opportunity.

She was directed onto the search area – standard procedure. She hadn’t been expecting anything less, but the familiarity of it tugged at her nerves all the same.

After being waved through, she stepped into the visitor’s area, the sound of the heavy door shutting behind her echoing ominously. The room was bleak and uninviting, just as she remembered. Pale, peeling walls. Harsh fluorescent lighting that made everyone look sickly. Even the smell – something sterile and metallic, with a hint of sweat – was the same.

Home sweet home.

It felt like the very walls carried memories, and she could recall like it was only yesterday how she’d been the one stuck here, with Alice, Jen or Lilia across from her.

She scanned the room. Seated at one of the tables near the far end, legs crossed as if she owned the place, was Hela.

Rio had almost forgotten how magnetic Hela could be. Even in her orange prison jumpsuit, handcuffed with her hair pulled back and dark circles under her eyes, she exuded a kind of effortless dominance that made people turn their heads. Rio’s lips twitched upward into a smirk.

“Well, well,” Hela drawled as Rio approached, sliding into the seat across from her. “If it isn’t Rio Vidal. Freedom suits you.”

Rio leaned back in her chair, arms crossed loosely. “Miss me?”

“Always,” Hela said with a smirk. Her sharp green eyes swept over Rio, like she was cataloging every change since the last time they’d seen each other. “No one else in this place is nearly as interesting.”

She studied Hela with her eyes. She looked almost menacingly the same, not that Rio had expected anything different – she’d only been out for about two months. But something about it, the way her black hair sat in a low bun, the way her eyes never stopped carrying that piercing look, it was a reminder of how slowly time moved in a place like this.

“You know, I’m pleasantly surprised that you actually kept your promise and came back to visit.” Hela leaned forward slightly. “If I were you, I’d be as far away from this place as possible by now.”

Rio snorted. “Well, loyalty never really was your forte.”

Hela grinned at her, all teeth. “Buy a lady a drink first.”

Rio rolled her eyes, but she couldn’t suppress the twitch of a smirk. Her and Hela’s relationship had never been anything more than practical – a means to an end. Sex was just sex, no strings attached. Sometimes she missed how easy that had felt.

“So, how’s this place doing without me?”

“It’s incredibly boring,” Hela said, waving her cuffed hand dismissively. “Without you I’ve got no one to keep me entertained. It’s tragic really.”

Rio scoffed. “You’ll survive. You always do.”

Hela leaned back, sighing dramatically. “Anyways, how’s freedom treating you? Is it everything you hoped for and more?”

It was going terribly, if she was honest, but she wasn’t about to give Hela the satisfaction.

She shrugged. “It’s good. Complicated, but good. Mostly.”

“Who are you trying to convince, me or yourself?” Hela teased.

“I have a situation,” Rio signed, not quite knowing how to start.

“Oh,” Hela’s eyebrows shot up. “And you want my help with it?”

“Don’t let it go to your head,” Rio muttered. The undeniable presence of the guards, standing along the walls of the lit up room, loomed over her as a restraint. She had to tread lightly.

“So, what’s the situation?” Hela asked, leaning forward.

Rio sighed, crossing her arms and looking up into the ceiling, unsure of what to say or how. “My therapist,” she cleared her throat, “well, psychiatrist – but that’s neither here nor there – she… Well, we had sex.”

“Holy shit,” Hela hissed, her eyes widening, “You bagged your therapist? Now that’s some great work. I’d pat you on the shoulder but-” she gestured to her hands, still in handcuffs.

Rio shrugged, “That’s not the situation, though.”

“You’ve been out for what, two months, and you’re already in deep shit?” Hela snickered, “Didn’t expect anything less but-”

“I killed someone,” Rio whispered, cutting Hela off – when Hela got started, she could make sarcastic jabs forever. She needed this conversation over and done with, to get out of this place as soon as possible.

A look of surprise flashed in Hela’s eyes, quickly replaced by a grin as she studied Rio from across the table. “Congratulations, would you like a round of applause?” Hela mused, “Now do you miss me that much, Vidal? That you need to get yourself locked up with me again?”

Rio scoffed, “I’d rather not be in this place again.”

“Then why the hell would you-”

“He was responsible for Agat- my therapist’s son’s death.” Rio explained, like that was all the context Hela needed.

“Okay,” Hela said carefully, dragging out the word for longer than necessary. “Do you really care that much about your shrink?”

“It’s not about her,” Rio hissed. “It’s about the son.”

Hela narrowed her eyes in thought, “The son? What, you knew him?”

“Kind of,” Rio shrugged. “I ran into him on the streets for a period of time. There was a whole group of them, kids who just wanted community I think. I kept trying to get them to clean their shit up.”

“And they listened?”

“Rarely,” Rio shrugged. “But that’s not important. What’s important is that I don’t know if I should tell her about it or not.”

“Rio,” Hela warned, the glint in her eye replaced by something darker. “Please tell me you’re not stupid enough to actually tell your shrink that you murdered someone while on probation.”

“I’ve already told her that,” Rio said nonchalantly.

“You did what?”

“I told her. She threatened to turn me in, but I knew she wouldn’t – I took a calculated risk.”

“Alright,” Hela held up her hands in surrender, “You’ve gotten your shrink to fall head over heels in love with you. Gotta give credit where credit’s due.”

Rio glared at her, “So, should I tell her or not?”

Hela’s eyebrows shot up in confusion. “What else could you possibly tell her?”

“Why I knew her son.”

_____

On the way back from the Prison, Rio decided to stop off at Lilia’s. After parking her car out front, she slid her key into the lock, the familiar click and creak of the old door almost comforting in its predictability. As she stepped inside, the rich aroma of garlic, onions, and something slow-roasting wrapped around her. Lilia’s house has been the longest place she had ever stayed in, and she still considered it home.

Lilia could drive her up the wall, but this house, this woman – this was safety.

She kicked the door shut behind her and dropped her bag onto the floor, boots scraping against the floor as she stepped further in, “Hey, it’s me!” she called, shrugging her coat off.

From the kitchen came the clatter of pots and pans, followed by Lilia’s sharp voice. “Rio?”

Lilia appeared in the doorway, wiping her hands on a towel, her face already set in that mix of concern and exasperation Rio knew so well. Her sharp eyes scanned Rio from head to toe, taking in every detail like she could see right through her.

“Where’ve you been? It’s been weeks.”

Rio shrugged as she hung her coat over the banister. “Around.”

Lilia’s brow furrowed, and she stepped closer, the towel still in one hand. “Don’t ‘around’ me, Rio. You don’t call, you don’t text, and now you show up looking like you haven’t slept in days. What’s going on?”

“I’ve been busy,” Rio sighed.

“Too busy to check in?” Lilia asked, her voice quieter now, but no less firm. “You know how I worry about you.”

Lilia’s care made something in her chest twist. The woman was the closest thing she had to a mother – she had dealt with more of Rio’s bullshit than anyone and had never once walked away. Rio didn’t want to cause her any worry. “I’m fine. Seriously.”

Lilia didn’t look convinced. She reached out and gently brushed Rio’s cheek with her hand, the gesture so natural it caught Rio off guard. She leaned into the touch, allowing herself the comfort.

Lilia patted her cheek softly before dropping her hand. “Alright, fine. But at least take your shoes off.”

Rio blinked. “What?”

“Your shoes,” Lilia repeated, nodding toward them. “Off. By the door.”

Rio rolled her eyes. “You’ve got to be kidding me.”

“Do I look like I’m kidding?” Lilia shot back, one brow arched.

Rio huffed, bending down to untie her boots. “I’m not fifteen anymore, you know.”

“And this is still my house,” Lilia countered, folding her arms.

Pulling off her boots with exaggerated effort, Rio shoved them toward the corner.

“Neatly,” Lilia said, her tone sharp but her eyes warm.

“I swear you live to torment me,” Rio muttered under her breath, crouching down to adjust the boots so they lined up perfectly.

Lilia’s smirk softened into something gentler as she reached out to ruffle Rio’s hair. “I missed you too.”

Rio batted her hand away, grumbling, “You’re insufferable.”

“And you’re dramatic,” Lilia teased, brushing past her back into the kitchen. Over her shoulder, she added, “You hungry? Dinner will be ready in 10.”

Rio followed the sound of clattering pots into the kitchen, the warmth of the oven hitting her. Lilia stood at the counter, chopping a handful of cilantro. The kitchen was the same as always – crowded but cozy, with mismatched tea towels hanging over the oven handle and a dozen little spice jars lined up on the windowsill.

Rio leaned against the doorway, crossing her arms. “So, why’d you talk to Agatha?”

Lilia paused mid-chop and turned her head slightly, her brow furrowing in confusion. “Who’s Agatha?”

“My therapist,” Rio said, shifting her weight.

“Oh,” Lilia said, her expression clearing. “Her. She stopped by a few days ago.”

Rio straightened, her stomach twisting. “She what?”

Lilia turned fully, wiping her hands on a towel. “Said she needed to talk to you about something. She looked like she’d been up half the night.”

“And you just… let her in?” Rio asked, trying to keep her voice steady.

Lilia’s dark eyes softened as she studied Rio, and her response was calm, almost gentle. “Of course I let her in. What was I supposed to do, Rio? Turn her away?”

“Yes!” Rio snapped, throwing her hands up. “She’s my therapist! You don’t just-”

“Rio.” Lilia’s voice was steady, but there was a familiar, maternal edge to it. “She seemed concerned about you. I wasn’t going to shut the door in her face. That would be rude.”

“That’s not the point,” Rio muttered, pacing a few steps. “Why would you even let her in?”

Lilia sighed, stepping closer. Her hand came up, gentle but firm, to brush a stray strand of hair from Rio’s face. “Because she’s worried about you,” she said softly. “And maybe because I’m worried too.”

Rio sighed, looking at Lilia’s earnest expression. “Why does everyone keep saying that? I’m fine,” she muttered, looking away.

She wasn’t a child, she didn’t need people fussing over here.

“You don’t look fine.” Lilia’s voice softened further, and her eyes searched Rio’s face. “When’s the last time you had a proper meal? Or slept more than a few hours?”

Rio shrugged. “I’ve been busy.”

Lilia let out a long breath, stepping back toward the counter. “You’re always busy,” she said, shaking her head. “And you’re always too stubborn to ask for help when you need it.” She turned back to the stove, lifting the lid of a bubbling pot. The steam rose, filling the air with a rich, savory scent.

Rio shifted on her feet, settling herself down on a chair by the table. “She didn’t… she didn’t say anything weird, did she?”

Lilia chuckled softly as she stirred the pot. “Weird? No. But let me tell you, Rio, it’s not normal for your therapist to come knocking on my door trying to find you. That’s not how therapy works, you know.”

“It’s complicated,” Rio muttered.

Lilia shot her a sharp but affectionate look. “Everything with you is complicated. What have you gotten yourself into now?”

“Nothing I can’t handle,” Rio said quickly, hoping she sounded more confident than she actually felt.

Lilia placed a glass in front of her. “Are you drinking enough water?” she asked, her voice laced with that familiar maternal concern.

Jesus this woman never stopped.

Rio resisted the urge to scoff. She had much bigger things going on right now than worrying about her daily water intake. “Yeah, sure,” she muttered, but it didn’t sound convincing even to her own ears.

Lilia, of course, didn’t let it slide. “You sure? Because you’re looking a little dehydrated. And are you getting enough sunlight?” she asked, her tone turning slightly teasing.

Rio groaned and leaned back further. “It’s literally November,” she deadpanned, making a face. “The sun’s barely out anyway.”

Lilia didn’t budge, her gaze firm. “I’ll take that as a ‘no,’” she replied, her voice stern but tinged with that hint of affection that Rio knew so well.

The silence between them stretched on, but it wasn’t awkward. Rio’s mind wandered, pulling at strings of thoughts she wasn’t ready to untangle just yet. How was it that her life was more of a mess out of prison than it was in it? Her fingers drummed absently on the edge of her glass, and she found herself subconsciously frowning, a deep crease forming between her brows. She didn’t even realize she was doing it until she heard a soft sigh.

Lilia put down the knife she’d been holding, the sound of it clinking against the counter louder than it should’ve been. She turned to face Rio, arms crossed, hands on her hips. There was that look, the one that told Rio that whatever she was trying to hide wasn’t staying hidden for long.

“I know that look,” Lilia said, her voice warm but firm.

Rio looked up at her, feigning innocence. “What look?”

Lilia’s gaze narrowed, and she stepped toward the table, her eyes never leaving Rio’s. “Don’t pretend you don’t know what I mean. What’s going on?”

Rio groaned, running a hand over her face as if trying to physically wipe away the weight that had settled on her shoulders. “Can I have some juice first, please?” she asked, stalling for time.

Lilia raised an eyebrow but nodded. “Fine. You’re lucky you’ve got a soft spot with me.” She moved to the fridge and pulled out a carton of cranberry juice, Rio’s favorite, pouring her a glass and setting it down in front of her with a small smile.

Rio took the glass without a word, bringing it to her lips and taking a long sip. She could feel Lilia’s eyes still on her, burning a hole into the side of her head. She knew Lilia wouldn’t be overly thrilled at her choice of weekend activities, but lying to the woman never seemed to work out well for her.

She set the glass down and let out a heavy breath.Her shoulders stiffened, and her voice dropped as she murmured, "I killed Castillo."

Lilia let out a long, audible breath, the sound heavy with frustration. Rio winced.

Without saying a word, Lilia put the knife down on the counter and slowly turned to face Rio, who was doing her best to look anywhere but at her.

"Rio," Lilia's voice broke through the tension, firm but laced with concern, "Look at me."

Rio hesitated, her gaze glued to the floor for a moment before she finally gave in and slowly met Lilia's eyes. The lines of worry were etched deeply into her features, mixed with an expression of care.

"You're not a vigilante," Lilia continued, her voice stern. "You can't just go around killing whoever you feel like."

Rio's lips curled into a tight line."But Castillo was an asshole," she muttered, the words coming out bitter, almost defensive.

Lilia shook her head, an exasperated sigh escaping her lips. "Yes, he was. But he's a powerful asshole, a big player in a gang. Taking out someone like him that high up is stupid and reckless and irresponsible. You could have gotten yourself killed, Rio."

Rio rolled her eyes, a soft scoff escaping her as she muttered under her breath. "Yeah, yeah. Stupid, reckless, irresponsible, blah blah blah. I've heard it all before."

Lilia’s wicked gaze snapped right to Rio as she glared at her sharply. “Would you like to repeat that?” she asked, her voice low but carrying a heavy weight.

Rio froze, heat crawling up her neck as she realized she had been caught. Her cheeks flushed, and she quickly looked away, rolling her eyes.

“Rio Vidal,” Lilia said sternly, her voice rising with the same authority it had when Rio was a teenager. “Don’t you dare roll your eyes at me.”

Rio groaned softly, the familiar feeling of being scolded creeping back into her bones. She slouched, arms crossing defensively across her chest, but the irritation in her eyes was quickly replaced by the quiet embarrassment she always felt when Lilia turned this intense on her.

Rio muttered a half-hearted “sorry” under her breath.

Even when she was being scolded – especially when she was being scolded – there was something comforting about Lilia’s presence. The way she handled things, steady and no- nonsense, was the closest thing Rio had ever had to stability.

Lilia let out a barely audible hum in response, then went right back to chopping. The rhythmic sound of the knife against the board filled the space between them, but Rio was still lost in thought. She barely registered when Lilia spoke again. “So, how’d you do it?”

Rio felt a smirk tug at her lips, dark satisfaction filling her. “I stabbed him,” she said nonchalantly. “A bunch of times. Right in his gut, then his chest. Real personal, you know? Felt good. He deserved it.”

Rio didn’t need to see Lilia’s face to know that she was smiling.“That’s my girl.”

Rio grinned wider, the satisfaction in her chest swelling at the words.

“Hang on,” Lilia said, sounding suddenly serious. “Don’t tell me this has something to do with that therapist of yours.”

Shit.

Rio’s stomach twisted. She opened her mouth, but no words came out at first. She let the silence stretch, and Lilia’s eyes narrowed as she waited for Rio to say something.

The seconds felt like hours before Rio finally muttered, “It’s… complicated.”

Lilia’s eyebrow twitched, and she groaned, rubbing her temple with the fingers of one hand. “Of course it is. Can you ever keep it simple?”

Rio just shrugged, letting her shoulders drop with an exaggerated sigh.

“Okay,” Lilia muttered, more to herself than to Rio, before pausing and letting out a resigned sigh. “Can you go set the table? I’ll finish up here, and we’ll talk about this over dinner.”

Rio groaned dramatically, leaning back in her chair and slumping a little. “Fine, fine. I’ll go set the damn table.” She stood up with exaggerated slowness, trying to buy a few more moments before the inevitable conversation came crashing down on her.
