She dragged her feet over to the cupboard, pulling out the plates with a little more force than necessary. The clatter of porcelain against wood echoed in the quiet kitchen, and she huffed.

Lilia set the food down in front of them, the faint sound of cutlery scraping against the surface filling the quiet kitchen. She glanced at Rio, raising an eyebrow as she took her seat. “You’re not a barn animal, Rio. Elbows off the table.”

Rio groaned but obeyed, sitting up straighter.She shoveled food onto her plate – not realizing how hungry she has been until now.

Lilia settled in, her expression shifting to one of quiet expectation as she took a bite herself. “Okay, talk,” she said, her voice light but giving Rio a look that left no room for argument.

Rio looked at her, the words sticking in her throat for a moment. “I killed him because he’s the one who’s responsible for Agatha’s son’s death,” she muttered, barely above a whisper, staring at the table. She could feel Lilia’s gaze on her, waiting for more, so she continued. “He’s the one who set it all up. If he hadn’t… if he hadn’t gotten involved, things would’ve turned out differently.”

Lilia leaned back in her chair, her arms crossing over her chest as she studied Rio. “And why do you care so much about this random kid’s death?” she asked, her voice incredulous. “It’s not like you knew him, right?”

Before Rio could respond, Lilia’s eyes sharpened, and she leaned forward slightly, lowering her voice. “Rio,” she said, her tone now filled with suspicion. “Please don’t tell me you’re hooking up with your therapist now.”

Rio froze, almost choking on her food as the words hung in the air. “What?” she asked, clearly caught off guard.

Lilia just raised an eyebrow, her gaze unwavering. “I’m serious, Rio. Please tell me you didn’t get involved with her.”

Rio’s face flushed slightly, though she tried to hide it by shrugging it off. “It was one time,” she muttered, not making eye contact.

Lilia sighed, rubbing the bridge of her nose. “I’m not even going to begin to explain to you how stupid that was,” she said, shaking her head. “I’m sure you already know. But just be careful, okay? Don’t let it mess with your head.”

Rio scowled, clearly irritated. “I’m always careful,” she said, her voice sharp, but there was a hint of defensiveness there that she couldn’t quite mask.

Lilia eyed her critically, her gaze softening slightly, but not enough to let Rio off the hook. “So what, you took out a gang leader because you’re trying to win Agatha over? Is that it?”

Rio blinked, her scowl deepening. “Ew, no, it’s not like that. I’m not trying to impress her or anything. God, stop trying to make everything sound like some soap opera.”

Lilia chuckled lightly, shaking her head. “Well, it’s hard not to, considering the drama that seems to follow you around.”

Rio stared at her plate, absently twirling a piece of pasta on her fork, her gaze distant. “Do you remember some of the kids I used to look out for on the streets?” she asked, her voice quiet.

Lilia’s brow furrowed for a moment as she thought back, the years gone by, but eventually, the memory clicked. “Yeah, I remember them. They practically worshipped you.”

Rio nodded slowly, but the weight of the memories seemed to press down on her. “Yeah. One of those kids was Agatha’s son. He got shot. It was some turf war thing gone wrong, and was caught in the crossfire. Wrong time, wrong place.”

Lilia’s face fell, a frown tugging at her lips as she took in the news. “That’s a real shame,” she murmured. “They were all good kids, just a little.. lost.”

Rio’s throat tightened, and she pushed the food around on her plate without really noticing. “Yeah, he was,” she said quietly, her voice thick with emotion. “That’s kind of the issue, though.”

Lilia raised an eyebrow, confused for a moment. “What do you mean?”

“Agatha… she had this image of him, like he was some kind of saint,” she said, her voice low. “And I know I should just tell her the truth, but I can’t. I don’t want to shatter that image she has of him. It’s all she’s holding onto. If I tell her what really happened, that he was… well she’s going to see him differently, and I-” Rio stopped, clenching her fist on the table as her words faltered. “I just don’t want to do that to her.”

Lilia’s gaze softened, but there was still a slight edge to her voice when she spoke. “Rio,” she said gently, “You can’t protect her from the truth forever. People aren’t saints. They’re human. And sometimes, they make choices that lead to… well, to things like this.” She shook her head, her expression a mixture of concern and understanding. “You have to be honest with her. It’s not just about what happened to him, it’s about what’s going to happen to her if you don’t.”

Rio looked up, meeting Lilia’s eyes, and for a moment, she saw nothing but the motherly concern she had known for years. “I know,” Rio muttered, a bitter laugh escaping her. “It’s just… It’s hard, Lilia. She’s been through enough. I don’t want to be the one to hurt her again.”

Lilia sighed deeply, running a hand through her hair. “I know you don’t, sweetheart,” she said, her voice softening. “But sometimes the only way to help someone is to be honest with them, even when it’s the hardest thing to do.”

_____

Rio pushed the door open to Agatha’s office. The familiar smell of coffee and that faint lavender-scented candle Agatha always burned greeted her, but it didn’t calm her nerves like it usually did.

Not that Rio ever got nervous. Nope. ‘Nervous’ wasn’t in her vocabulary. Except, maybe today it was. Just a little. Not that she’d admit it. Not even to herself.

She glanced over at Agatha, who looked up from her notebook and offered a small, polite smile. The tension in the air was subtle but present, hanging like smoke from a fire they’d both been ignoring. Agatha’s face didn’t betray much, but Rio could feel the shift in her. She was more cautious now, guarded in a way that pricked at Rio’s ego and set her teeth on edge.

“Morning,” Rio said, forcing her usual drawl as she sauntered to her seat. She slouched into the couch, her legs spread just a bit too wide, as if she were trying to take up as much space as possible.

She needed to get a grip.

“Morning, Rio,” Agatha replied, her tone carefully even. She gestured to the coffee table between them. “There’s coffee, if you want some.”

Rio’s eyes flicked to the steaming mug. Coffee had never failed her before, but today the idea of drinking it made her stomach twist. Her throat felt dry. “I’ll take water instead,” she said, her voice casual, but the words still felt wrong coming out of her mouth.

Agatha paused for half a second, her eyebrow arching slightly. She didn’t say anything, but the look was enough. Rio shifted, forcing herself to pull it together.

“Water it is,” Agatha said, standing to grab a bottle from the side. She handed it to Rio without another word and settled back into her seat, flipping to a fresh page in her notebook.

Rio twisted the cap off the bottle, taking a long swig to buy herself a moment. Her fingers tapped a restless rhythm on the plastic. She was good at hiding her emotions, a damn expert at it, but even she felt like she was treading water in quicksand right now.

Agatha crossed one leg over the other, her pen poised. “I was thinking we could talk about the time you spent on the streets. When you were running away.”

Internally, Rio groaned so hard she was surprised it didn’t echo. She tilted her head back slightly, staring at the ceiling. “Oh, yay,” she muttered. “A trip down memory lane.”

“I know it’s not easy to talk about,” Agatha said gently. “But it’s important to understand how those experiences shaped you.”

Rio opened her mouth to refuse, but she stopped herself. As much as that was a topic she really didn’t feel like getting into today, it would be the perfect segue into talking about him. About Nicky. She clicked her tongue. “Sure. Why not.”

Agatha blinked, clearly surprised by Rio’s lack of pushback. Her pen stilled against the paper for a moment before she nodded. “Alright. Let’s start at the beginning. How old were you when you first ran away?”

Rio rolled the bottle between her hands, watching the condensation drip down the sides. “Eleven,” she said.

“And what led you to that decision?”

“Didn’t feel like I had a choice,” Rio said, her voice clipped. “The group home was hell. Seemed safer out there.”

Agatha nodded, writing something down and looking back at her expectantly.

Rio really hated that fucking notebook.

She let out a long breath, cringing at how exposed she felt. But if she played along, maybe she could steer this where she wanted it to go. “I thought it’d be better, you know? Being on my own. No rules, no one breathing down my neck. But the streets… they’re their own kind of hell.”

Agatha’s eyes softened slightly. “What do you mean?”

Rio huffed a laugh, though there was no humor in it. “You name it: gangs, fights, cops, dealers, pervs.” She leaned forward, her elbows on her knees. “You learn fast, or you don’t survive. Violence was just… normal. Like breathing. Same with…” She gestured vaguely, not wanting to say the words. “Other things.”

Agatha’s expression tightened, her pen hovering. “Other things?”

“You know,” Rio said, her tone dismissive. “Guys who think they can take what they want ‘cause you’re young and desperate.”

Agatha’s jaw tightened. “Was it on the streets that you were first introduced to drugs?”

Rio exhaled sharply through her nose. “Yeah. Almost everyone out there does them. It’s… just a way of life. Keeps the edge off, you know? If you’re hungry, cold, scared out of your mind, a little something takes that away for a while.”

Agatha was gazing at her with an expression that looked to Rio a little too much like pity. Rio hated it.

“Look, it wasn’t all bad,” she said, trying to shift gears. “We looked out for each other. The kids, I mean. We had to. No one else was gonna do it.”

Agatha tilted her head slightly. “You found a sense of community?”

Rio shrugged. “Something like that. For example, there was this one kid – a few years younger than me. We got friendly.”

She could feel Agatha watching her closely now. Rio took another swig of water, her grip tightening on the bottle. She couldn’t back out now.

“He was… my dealer,” Rio said finally. “But he was a good kid. Just made some bad choices, like the rest of us. I tried to look out for him, you know? Keep him out of trouble.”

Agatha frowned, her pen slowing. “What happened to him?”

Rio hesitated, the words caught in her throat. Her fingers drummed against the bottle as she forced herself to meet Agatha’s gaze. “He died. Turf war. Wrong time, wrong place. I… I should’ve done more.”

Agatha looked up, her expression softer now. “You were just a kid yourself, Rio. It wasn’t your responsibility.”

Rio didn’t know how to respond to that.

The silence stretched between them, thick and heavy. Rio felt like she was standing on the edge of a cliff, waiting to see if the ground would crumble beneath her.

“So, you two got close?”

“It wasn’t-” Rio knew that, at this point, it’s too late. No turnarounds, no do-overs, no backtracking. “I looked out for him, yes.” She sighed, eventually. “He was my dealer, but I felt bad for him, you know? It was clear that he was just on the wrong path, that he didn’t know what he was getting himself in too. I wanted to help him.”

“Why’d you feel like it was your responsibility?”

“I didn’t think it was my responsibility. But I wanted to do it. Good kids shouldn’t be in places like that, it fucks them up. I mean I’m like a walking poster child for that. It was too late for me, but he was young. He didn’t deserve what happened to him – not that any of them did – but I was fond of him. He was so nice, so caring. He even refused to sell to me a few times, when he thought I was too gone already. That took guts.”

“So it must’ve affected you pretty badly, when he died?”

Rio could feel the words pull at her heart strings. “Yeah,” she sighed, fighting the urge to throw up. “I had done my best for so long to keep him out of trouble. I told him everything I knew, how he could protect himself. But when it really mattered, I wasn’t there. I really should’ve been there, Agatha. Maybe then I would have died instead of him. I think that would have been better.”

For a sickening second, Rio felt like she might be about to cry. She hadn’t done that since she was a child.

“What was his name?” Agatha asked softly.

Rio’s heart thudded painfully in her chest. She clenched her jaw, bracing herself for the impact.

“Nicky.”

Chapter 15

Chapter Notes

Hello! This chapter is heavy, and so we want to give you guys a fair TW before the chapter begins. Once again, please read through the tags.

Additional TW: Drug use, Descriptions of grief

Sorry in advance...

Agatha

If someone told Agatha that the world stopped spinning at that moment, she would believe them.

She could feel it, the force of her entire life crashing in on her, time spinning backwards – it rewrote everything she ever knew, in the matter of seconds.

Her mouth fell open as she simply stared at Rio’s uncertain expression. Her eyes were so expressive, so telling of exactly what went on behind them – Guilt, shame, sorrow, hope.

Agatha couldn’t move, couldn’t speak. It felt like Rio’s words had taken over her, crept under her skin and tightening the feeling in her stomach and throat.

It was too much, too overwhelming. Every feeling of sadness, guilt, anger and regret came crashing over her at once. She felt a wet streak down her cheek, brushing it away furiously with the back of her hand.

She couldn’t bear to look at Rio, the weight of the truth she’d just told like an infinite invisible barrier. So instead, she focused her eyes on a spot on the floor, studying the scratch created by her moving the armchair around.

Rio’s breathing was heavy, filling the room and echoing between the walls. Agatha could hear them, almost forcing their way into her own lungs, breathing in tandem.

It had been quiet for almost a minute, which was always unusual when Rio was around. The tension was almost unbearable, hanging between them like the air was water.

“I’m sorry…” Rio said suddenly, her voice unsure and weak, but still slicing through the air like a razor sharp blade. “I know that-“ she paused, her breathing once again turning heavy. “I just wanted you to know – you deserve to know – and I know you had this picture about what kind of person he was, but people make mistakes and-“

“Stop fucking talking,” Agatha gritted between her teeth, because at that point, the world might as well end if Rio finished that sentence.

Rio fell silent.

“He was-” Agatha tried to stifle a sob, it was futile. Her voice cracked in the force of it. “You’re not telling the truth.”

Rio sighed sadly, “I’m sorry, Agatha.”

“I refuse to-“

“Listen, I know you have a picture of him, how he was. I’m telling you, that was all true. He made one bad choice, that doesn’t define him.”

Agatha let the silent tears fall into her lap, landing and painting dark dots on her jeans. “Don’t you dare tell me what he was-“

“Okay,” Rio fell quiet again. She put her hands up in surrender. “But I’ll sit here until- Well, in case you want to… talk about it.”

“I don’t,” Agatha snapped. “Don’t make me talk about it.”

Every word spoken and heard felt like a stab in an old wound, opening her up again. She felt vulnerable, weak – like her very voice betrayed more than a thousand worlds ever could.

When she thought of Nicky, she thought of him in his pajamas, hunched over his guitar, a bowl of cereal or his homework. She thought of his smile, the way his eyes would turn bright.

He had her eyes, she’d always been so grateful over that. At first, she’d been scared he’d get the dad’s eyes – whatever color they were – and looking him in the eyes would be a direct reminder of the father Nicky didn’t have.

She was proud, raising him on her own. She was proud of the young man he’d become. How he always seemed to understand, how his eyes were analyzing but never overbearing – like he saw right through you.

She was proud of his manners, the way he always carried a deep respect for other people, children and adults alike. How he never excluded anyone, how he always seemed to take other people before himself.

It annoyed her sometimes, how he’d always prioritise himself last. She’d fought about it with him once, when he did his friend’s homework instead of his own. When Agatha had asked why, he had shrugged, telling her that his friend needed help, and he wanted to do it.

Agatha had been mad, telling him that he can’t trust anyone and that his friend was using him for his own gain. Nicky had looked sad, staring up at her with disbelief in his eyes. She’d apologised later that night – which was a hard thing for her to do.

Accountability was never her thing, after all.

She remembered all those nights, when he’d leave right after dinner and come home way too late for Agatha’s liking. But she allowed it, knowing that socialising is important for adolescence, that Nicky shouldn’t be restrained and isolated like she’d been during her childhood.

Pictures that had earlier been her imagining Nicky playing music and laughing with his friends, were suddenly replaced with him, walking around hollow alleyways. And worst of all, Rio was there.

She saw her, a younger version, and Nicky talking quietly. Whispering while exchanging something in the dark corner.

Worst of all, she blamed herself for the way he’d turned out. If it was all a big mistake, one wrong decision, maybe it was caused by something. A fight? A night where she didn’t come home, and he went out?

Maybe it wouldn’t have happened at all if she’d been more present during his childhood. If he didn’t need to reach out to someone, if he’d felt more loved in his own home maybe-

“I can see you spiraling,” Rio commented, once again breaking the heavy silence.

Agatha scoffed, although it came out as closer to a sob. She was spiralling, yes, but the last thing she needed right now was to hear Rio’s voice.

She liked that Rio was something different, something dangerous to the point where it was exciting. But now, suddenly looking at her she no longer saw Rio, she saw the little girl she’d been in that photo in Lilia’s place, standing with her Nicky.

Rio wasn’t hers alone anymore, she’d almost been tainted entirely by the heartbreaking memory of Nicky.

“I need you to get out,” She forced, standing up promptly and walking over to her desk, not able to look at Rio’s reaction.

“Agatha, please,” Rio practically begged, as Agatha could hear her light footsteps over the wooden floor.

“Don’t,” She tried weakly.

“I want to be here with you through this.” Rio said carefully, taking a step closer. “I don’t want to leave you again.”

“Leave,” Agatha’s voice was so stern, she could hear Rio retracting. “I don’t want you here.”

“But-“

“No,” Agatha gritted between her teeth, turning around and leaning against the desk for stability she couldn’t seem to find anywhere else. “Leave or I’ll make you leave.” She looked directly into Rio’s eyes, no matter how much it hurt. She could see the bewilderment mixed with the hurt and the powerlessness she felt at that moment.

Agatha wasn’t sure whether or not to brace for a fight, if she needed to forcefully make Rio leave she’d do so.

What she didn’t expect, however, was the lone tear that fell down Rio’s cheek at that second. It was wiped away like it was pure poison, but the proof was there, the line still visible. “Please let me-“

“I don’t want to see your face,” Agatha stated bluntly, not sure how to phrase it differently. “I can’t-“ her voice broke.

Rio’s face was a constant reminder of what Nicky was- or rather, what he wasn’t. He wasn’t her painted picture of an innocent, perfect boy anymore.

It had totally fallen. And now, she couldn’t see past the lies.

“You don’t mean that,” Rio said weakly.

Agatha scoffed. “I’ve never meant anything more in my entire life,” she gritted between her teeth, her stiff cheeks from the tears a constant reminder of how weak she’d been mere minutes ago.

Rio’s posture failed, but she never once broke eye contact. She looked at Agatha challengingly, almost as if daring her to say it again.

“I wish you died instead.”

Rio flinched, something unreadable flashing through her eyes.

Agatha expected Rio to fight, to scream, to counter with something – but instead, she remained quiet as she turned around, her eyes lingering on Agatha for a second too long, like she was waiting for Agatha to backtrack, to say she didn’t mean it.

But Agatha had always been stubborn.

Rio stormed out a second later, the door slamming closed behind her and leaving Agatha alone in the empty, silent office.

She knew she’d fucked up, but she couldn’t, at that moment, even seem to care.

Rio

Rio stepped out onto the street, the door slamming behind her as the bitter, November air nipped at her skin. In the rush, she’d left her coat back on Agatha’s couch, but she couldn’t exactly ring on the buzzer and ask for it back. Her jumper did little in terms of warmth, but she welcomed it. It was grounding, biting enough to momentarily distract her from the mess that was going on inside her head.

Her fingers curled into fists so tightly that her nails bit into her palms. She desperately blinked away the tears clouding her eyes. She couldn’t cry, not here. Not when she was so exposed.

She knew she’d done the right thing telling Agatha the truth about Nicky. That didn’t make the sharp stab of rejection any less brutal though.

She’d opened up to Agatha far more than she’d intended to – maybe more than she’d opened up to anyone, bar Lilia. For the first time, she had begun to let someone in, begun to peel back the defenses she had so carefully constructed for herself.

Agatha had assured her that being vulnerable wouldn’t get her hurt, and yet here she was, fighting the urge to completely break down in the middle of the street.

The worst part was that Agatha had given voice to words which had been already echoing in her head for years .

“I wish you died instead.”

Rio wished she had too. Nicky had been good. Better than her. She was a screw up – it was too late for her, but he’s still had time to get his life together. To have the future he deserved. And yet he was gone, and she was still here. She wished she could disappear.

She’d taken the long way home without even thinking about it. The route was instinctual, and she let her feet carry her down the dimly lit side streets and back alleys.

She turned a corner and came face to face with a boy, no older than Nicky had been when he was killed. His hands fidgeting nervously as he glanced up and down the street, looking at her with wide eyes as she almost knocked him over.

Rio didn’t even need to ask to know that he was a dealer.

He looked around sixteen, with scruffy brown hair that desperately needed a cut and a black hoodie which almost swallowed him whole. He was small for his age, shorter than her, and looked at her with wary eyes that told Rio he was relatively new to this occupation.

He reminded her far too much of Nicky. She fought down the overwhelming urge to throw up. Nicky had died, and yet on the streets that was just an everyday occurrence. There would always be someone else to take his place, another kid who’d made a wrong choice to step into his shoes. It made her overwhelmingly angry.

“What the hell are you doing?”

The boy flinched, his head snapping up to look at her. “You don’t know me, lady. Keep walking.”

Rio’s heart pounded, her hands trembling at her sides. When she looked at him, all she could see was Nicky. “What are you, sixteen? Fifteen? You’re just a kid.”

The boy straightened his spine, trying to look taller, tougher, but Rio could see the cracks in his facade. His hands fidgeted at his sides, his lips twitching with words he hadn’t decided on yet. “I’m not a kid. Back off.”

Rio didn’t. She stepped closer. “You think this is gonna get you somewhere? That this is worth it?! You’ve got no fucking idea what you’re getting yourself into.”

“Yeah?” He laughed, but it came out shaky. “And what would you know?”

What did she know? Too much. She knew that it was a slippery slope, a cycle that would chew him up and spit him out.

“I know it doesn’t end well,” she said, her voice cracking slightly. “I know it swallows you whole, leaves you with nothing. You don’t want this. You don’t.”

The boy shrugged, the movement stiff and defensive. “Gotta make a living somehow.”

“Not like this.” She took another step forward, close enough now to see the shadows under his eyes, the tightness in his jaw. “Hand it over.”

“What?” His laugh was sharper this time, desperate. “You’re crazy.”

“The drugs. Hand them over.”

He didn’t move, his defiance flaring again. “Or what?”

Rio’s chest heaved, her thoughts spiraling. The anger roared in her veins, mingling with the ache of grief and guilt that had followed her since the day Nicky died. She felt it building, the need to do something.

Her hand slid into her pocket, fingers closing around cold steel. She pulled out her pocket knife and flicked it open with a sharp click.

The boy froze, his bravado shattering in an instant. His wide eyes darted between the blade and her face, and for a moment, she hated herself for being this person. For being the threat.

But she couldn’t back down.

“Don’t make me use this. This is for your own good kid, trust me. There’s help out there – people who’ll give you a bed, a meal, a real chance.”

The boy stared at her, uncertainty warring with fear. His hands twitched, and for a moment, she thought he might run. But then, slowly, he reached into his pocket and pulled out a small clear bag, filled with white powder. He held it out hesitantly, his fingers trembling.

Rio snatched it from him, the knife still loose in her other hand. “Here,” she said, digging into her jacket and pulling out a few five dollar notes. “Use this however you want, but please be smart.”

The boy looked at the money, then at her, suspicion etched into every line of his face. “Why do you care?”

Rio opened her mouth to answer, but the words stuck in her throat. Why did she care? Because she saw Nicky in him. Because she saw herself. Because if she didn’t, who would?

“Just go,” she muttered, shoving the notes into his hand.

The boy hesitated, then nodded, shoving the money into his pocket before turning and disappearing into the shadows.

Rio stood there for a long moment, her breath coming in uneven gasps, the bag crumpled in her fist. She’d dispose of it once she got home. She clicked her knife shut and shoved it back into her pocket. Her breathing hitched as she turned and started walking again, her pace quickening.

By the time she reached her apartment, she was shaking.

She barely registered the sound of her keys hitting the floor as she slammed the door shut behind her. She sank down against the door, her head dropping to her knees, and the tears came, hot and heavy.

Her fingers curled into her jumper, tugging at the fabric as though she could rip the pain out of her chest. “I’m sorry,” she whispered to no one, her voice cracking under the weight of her grief. “I’m so fucking sorry.”

She wasn’t good at crying. She wasn’t good at feeling. The sharp ache in her chest made her want to scream, to punch the walls, to throw something – anything to make it stop. But she didn’t. She just sat there, letting the sobs wreck her body until she felt like she might come apart at the seams.

Her hands moved to her hair, fingers tangling and tugging at the strands with a desperation that bordered on violence. It was too much – too much pain, too much guilt, too much everything.

And then she felt it.

The small, crumpled bag in her pocket, pressing against her hip. Her breathing hitched, her fingers freezing mid-tug as her mind zeroed in on it. She should throw it away. That was what she’d told herself when she’d taken it. She’d been clean for seven years, she couldn’t break that.

Or could she?

Rio’s chest heaved as her hand hovered over the pocket, her mind spinning.

She shouldn’t do it. She didn’t need to. She was better than this, right? She could see Lilia’s face, hear her voice as she’d begged Rio to talk to her, back when she’d first started spiralling.

But then came another voice, cruel and cutting. Agatha’s.

“I wish you died instead.”

Her lips twisted into a grimace, her fingers curling into fists. Why shouldn’t she give in, it was what everyone expected of her anyways. She was too far fucked up to ever be anything more than a screw up. Agatha had tried to convince her otherwise, and that had blown up in her face just like everything else did.

Her hand moved to her pocket, trembling as she fished out the plastic bag. She stared at it, her jaw tightening as a fresh wave of anger and shame crashed over her. She swiped at her tear-streaked face, her vision blurring as her resolve cracked.

She stumbled into the kitchen, grabbing what she needed from various cupboards and draws before rushing to the bathroom. Her movements were frantic, as though stopping for even a second might let her reason catch up and drag her back from the edge.

She locked the door behind her and slid to the floor, the cold tiles pressing into her legs through her jeans. Her hands shook violently as she set the lighter and spoon on the toilet lid, her vision blurring.

With fumbling fingers, she ripped open the bag and tipped the powder into the spoon, spilling some onto her jeans in her haste. A growl of frustration tore from her throat as she brushed it off, smearing some into the fabric.

Her hands weren't steady enough, but she didn't care.

Her mind was a whirlwind of emotions: guilt, anger, despair. She hated Agatha for saying those words, but she hated herself more for giving her a reason to.

The lighter sparked in her hand, the tiny flame dancing as she held it under the spoon. Her knuckles whitened from the force of her grip, and the heat licked at her fingers as she angled the spoon too sharply.

The metal burned her skin, but she didn't flinch. She deserved worse.

Her tears finally spilled over as she reached for the syringe, drawing the liquid through the filter with an unsteady hand. She bit her lip so hard she tasted blood, her mind screaming at her to stop, to throw it all away. But she couldn't. Not now.

Her phone vibrated in her pocket, breaking her spiral for just a moment. She pulled it out with shaking hands, the screen lighting up with Alice’s name.

Alice: Dinner tonight? Jen is finally free, we could have a girls night!

Rio’s chest twisted. She didn’t deserve her friends, not when everything she touched seemed to fall apart. She’d spent her entire life trying to prove to people that she wasn’t some kind of bad luck charm – social workers, foster family’s, teachers.

Maybe, just maybe, they had been right all along.

Rio stared at the syringe in her hand, the needle glinting in the harsh bathroom light. The shame and self-loathing churned in her stomach, but it wasn't enough to make her stop. She hated it. Hated that it was there, hated that she hadn’t got rid of it, hated that she wanted it. But she did. She wanted it more than she wanted to breathe.

She opened Alice’s message, her fingers trembling as she typed:

Rio: I fucked up. I’m sorry.

Agatha

Agatha paced her living room frantically, her head spinning. She had meant what she’d said, and she hated herself for it. The grief she felt over losing Nicky came tumbling over her once again, the old wound had been opened.

She felt regret over the way she let her emotions control her actions, how she let them get the best of her and make them go out over Rio.

Before, she hadn’t admitted it – not even to herself – but her view of Nicky was a protective barrier over her grief. The false image created a comfort, something to distance herself from the harsh reality of his death. She held onto it like a lifeline.

That lifeline had split in two.

She halted when her phone started buzzing from her back pocket. She knew it was probably Wanda, reaching out to her again, but she couldn’t talk to her. After everything with Rio, she was scared of how Wanda would react, what Wanda would tell her.

That she did the wrong thing in saying those words, that she hadn’t meant them.

Without picking up, she let the call go through while she walked into the kitchen to prepare something remotely close to a dinner. But her phone rang again, and this time, fury took over her actions once again, so she picked up.

“If you’ve called to lecture me-”

“Agatha, It’s Alice.”

Agatha paused, sitting down by her dinner table to avoid falling. Alice? She rested her head against her hand, closing her eyes and groaning. “Sorry, I thought it was Wanda.”

“Not Wanda,” Alice said, her tone unwelcomely harsh. “You fucked up.”

“What?”

“You fucked up and you need to fucking fix it, okay?” Alice sounded almost panicked now. “I don’t know what you mean.”

“Oh cut the crap, Harkness. We can’t reach Rio, we’ve tried everything and she’s not even answering her door.”

“Try Lilia?” Agatha said dismissively, pulling at the roots of her hair.

“No,” Alice’s tone was firm. “Lilia will freak. Just please come, I’m scared.”

“Maybe she just wants to be left alone,” Agatha tried, getting ready to hang up.

“Agatha, I think she has relapsed.”

That makes Agatha stop in her tracks. She wavers over the button, not sure whether or not to leave this as is. She’s her therapist, yes, and therefore she probably shouldn’t be getting involved in her patients private life. But this is also a patient who has been struggling with addiction, and she’s supposed to help. Not cause a fucking relapse.

“What makes you say that?”

“Me and Jen got a weird text in the group chat earlier, so we went over and she’s not answering the fucking door!” Alice yelled, followed by three harsh knocks on a door. Agatha could hear the panic in Alice’s voice, and it was almost enough to convince her.

“What was the text?”

“It’s-” Alice paused, Agatha could almost hear a sob from the other end of the line. “It’s just ‘I fucked up, I’m sorry’ and nothing more.”

Agatha could swear her heart stopped beating at that moment, a million scenarios rushing through her mind. “I’m on my way.”

She hung up not even a second later, grabbing her coat in a rush and almost forgetting to lock her door. Fear and guilt crippled inside of her. She didn’t even know if going over to Rio’s place would help, if Rio would even answer the door.

But would she ever be able to look herself in the mirror again if she didn’t at least try?

The Uber ride took way too long in her opinion. She drummed her fingers anxiously against her thigh, trying to calm herself and prevent herself from straight up screaming at the driver. When they sat for a little too long at a red light, Agatha was close to getting out of the car and walking the rest of the way on foot, despite the cold weather.

Eventually, though, the Uber came to a halt. She practically ran into the apartment building, hitting the elevator button way too many times.

She had to hold on to the silver railing in the elevator, scared she might fall from how dizzy she felt. The last few moments of the session flashed through her mind, and it made her stomach turn, her mouth tasted bitter.

Alice and Jen were sitting by the stairs outside of Rio’s door, holding each other close and looking thoroughly panicked. Agatha approached them carefully, but her heels against the stone floor exposed her.

They turned around, Alice looked relieved, whereas Jen simply glared at her.

“You know this is your fault, right?” Jen said accusingly, standing up.

Agatha swallowed, “Did she tell you that?”

“No,” Alice said, jumping in. “But who else? Let’s be honest here, no one else in her life could’ve done something to cause whatever this is.”

Agatha scoffed. She didn’t really believe that, but chose to ignore the statement, moving to Rio’s door instead. “When did you try knocking last?”

“Right after I called you,” Alice sounded weak, exhausted. “We heard some movement inside a few minutes ago though, so she’s definitely inside.”

“And you think she’ll answer when I call the door because…?” Agatha asked, raising an eyebrow in confusion. Her hand hovered over the door, ready to knock. Her pulse was rushing, warming up her skin underneath the warm winter coat.

“Just try, will you?” Jen asked, exasperated.

Agatha wanted to roll her eyes, to tell Jen off for being rude – but thinking of Rio alone inside the apartment, not even wanting to answer the door for her best friends, it worried her to no end. She could feel it settling in her stomach, twisting and turning it with anxiety.

She knocked hard on the door with the palm of her hand three times, feeling it shake from the impact. When Rio didn’t immediately answer, Agatha could feel her patience waning. She knocked again, her palm beginning to sting, but she didn’t care.

“Rio!” She yelled, Alice and Jen flinched behind her. “Let me the fuck in!”

She could hear unbalanced steps from inside moving closer. Her heart started beating quicker, maybe this was gonna work. Something crashed against the floor somewhere in the apartment, the sound followed by multiple muted curses.

“Rio?” She asked, lower this time. “Rio, please let me in.”

At this point, she could practically hear Rio sobbing from the other side of the door.

“It’s just me,” She continued, glaring back at Alice and Jen to make them leave.

To her surprise, they did. Not even a second went by before they grabbed their stuff, waved a small goodbye and Alice mouthed a ‘thank you’ before the elevator doors closed, leaving Agatha alone in the otherwise empty stairwell.

She knocked again, but when Rio still didn’t open the door, she began to panic. She knew for a fact Rio was inside because she could still hear the muffled sobs from the other side, but she started to regret her decision to come here in the first place.

Clearly, Rio didn’t want company – and if she wanted it, it surely isn’t with the person causing her to feel like this in the first place.

“I don’t want to leave you here,” She tried, turning around and sliding her back against the door until she sat down on the cold stone floor.

She closed her eyes, letting her head hit the door with a low thud as she tried to control her ragged breathing. Trying to rationalise this entire situation.

Maybe Rio just really wanted to be left alone, and there was nothing more to it. Maybe she needed to collect her thoughts, to think through what Agatha had said to her during the session – Agatha should give her the space she needs to do that.

But also, Rio wasn’t the only one who needed space. Who needed time off. Everything she said and thought at the office was still true. Every second spent with Rio now felt like a second spent with the false memory of Nicky. Every time she looked Rio in the eyes, all she could see was Nicky, sixteen years old, coming home after a night at his ‘friend's house’.

She allowed herself to think back to those nights for a second, and she sat there on the floor. Had he met Rio earlier that night when he came home with a bruise on his arm, and blamed it on a heated game with his friends?

Or that night when he’d come home, threw himself on the couch as the tears came spiralling down? When Agatha asked what had happened and he had blamed it on a fight with his friend.

Suddenly, Rio was everywhere. Agatha hated her for it, for clouding all the perfectly sound memories of Nicky with herself. For telling Agatha the harsh truth, when living in her delusion felt more secure.

She heard a shift somewhere behind her, pulling her out of her thoughts. And suddenly, the door opened slightly – Agatha was blocking the way. She stood up quickly, feeling her pulse hammering underneath her skin as she looked at Rio.

Her smile faded a second later. Rio looked awful. Not only based on the state of her hair, which was a tangled mess, or her clothes. But she noticed immediately her pupils – they were small, like she was staring directly into a light, her brows irises blown wide.

Agatha paused, not able to look away for a second but wanting to so badly. The visible pain in Rio’s face made her want to throw up, the way she had to hold onto the handle like an anchor to keep her standing.

“Can I come in?” Agatha asked carefully, trying to keep eye contact, no matter what. Rio held it, but it was clear that she wasn’t present, like she was looking straight through Agatha, eyes unfocused and hazy.

Rio shrugged weakly but took a step back, allowing Agatha through the door.

Once inside, Agatha took a second to look around, but she didn’t get far before she heard a thud behind her. She quickly turned around, searching for what Rio had undeniably knocked over.

Rio had fallen, now laying on the floor, pressed against the doormat, her cheeks red and stained from tears. She held her eyes closed to stop more tears from silently streaming down her cheeks.

Agatha stood still for a second, unsure of what to do. If she should say anything, help Rio up from the floor – leave her alone?

That last option was out of the question, of course.

So Agatha bent down – her knees be damned – and carefully placed the back of her hand against Rio’s cheek, stroking gently to make Rio open her eyes.

When Rio does, Agatha’s once again met by those small pupils – the clear proof of what’s going on. That Alice was right. Agatha’s words had caused her to relapse.

She felt the revelation tug somewhere in her chest, like a hand squishing her heart tightly.

“Rio,” She asked carefully when Rio closed her eyes again.

Rio hummed in response, pulling her hand up to her head to rest against it.

Normally, Agatha would’ve rolled her eyes, but her worry was overbearing.

“Rio, you can’t sleep here,” She said softly instead, trying to contain the situation. “Let’s get you into bed, okay?”

Rio hummed again, blinking her eyes open.

“Let’s go,” Agatha whispered, reaching out a hand to grab around Rio’s waist to help her up.

Rio’s body felt like dead weight. Agatha sighed heavily, trying again with more force, tugging at her waist to make Rio realize she needed to be somewhat helpful.

Eventually, after a long minute of struggle, Rio stood up, leaning against the door and breathing heavily. Agatha grabbed her waist harder, forcing all her weight against her to practically carry her through the apartment.

With every heavy step, Rio plastered to her side, she regretted everything more and more. She knew the words had been hurtful, of course – that’s why she’d said them. To get Rio away, to make her understand how much Agatha was hurting at that moment.

What she hadn’t thought about, however, was that Rio was a person too. Rio could be just as affected by words as she could, Rio wasn’t immune to the hurtful stuff Agatha spits out. Rather, she’s very fragile to them.

The apartment was rather small, just a living room-kitchen area, a bathroom and a bedroom, so finding the way through wasn’t too hard. Agatha would love to look at the details, look at the furniture, the tidiness – usually, that can tell a lot about a person.

But she was too distracted trying to keep Rio upright, looking down so she wouldn't trip over her feet on the way to the bedroom.

Once inside, she carefully placed Rio on the bed, moving to sit down next to her. Rio sighed heavily, leaning her entire body weight against Agatha.

“You’re not mad at me?” Rio asked, slurred. It took Agatha a few seconds to register the words.

When she did, she leaned back, throwing her head against the headboard and started up into the ceiling, unsure of what to say.

Because the truth was yes, she was mad. She’s mad at Rio for not sharing the information earlier, but at the same time she’s mad that she did say anything at all.

She’s mad at herself for not being present during Nicky’s childhood – wishing to reverse the time and change everything, from the very start.

She’s mad that she can’t be mad. That however angry, frustrated or sad she is by the news, sitting next to Rio on the bed, she suddenly can’t feel anything at all. Nothing except worry.

So instead of telling the truth, she sighed. “We shouldn’t talk about that right now.”

Rio let out a quiet sob, hurrying her face in Agatha’s shoulder. “But are you?”

“Rio,” Agatha warned, trying to keep her tone gentle. “Can we not talk about this right now?”

“But-“

“Rio.”

Rio groaned, turning around and facing her back to Agatha.

Agatha sat quiet for a moment, not sure what else to do. She’d gotten in, she was free to call Alice, tell her to come back and take over the care. But something within her strained at the thought of leaving Rio in this state.

“Why would you say that?” Rio asked over her shoulder.

“Say what?” Agatha asked carefully, knowing exactly what she meant.

“All of it.” Rio’s body was shaking now, Agatha was unsure whether it was sobs or the fact that Rio was freezing. She settled on the latter, refusing to face the truth of the first. Slowly, she brought the covers up, tucking Rio in carefully before leaning down again, staring up at the ceiling and mapping the white ceiling with her eyes.

“Because-“ She paused, taking a few controlled breaths. “I miss Nicky.”

Rio let out an audible sob, her entire body flinching at the force of it. “Me too.”

Agatha felt like someone had just ripped out her heart, later it was on display for everyone to see. Except it was only her and Rio in that small bedroom, no other eyes could see her.

The words got stuck in her throat, the thought of even breathing felt unfair. “Rio,” She exhaled slowly, trying to format the next sentence. “I’m…”

Sorry?

The words go unspoken.

“I’m gonna call Alice, okay?”

“No,” Rio whined, turning around once again. “Don’t leave me.”

God, Agatha could laugh at the pout on Rio’s lips, but the moment fell all too serious. “I can’t stay with you.”

Rio pouted more, “Why?”

“Because-“ Fuck. “Well, I have work in the morning, and I can’t… I can’t be with you right now.”

A single tear fell silently down Rio’s cheek, landing softly on the pillow underneath. “Please.”

“Rio-“ Agatha groaned. “I’m- Seeing your face right now… it reminds me of Nicky too much. And none of the good stuff – the bad stuff, Rio. Seeing you reminds me of every lie he told me, every night he came home late. I can’t-“ She stifled a sob. “I can’t be reminded of all that.”

The pool on the pillow grew significantly as the tears kept falling, “I’m sorry.”

It’s not your fault. I’m sorry too. I appreciate you helping him during that time.

Even more stuff left unspoken, silent apologies she’d never get out, that Rio would never hear.

A second later, Agatha dialed Alice.

After only one signal, Alice’s panicked voice sounded through the speaker. “How’s it going?”

Agatha exhaled softly, ”Can you come over?”

“You’re inside?” Alice's tone was laced with disbelief.

“Yes, but I can’t stay. I have work tomorrow morning.” She glanced over at Rio as she spoke, almost suddenly afraid that Rio’s hearing everything on the phone. But Rio’s eyes are closed, her entire demeanor relaxed. Agatha wouldn’t be surprised to find she was asleep.

“I’m five minutes away.” Alice said quickly before hanging up, leaving Agatha alone in the almost silent room. The only sound being Rio’s labored breathing bouncing through the walls, a vivid proof of how affected she was.

Agatha let out a quiet sigh, not able to watch Rio so she once again stared up at the ceiling. She noticed the way the lamp had a small flicker, the way the white streaks on the ceiling met and circled around each other.

She couldn’t face Rio again, not after tonight. Not after the session earlier today. It all felt too heavy, too much weight pressing down on her. Everything came crashing down at once, her regret, sadness and anger because of Nicky’s death. How she handled the entire Rio situation.

But most of all, she felt guilt.

Guilt for being the one to once again put Rio in this position. For being the one to cause her relapse. For not realizing her words had a greater impact than she’d originally thought, that they genuinely affected Rio’s well being and inevitably caused this to happen.

How could she ever face a client she’d done that to? How could she ever look Rio into the eyes again after today?

The answer was clear. She couldn’t.
